# Chapter 1: What Is Written

*The Crossing · CR 611*

The man's name was Emeric Saal, and he had eleven thousand four hundred and six marks left to pay when the coil housing closed on him.

Annick Vell knew the figure before she knew his face. It came up on her tally as she pulled herself down the aft gallery hand over hand, the alarm still whooping in the spine, and she read it the way she read everything, quickly, with her mouth shut. *Saal, Emeric. V5-1180. Bonded, Wake rotation thirty-one. Coil gang. Balance outstanding: 11,406 m. Dependents: Saal, Lise, V5-1181 (cradle). Saal, Tam, V5-1182 (cradle). Saal, Orrin, V5-1183 (cradle).*

Three cradles. She put the tally in her coat and kept climbing.

The fold coil of the *Merciful Increase* was a ring of black segments forty metres across, and after a jump it was the hottest thing for a light-year in any direction. It bled heat for weeks. To help it, the segments of its housing rolled slowly open and shut like the gills of something enormous, venting into radiator fins, and in the hours after each opening the coil gang went in with scrapers to clear the crust of coil-salt that grew on the inner faces. It was not complicated work. It was the kind of work you gave to people whose time was cheaper than a machine's.

The segment had closed early. That was all. A relay had stuck and then unstuck, and a housing plate weighing four tonnes had swung back into place with Emeric Saal's upper body between it and its seat.

There were twenty of the gang in the gallery, holding the rails, their dun coveralls dark with sweat. The air back here was hot and tasted of metal. Two proctors in grey kept the hatchway, stingers clipped to their belts and their visors up because of the heat. The doctor was already at the plate with a pry bar he had no hope of using. Yusuf Haddad was a narrow, tidy man who looked, in any emergency, slightly embarrassed for everyone involved. He glanced up at her and shook his head once.

"They're bringing the jack," he said. "There's no hurry for it."

Saal's legs hung out from under the edge of the plate. One boot had come off and floated a little way off, turning slowly, and nobody had caught it. On his left wrist, where his sleeve had ridden up, Annick could see the edge of his number in faded blue and, above it, the band of his air meter. The meter's little window was dark. It had stopped counting when he stopped breathing. She noticed, because it was her job to notice, that it had stopped on 4,113 litres for the shift.

She took out her tally and her stylus.

The gang watched her do it. She felt them watching the way she felt cold through a suit: a pressure, a temperature, not anything she could argue with. She had grown up in the stacks of Callis with people like these, had eaten what they ate, had been, for the first seventeen years of her life, one of them. On the ship they had a name for her. They said it now, somewhere at the back, low enough that she could choose not to hear it.

*Ink.*

"Time of death," she said to Haddad.

"Call it eleven forty ship. Crush asphyxia, I'd guess, before the rest of it. It would have been quick."

"Would it."

"No," said Haddad, after a moment. "But you can write that it was."

She wrote eleven forty. She wrote *accident in the course of labour*. She wrote the plate's segment number and the name of the gang chief, a big Callisian with a burned face who was holding the rail beside her with both hands and staring at the boot.

Then she came to the part she had to say aloud.

Field entries were provisional. The binding record was made in the Book itself, under the key, and spoken there; but the Charter liked its pursers to announce a transfer at the place where it occurred, before the people it concerned, so that no one could say afterward that they had not been told. It was meant as a courtesy. Annick had long ago stopped trying to decide what it was.

"Emeric Saal," she said, "V5-1180, is dead in the course of labour. His balance of eleven thousand four hundred and six marks passes under the Ordinances to his household. To Lise Saal, his wife. To Tam Saal and Orrin Saal, his children." She did not raise her voice. She did not need to; the gallery was very quiet now, with the coil ticking as it cooled. "The transfer will be sealed in the Book at the end of the watch. Any person who disputes it may come to the purser's office before then."

Nobody would come. Nobody ever came.

She was turning away when a voice said, clear and flat from among the gang, "Will you tell them?"

Annick knew the voice. She would have known it in a storm. She looked along the rail and found her sister holding a scraper against her chest like a shield, a smear of coil-salt across one cheek, her dark hair tied back with a twist of wire.

"They're asleep," said Dace. "The wife and the boys. Will you go down to the hold and tell them? Or will they wake up at Absalom owing it and find out from the bill?"

The proctor at the hatch shifted. The gang chief said, not looking around, "Leave it, Vell."

"I'm asking the purser a question."

"The assistant purser," said Annick, "will enter the transfer at the end of the watch." It came out exactly as level as she meant it to. "The household will be informed at waking, in the usual way."

Dace looked at her for a long moment. Then she turned back to the plate and put her hand flat on it, beside the dead man's leg, the way you would put your hand on an animal to calm it.

Annick went back up the gallery and did not count her breaths until she was out of sight. Then she counted them, in, out, the way she had counted them as a child in the stacks when the air was bad and the meters on the walls ran red: four in, hold, four out. It did not help. It never had. It was only something to do with the part of her that wanted to do something else.

* * *

The strongroom of the *Merciful Increase* sat in the spine of the ship, in the long weightless core that ran from the Lantern at the bow to the coil at the stern, and it sat there for a reason. The Book must be near the people it binds. That was the Charter's phrase, written into the architecture of every transport the Company had ever built, and Annick suspected that whoever first wrote it had meant it as a piety and whoever first built it had understood it as a convenience. The strongroom was a bulkhead away from the Hold, where three thousand six hundred and twelve bonded colonists lay frozen in their cradles. On the far side of it, beyond a thickness of shielding, was Ballast Tank Four, one of the four great water tanks that wrapped the spine like the segments of a seed and gave the ship its mass and its protection against the radiation of the dark.

She went in through the iron door. Over the lintel, in letters of Charter red as high as her hand, someone long ago had painted WHAT IS WRITTEN IS OWED. The paint had flaked at the corners and been touched up, flaked and been touched up, so that the letters had a slightly swollen look, like the knuckles of an old man.

The old man himself was at the cradle, coughing.

Aurelio Benn had been purser of the *Merciful Increase* for twenty-eight years and four voyages. He was seventy-one, and something was wrong with his lungs that Haddad had a name for and no cure for, and he wore an oxygen line looped over his ears and down to a concentrator clipped at his belt. In zero gravity his white hair stood out around his head like the down of a seed. He had a long, mournful, humorous face, and he was, as far as Annick had been able to discover in eleven years as his clerk and then his assistant, the only honest man in the employ of the Meridian Charter Company. She did not hold it against him. He had been honest for so long that it had worn smooth, like a step.

"Saal," he said, when he could. "I heard."

"Yes."

"Did you announce?"

"Yes."

He looked at her over the cradle. "Your sister was on the coil gang."

"Yes."

"Ah," said Benn, and left it there, which was one of the things she loved him for.

The cradle was a padded iron bracket on a pedestal, sized to hold one object and hold it absolutely still. The object it held was the Book.

It was an iron case forty centimetres by thirty by twelve, heavy enough in gravity that she had to lift it with both arms. On its cover was the Charter's sunburst over a pair of scales, worn nearly flat at the high points by a century of hands. Its hasp was a slot of polished steel. Inside it, protected by the iron and by a cushion of grey foam, was the lattice: a block of grown crystal the colour of weak tea, in which every debt of every person aboard was inscribed atom by atom and could not be scraped out again. The Book did not forget. That was its whole purpose. A ledger on paper could be burned, a ledger in a machine could be wiped, but a lattice grown under seal held its entries the way a tree holds its rings, and like a tree's rings they could be read by anyone who knew how, and dated, and trusted.

On the wall to the left of the cradle hung a brass plaque engraved with the Purser's Ordinances, twenty-two articles in a cramped legal hand. To the right was the dead shelf, where the Books of the ship's four previous voyages lay strapped in a row, identical to this one in every respect except that their hasps had been closed with lead and their cover plates stamped VOYAGE I, VOYAGE II, VOYAGE III, VOYAGE IV. Their debts were all discharged or transferred, their ships' companies long since landed and dispersed across the colonies of the Charter, and the Books were kept only because the law said a Book could not be destroyed. Below the shelf, set into the wall, was the privy box — a little steel safe a hand's breadth square, which opened to the purser's key and nothing else, and in which, Benn said, generations of pursers had kept their wills, their letters and on one occasion a bottle of Callisian brandy that he had been too frightened of to drink. In the floor beside the pedestal was a square hatch with a red lever and a seal-wire looped through it.

Annick took the key out of her collar.

It was iron, too, a long old-fashioned key the length of her palm, its bit cut in an intricate pattern of notches, with a bead of lattice set into the bow like a dull eye. Stamped on the shank, very small, was the number 0447. It was the assistant purser's key — Benn wore the master on a cord around his neck — but the Book did not care which of the two opened it. It cared only that the key was a purser's key. Whose hand turned it was, by the first of the Ordinances, beside the point.

She set the bit into the hasp and turned it. The case unlocked with a deep, soft sound, like a door closing somewhere far away. She opened the lid. Across the inside of it, the page-glass woke and filled with the dense columns of the voyage's entries, blue-white, faintly trembling, as though the light were not quite sure of itself.

"Speak it," said Benn.

She put her thumb to the bead and spoke it. She gave the date, the hour, her name and her office. She gave Emeric Saal's name and number and the manner of his death. She gave the balance, and the household, and the names of the children, and she said that under the Ordinances the debt now passed to them in equal parts and would bear interest at the rate of the voyage from this hour. She said it all in the same voice she had used in the gallery, and as she spoke the page-glass inscribed it in neat columns, and the lattice took it, and somewhere in the depths of the crystal a pattern grew that would outlast every person who had ever heard of Emeric Saal.

"Sealed," she said, and took her thumb away.

"Play it back."

She did not want to. She touched the glass and the Book gave her own voice back to her, clear, a little thin with the small speaker:

*— to Tam Saal and Orrin Saal, his children, in equal parts —*

It did not shake. She listened for it, and it did not.

Benn listened too, his head tilted. "Do you know why the Book keeps the voice?" he said. "I've told you."

"So that the record can be heard in court."

"That's what the clerks' manual says. It's not why." He coughed, and waited, and went on. "A hand can be forced. You can hold a man's hand and make it sign. But you cannot make a voice tell the truth in the way it sounds. A voice shakes. That's why the Book keeps it. So that someday, someone listening can tell." He smiled at her with his long mournful face. "Yours didn't shake."

"Is that good?"

"I don't know," said Benn. "Is it?"

She closed the lid. The page-glass went dark, and she took the key from the hasp, and the Book was only a block of iron again.

"Recite," said Benn.

It was a habit of his, older than her time with him; he said his own purser had made him do it every watch for nine years, and he had resented it for eight and a half. She hooked her foot under the rail beside the plaque so as not to drift and recited, without looking at it, the articles he cared about. The clerks' manual taught four of the twenty-two. Benn had made her learn them all, and then he had made her learn which ones mattered.

"Article One. The Purser is whosoever holds the Key and the Book together."

"Why together?"

"Because a key without the Book is a piece of iron, and a Book without the key is a locked box. Only the two together make the office."

"Go on."

"Article Four. What is written under seal is owed."

He nodded at the door, at the letters she could not see from here. "Go on."

"Article Nine. Should the vessel be taken by any party not lawfully in command, the Purser shall commit the Book to the ballast, that it be not suborned."

"The drop," said Benn, and looked at the red lever with something like affection. "Every Charter hull has one. A lead sleeve, a chute, and twelve metres down to the bottom of Tank Four. Pirates used to take ships and make the purser write them in as owners. So the Charter decided that if the ship were ever taken, the Book would go into the water and stay there, and whoever had taken her would have a ship and no title to it." He wheezed. "No purser's used one in a hundred years. Pirates found it cheaper to buy ships than steal them. Go on."

"Article Eleven. An entry read aloud under seal before witnesses stands as the Book itself."

"Which means?"

"That a purser can read out what the Book says, and if enough people hear it and it's sealed when it's read, it counts even if the Book is lost."

"It was meant for wrecks. A purser in a lifeboat with the key and no Book, swearing to what was in it. It never worked very well. Witnesses forget. Go on."

"Article Fourteen. The Master's authority is that which the Book records."

"And so?"

"And so the ship obeys the Master recorded in the Book. The Lantern's locks are keyed to the entry. A captain the Book does not name cannot fly her."

"Twenty."

"A debt discharged under seal is discharged forever, against all heirs and assigns."

"Which no purser of the Meridian Charter Company has ever done," said Benn, "except by payment. Good." He closed his eyes. "That'll do. You don't need the other seventeen tonight. They're mostly about fees."

She unhooked her foot and drifted a little toward the door. Behind her, Benn had begun coughing again, a long dry rattle that went on and on. She waited it out. When it was done he said, with his eyes still closed, "She's angry with you."

"She's angry with everyone."

"No," said Benn. "Just you. You're the one she expected something of." He opened his eyes. "Go and eat. You've had nothing since the alarm. I'll close."

* * *

She did not eat. She had got as far as the spoke lift to the ring, where the gravity of the spinning habitat would come up under her feet like the floor of a slow elevator and she would be able to sit down at a table and look at a bowl of slab without having to hold it, when the ship's voice spoke in every corridor at once.

*Courier contact. Courier contact. Purser's office to the Lantern.*

It was a sound she had heard four times in eleven years. The *Merciful Increase* was eight jumps out from Callis on the Long Lane to Absalom, drifting at the dim waypoint called Pell while the coil cooled, and no ordinary signal could catch a ship between fold points. Only a courier could: a fold-runner, a thing like a black spearhead three metres long, all drive and no room, launched from a Charter port with its little sealed packet and a navigational solution that would bring it out of fold within a few thousand kilometres of a ship whose position it had been given weeks before. They were expensive. They were dangerous. The Charter sent them for one of two reasons, and the second reason was war.

She turned around in the spoke and went forward.

The Lantern was the bridge of the ship, and it was named for the dome of armoured glass that capped the bow, so that the officers on watch sat in a bowl of light with the whole sky above them. At Pell there was not much sky to see. The waypoint's brown dwarf hung off to port like a coal at the end of a fire, a dull red disc that gave less light than the instruments, and beyond it the stars of the Long Lane were thin and few. The courier had come out of fold four hundred kilometres off the starboard bow and was closing on its own small fire. She could see it from the hatch: a spark moving against the dark.

There were six people on the Lantern's watch. Three of them mattered.

Captain Harrow Lindqvist stood at the rail of the command ring with his hands behind his back. He was sixty, grey all over — grey hair, grey stubble, a grey uniform coat worn to the threads at the cuffs — and he had the stillness of a man who had learned to save his strength for things he could change. He nodded at her as she came in, which was more than many captains would have done for an assistant purser.

Beside him, holding a stanchion with one hand and a slate with the other, was the First Officer. Nadia Szell was forty-five and looked younger, a compact woman with close-cropped black hair and eyes the colour of strong tea. She was the best pilot Annick had ever seen. The crew said she could thread a fold point with her eyes shut and probably had, and they said it with the particular mixture of pride and unease that crews reserve for officers who are better than the ship deserves. She did not nod at Annick. She looked at her, steadily, as if Annick were an instrument whose reading she was not sure of.

The third was Edwin Quarry, Factor of the Meridian Charter Company, who was not an officer of the ship at all.

Every Charter transport carried a Factor, as every Charter transport carried a purser, and the two offices were meant to watch each other. The purser kept the Book. The Factor kept the Charter's interest — its contracts, its instructions, its law. He commanded the ship's twenty-four proctors. He could countermand the Master on any matter touching the Company's property, and on a colony transport the Company's property included everything aboard that could be sold, which was everything. Quarry was fifty, tall, beautifully barbered, with a soft voice and soft hands and a habit of looking at people as if he were pricing them. He was looking at Annick like that now.

"The purser's not with you," he said.

"Purser Benn is closing the Book, Factor. I have the office's seal." She held up her slate. "Courier receipt must be logged before the packet is opened."

"Then log it."

She did. The Charter's procedures were precise about couriers, because couriers were the only way the Company could speak to its ships, and the Company did not like to be misquoted. A dock tender went out on a line and brought the runner in, its black skin still smoking with fold-frost, and two crew in hard suits cracked the packet housing in the lock and carried in a sealed cylinder the size of a forearm. Annick recorded the hour of contact, the runner's serial, the integrity of the seal. She photographed the cylinder. She put her thumb to her slate and attested that the seal was unbroken at receipt, and the slate passed the attestation along a line to the Book in the spine, where it would be grown into the voyage's annex. Then she held out the cylinder to the captain.

"Factor," said Lindqvist, without moving.

Quarry took it. The seal was a Charter seal, a disc of lattice the size of a coin, and it opened to the Factor's thumb. Inside was a single sheet of film and a smaller sealed packet, which Quarry put away in his coat without looking at it. He unrolled the film and read it.

Annick watched him read. She had watched a great many people read things that changed their lives. It was part of her work: the moment the bill was presented, the moment the transfer was explained, the moment a man understood what his father had left him. People's faces did not do what you expected. They did not fall or crumple. Mostly they became very still, and something behind the stillness began to move very fast.

Quarry's face became very still. Then he smiled slightly, the way a man smiles who has been told something he has been expecting for a long time, and handed the film to the captain.

Lindqvist read it. He read it twice. Annick watched him reach the bottom and go back to the top, and then he did a thing she had never seen him do: he sat down. There was a seat at the command ring that captains used only in fold, and he lowered himself into it as if it were in gravity and his knees had gone.

Nadia Szell had not taken her eyes off Annick. She did not look at the film at all. She looked at the assistant purser, who was looking at the captain, and when Annick turned and met her gaze, the First Officer's mouth moved very slightly at one corner. It was not a smile. It was the expression of someone noting a reading, and filing it.

"Thank you, Assistant Purser," said Quarry gently. "That will be all."

* * *

Dace was waiting for her at the spoke.

She was off shift. She had washed the coil-salt off her face but not quite off her neck, and she had changed into the grey knit that bonded wore for sleeping, and she was sitting on the lip of the lift hatch with her feet hooked under the rail, the way they used to sit on the edge of the stack roofs on Callis and look at the lights of the port. She was twenty-two. Annick had been twelve when she was born and fifteen when their mother died, and for two years after that she had been the only thing between Dace and the stacks, and then she had taken the Charter's clerk bond and gone into the Company's service and left Dace with an aunt and a promise. She had sent money every month for fifteen years. She had been very careful about it. She had kept a ledger.

"What was it?" said Dace.

"I can't tell you that."

"A courier. Everyone heard. What was it?"

"Packets are for the Master and the Factor. I don't read them."

"No. You just write down that they came." Dace pushed off the hatch rim and drifted closer, catching the rail beside her. Up close she smelled of the bonded quarters — of too many people, and the cheap soap, and the faint chemical sweetness of the cradle-gel that never quite washed out of anyone who had been frozen. "You wrote his children into the Book."

"I recorded a transfer."

"Two boys. One's six. I asked the gang chief. He's six, Annick. He's lying in a box in the hold with frost on his eyelids and he owes the Company four thousand marks he didn't owe this morning, and when he wakes up at Absalom somebody's going to hand him a bill with his father's name on it and that's how he'll find out."

"That's the law."

"I know it's the law. You're the law. That's what I'm saying."

Annick said nothing. There was nothing to say that she had not already said, years ago, in the cramped office on the Callis docks where she had found Dace's name on the voyage manifest — *Vell, Dace. Bonded. Passage to Absalom on credit* — and understood, too late, that the money she had sent every month for fifteen years had not been enough, that the aunt had died owing, that the debt had come down to Dace as debts did, and that her sister had looked at the stacks and the debt and the long grey future of them and chosen instead to sign herself onto a ship going to the end of the known world. The ship her sister worked on. Annick had gone to the Factor's office that afternoon with her savings. It had been two hundred marks short of the buy-out. It had been two hundred marks short, and her own wages were bonded to the Company for another nine years and could not be borrowed against, and she had gone back to her desk and entered the manifest and sealed it.

"You could have told me not to," Dace had said, the first time they spoke aboard.

"You'd have done it anyway."

"You don't know that. You never asked."

They had had the same conversation, in different words, perhaps twenty times since. They were having it again now, without saying any of it.

"Go to sleep, Dace," said Annick.

Her sister looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, in a different voice, quite quietly, "What was on the courier? Just tell me if it's bad."

Annick thought of the captain sitting down.

"I don't know," she said, which was true, and then, because it was also true and she was tired, "Yes. I think so."

Dace nodded slowly, as if this were what she had expected. She let go of the rail and drifted back toward the hatch. At the rim she stopped and said, without turning, "There's a song the coil gang sings. Did you know? When they're scraping. *Heave the iron, heave it slow, the Book is heavy, down we go.*" She said it rather than sang it; Dace had never been able to carry a tune. "They sang it for Emeric after you left. While they were jacking the plate. Thought you'd want to know. For your records."

* * *

Benn came back to his cabin in the ring at four in the morning, ship's time. Annick knew because she was awake. Her cabin was beside his — the purser's office had two berths, a desk and a cupboard of forms, on the ring's third deck between the paymaster and the chaplain who had died at Callis and not been replaced — and the bulkhead was thin, and she had been lying on her bunk in the ring's light gravity listening to the hum of the ventilation and counting.

She heard his door. She heard him sit down on his bunk, heavily, and the click of his concentrator, and then for a long time nothing at all. Then he knocked on the bulkhead, twice, the way he did when he wanted her and did not want to shout.

He was sitting with his elbows on his knees and his oxygen line in his hand, as if he had forgotten what it was for. In the gravity of the ring he looked older; his hair lay flat, and his shoulders were an old man's shoulders. He had been in the captain's day room for five hours.

"Shut the door," he said.

She shut it.

"Meridian is gone."

She stood in the middle of the tiny cabin and waited for the rest of it. It did not come at once. Benn put the line back over his ears, carefully, and took two breaths from it, and looked at the floor.

"The Bank of Vey called its loans," he said. "All of them. Six weeks ago, or seven — the packet's dated from Callis. The Charter couldn't pay. There was a run. There's been — I don't know what there's been. Riots at the port. A Receiver appointed. The Company's in liquidation, Annick, and everything it owns belongs to the Receivers of Vey until they've sold it, and they intend to sell it."

"Everything it owns," said Annick.

"Yes."

The ship. The cargo. The Book, and everything written in the Book. Three thousand six hundred and twelve debts in the hold and two hundred and forty awake, and the debts of the dead passing to the sleeping.

"What do they want us to do?"

Benn was silent for so long that she thought he had not heard her. When he spoke again his voice had changed. It was the voice he used in the strongroom when he made her recite: patient, insistent, a teacher's voice.

"They will want me to seal something," he said. "I won't."

"Seal what?"

"I'm not going to tell you. Not tonight. Not until I have to." He looked up at her. "I'm seventy-one, Annick. I have perhaps a year left in me with this—" he lifted the line — "and I'd rather spend it saying no than saying yes. It's a luxury. I've had very few of them. Let me have this one for a day or two before you know about it."

"And if they don't take no?"

"Then they'll come to you."

"Why would they come to me?"

He looked at her steadily. It was a look she had seen him give to bonded colonists at the moment of the bill — not unkind, not anything, only clear.

"Because you're the assistant purser," he said, "and there are two keys, and I'm old. And because you're ballast-born, and your sister is in the Wake, and the Factor has known both of those things since the day you came aboard." He let her take that. "When they come to you — and they may not; perhaps I'm wrong, perhaps I'll live forever out of spite — I want you to remember the Ordinances. All of them. Not just the four they teach clerks. Not just the ones they'll quote at you."

"I know them."

"You know the words. Remember what they're for." He lay back on the bunk with a grunt and closed his eyes. "Go to bed. I'm sorry. I shouldn't have told you at four in the morning. I wanted to tell someone, and you were awake."

"How did you know I was awake?"

"Because you're always awake," said Benn, with his eyes closed. "You lie in there counting. I can hear you through the wall."

She went back to her own bunk. She lay down. The ring turned, and the gravity held her, and the ventilation hummed, and the ship drifted at Pell with the coil ticking as it cooled and the courier's empty hull clamped to its side like a tick. Somewhere far below her in the spine, in the dark beside the Hold, the Book lay in its cradle with Emeric Saal's children newly written into it, and three thousand six hundred and twelve people slept on in their cradles with frost on their eyelids, owing.

She counted. Four in. Hold. Four out. She lay there and kept her accounts, as she had kept them every night for eleven years, the shadow ledger she carried in her head and had never written anywhere: the names of everyone she had entered dead. She added Emeric Saal to the bottom of it. Then, because she could not help it, she lay awake and wondered what it was that the courier had asked for that would make a captain sit down, and a Factor smile, and an old man who had said yes for twenty-eight years decide to say no.


# Chapter 2: Liberty Owes Liberty

*The Account · CR 929*

I struck Ewan Haskell to the Deep Ice twelve days before the Burning, for two hundred and twelve marks.

I'd like to tell you I remember it because of everything that came after. I don't. I remember it because I remember all of them. I've been an Assessor of the Office of the Account for twenty years, and in that time I've struck three hundred and eight people to the ice, and I can give you every name and every figure and most of the faces. It's the one gift the Office ever gave me that I didn't have to pay back. A good memory for numbers. They saw it in me when I was twelve and they lifted me up out of Gallows Reach by it, like a fish on a line, and I've been hanging there ever since, looking down.

Gallows Reach is the oldest settlement on Tamar. You won't find that in the Houses' histories, which begin at the Hulk and work outward, but it's true. The first people the Founders sent down to the ice were sent here — the first Struck, the first defaulters, whoever they were — and they dug the first galleries with hand tools into the face of a glacier eight hundred metres thick, and their children dug the next ones, and so on for three hundred years. The town goes down into the ice like a set of stairs. At the top, near the ferry port, the galleries are wide and lit and there's a Clear quarter with real windows looking out onto the black sky and the great rust-coloured face of Absalom hanging over the horizon. At the bottom, near the mine heads, the galleries are just big enough for a man to stand in, and there's no sky at all, and the walls sweat.

The Haskells lived near the bottom. Eleven hundred metres down, gallery forty, third door on the left past the tea shop that wasn't a tea shop. I'd been there twice before that year. I knew the way.

You learn to count as an Assessor, if you didn't already know how. Not the portions — anyone can count portions, the tally does it for you — but the other things. How many steps from the lift to the door. How many people in the room when you arrive and how many when you leave. How long a man takes to answer when you tell him the figure. How many times his wife looks at the door to the back room, which is where the son is. I counted all of those at the Haskells'. It was something to do with the part of me that might otherwise have done something else.

Piet Haskell was forty-six and looked sixty. He'd worked the refinery belts for thirty years, and the belts had taken his hearing and most of his left hand, and he paid his household's Liberty portion every quarter in labour marks from the belt and his wife's piecework, and for the last three quarters he'd been short. Two hundred and twelve marks short this time. Not much. About a month's wages for a Clear clerk in Saltmarch. Not much, except that it was the third time, and the Account doesn't care how much.

"I'll have it by the Burning," he said. "There's overtime at the Burning. Everyone knows there's overtime."

"There's overtime," I agreed.

"So I'll have it."

"You'll have some of it. You'll have about ninety marks of it, if the belt holds and you don't miss a shift. I've seen the overtime figures." I had them on the tally. I didn't show him. "And it doesn't matter, Mr Haskell. You know it doesn't. The quarter closed yesterday. Three short quarters and the portion's in default, and when a portion's in default the surety is struck for the shortfall. That's the law."

"He's seventeen."

"I know how old he is."

The schoolroom was next door. I could hear it through the ice — a thin, high chorus of children, perhaps twenty of them, reciting the Catechism of Mercy the way I'd recited it at their age in a schoolroom two galleries up, in the same singsong, with the same little hitch before the hard answers.

*Who carried us?*
*The Increase carried us.*
*Who would have sold us?*
*The Purser would have sold us.*
*What was her name?*
*Her name was Annick Vell, and it is written in ash.*
*Who saved us?*
*The Founders saved us.*
*What did they burn?*
*They burned the Book.*
*What do we owe?*
*We owe our Liberty.*
*To whom?*
*To those who paid for it.*

We learned it before we learned to count, which was the point. Every child on Tamar can tell you the name of the Purser who sold her own people. No child on Tamar can tell you the name of a single Founder, because the Founders are not named. That's piety, the Houses say. A Founder's name belongs to God and the Book, and the Book is ash. Ottilie used to say it was also very convenient, but Ottilie said a lot of things.

"There's a review," said Haskell's wife. She was a small woman with white burn marks on her forearms from the piecework, the kind you get from salt-etch. She hadn't sat down since I came in. "Before a strike. There's a review. Someone told me."

"There's an appeal to the Chief Assessor," I said. "You can lodge it. It takes six months, and the surety serves while it's heard, and it's never once been granted in my time." I made myself look at her. "I'm sorry, Mrs Haskell."

That was a lie, or not quite a lie. I'm always sorry. It doesn't cost anything and it doesn't change anything, and I said it the way I said the figure, in the same tone, because the Office trains you to keep your voice level at the moment of the bill. People can stand almost anything if you say it to them in a level voice. It's when your voice goes that they start to think there might be some other way.

The son came out of the back room on his own. I've always been grateful to the ones who do that. He was big for seventeen, a refinery boy, with his father's shoulders and his mother's pointed chin and a faint grey smudge on the inside of his left wrist where last year's Burning ash hadn't quite washed out. They mark you there on Burning Day, over the place where the old numbers used to go. Liberty's mark, the Houses call it. In Gallows Reach they call it something else.

"How long?" he said.

"Two hundred and twelve marks at the Deep Ice rate. Fourteen months."

He nodded. He'd known. They always know; there's nothing in Gallows Reach anybody doesn't know about the Deep Ice. Fourteen months in the penal mines at the bottom of the world, cutting volatiles out of the ice with a thermal lance in a cold so deep it gets into the bone. People come back from fourteen months. Most of them.

I took out my tally and held it to his wrist, and the tally read the chip under the skin and gave me his name and his portion and the figure, and I spoke the strike. *Ewan Haskell, surety of the Haskell portion, Gallows Reach forty, struck to the Deep Ice for the term of fourteen months in satisfaction of two hundred and twelve marks.* I said it level. The tally took it and sent it up to the Office on the Hulk, where it would be written into the Account in the presence of a Keeper and become a fact about the world, as permanent as the ice.

There was a long chain round my neck, under my shirt. I could feel it as I bent over the boy's wrist. On the end of it hung an iron key that had been my mother's, and her mother's, and so on back into the ice for longer than anyone remembered: a long, old-fashioned key the length of my palm, with a notched bit and a dull bead of something set into the bow. It didn't open anything. Every ice family in Gallows Reach has some piece of junk like it, handed down — a spoon, a ring, a button off a coat nobody living has seen. Ours was the key. My mother put it round my neck the day the Office lifted me, at the ferry port, with the Assessor who'd come for me standing a polite distance off, and she said, *Don't sell it.* That was all. Not *remember us*. Not *come back*. *Don't sell it.* I've never known whether she meant it as a joke.

I always feel it when I strike someone. I don't know why. It swings forward on its chain and knocks against my breastbone, about where you'd put your hand if you were swearing an oath.

* * *

The call came as I was leaving the gallery. It came on my Office handset with a priority tone I'd heard perhaps three times in my career, and it was Lucan Marsh himself, the Chief Assessor, which I'd never heard at all.

"Hollis." His voice was strange. "Where are you?"

"Gallows Reach. I've just closed the Haskell default."

"Leave it. Take the next Office ferry. Come straight to the Index." A pause, and then, in a voice I'd known for twenty-nine years and had never heard do what it did then: "It's Ottilie."

I took the next Office ferry.

From Tamar you go up to the Hulk by ferry from the Saltmarch ice port, a forty-minute burn in a squat armoured hopper with forty seats and no windows, and if you're an Assessor you go up in the forward cabin with the House stewards and the Clear clerks and you don't look at the ballast in the back. I looked at nothing. I sat with my hands on my knees and counted the burn — forty-one minutes, a minute long — and thought about nothing at all, very carefully, until we docked.

Increase Station rides at the inner Lagrange point between Tamar and Absalom, where the pull of the moon and the pull of the giant balance, held there by its station-keeping engines like a stone balanced on a blade. At the centre of it is the Hulk. Everything else — the docks, the radiator vanes, the spoke lifts, the great slow-turning ring of the Crown where the Houses live — was built onto her over three centuries, but she's still there at the heart of it, if you know how to look: a long black seed-shaped spine eleven hundred metres from bow to stern, scarred and patched and studded with later work, but with her old Charter lines still showing under it. At her bow is the dome they used to call the Lantern, where her captains stood. The Houses use it as a chapel now. At her stern, where the fold coil once was, there's a cluster of foundries. And down the length of her spine, in the zero gravity where the Founders lived when they first came, is the Office of the Account.

You move through the Office hand over hand. The handholds are three hundred years old, brass, worn into shallow saddles by the palms of generations of Keepers and Assessors and clerks, and the walls are lined with the old Charter fittings — conduits, hatch frames, the faded red and yellow hazard stripes of a ship that once carried thousands of people asleep across the dark. The Founders kept as much of her as they could. Out of reverence, the Houses say. The Office of the Account occupies her old purser's deck and the stores around it, and the Index occupies the long gallery where her crew once kept spares, and the Account itself — the master lattice, the great grown block of crystal in which every portion on Tamar is written — sits in her old strongroom behind a door of black iron, under four words of red paint that have been touched up so many times the letters have gone soft and swollen, like the knuckles of an old man.

WHAT IS WRITTEN IS OWED.

I touched the doorframe as I went past, as everyone does. Then I went down the gallery to the Index, and there were Office proctors at the hatch in their black coats, and Bastian Oduya was waiting for me with a face the colour of cheese.

"Assessor Fen. Sir. The Chief's with her. He said to bring you straight—"

"I know what he said."

The Index is the Office's memory. The Account is the law — every portion, every payment, every strike and Clearance, grown into the master lattice under seal — but nobody can read lattice fast, so for three hundred years the Keepers have maintained a parallel record on film: index cards, millions of them, in steel cabinets that line the gallery floor to ceiling on both sides and in rows down the middle, with narrow aisles between, so that walking the Index in zero gravity is like swimming down a canyon of drawers. Each card gives a portion's number, its family, its holder, its standing, and the coordinates in the lattice where the full entry lives. The living accounts are near the hatch. The Clear accounts are further in. Right at the end, in the old cabinets nobody opens, are the dead accounts — portions of families that died out or were extinguished, closed and cross-referenced and left.

Ottilie Brask was in aisle nine, among the Clearances. Someone had tied her to a cabinet handle by the belt of her coat, so that she wouldn't drift, and she hung there with her arms floating up a little in front of her and her grey hair spread out around her face like weed in water, and her head was at an angle no living head can make.

I'd known her for twenty-nine years. She'd been Keeper of the Index for twenty-two of them. When I was twelve, a ballast boy from the bottom of Gallows Reach with an Office scholarship and a number for a name, she'd been the one who taught me to read lattice — not the Account, which no junior was allowed to touch, but the training blocks, old Charter crystals with nothing on them but practice entries. *You read it like the rings of a tree*, she'd told me. *Every entry grows a layer. Look at the layer and you can see when it was written. Look at how thick it is, how even. You can tell if the purser's hand was steady. You can tell if they were tired. You can tell, sometimes, if they were lying.* She'd been fifty-four then, with ink on her fingers; she still wrote her notes on paper, which nobody did, and her fingers were always stained a little blue at the tips. I'd thought she was the cleverest person in the world. I'd thought that until I was about twenty, and then I'd stopped thinking it, and then at about thirty-five I'd started thinking it again.

On the cabinet face beside her, someone had sprayed three words in red paint.

WE WERE CARGO.

"The Hold," said Bastian behind me. He said it the way people said it in the Crown, as if it were a disease. "They've been painting it all over the Saltmarch docks. Sir, the proctors think—"

"I know what the proctors think."

Lucan Marsh was on the far side of her, holding a cabinet with one hand. He was sixty-eight, tall and stooping, with a long clever face and soft white hair, and he wore the Chief Assessor's chain over his grey coat — a heavy thing of brass links with the Office's scales on a medallion — as if it were a weight he'd been asked to carry for someone else. He'd lifted me out of Gallows Reach. He'd found me at twelve doing sums in the dirt outside the schoolroom, sums the teacher had set for the seniors, and he'd crouched down beside me in his good coat and checked them, and then he'd asked me my name. I'd told him my number. *No*, he'd said, *your name*, and I'd told him, and he'd said, *Well, Hollis Fen, how would you like to come up and see the stars?* For twenty-nine years he'd been the nearest thing to a father I'd had. I'd hated him for some of them and loved him for the rest, and I'd never once in all that time seen him look the way he looked over Ottilie's body.

"Hollis," he said. "Thank God."

"When?"

"Last night. The cleaners found her an hour into the morning watch. Doctor Vasquez thinks between twenty-one and midnight." He looked at the paint. "There's an Office ferry log entry. Two unregistered passengers from Saltmarch, yesterday evening, on the twenty o'clock. Ballast tickets. They didn't take the return."

"That's quick work."

"The proctors are very quick when an officer of the Office is murdered." His voice cracked. He steadied it. "I want you on it. Not the proctors. You. I want whoever did this found and struck and I want it done before the Burning. The Concordance verifier arrives in two days, Hollis, and the Speaker is—" He stopped, and something passed over his face, a weariness or a distaste. "The Speaker would prefer that the Concordance not arrive to find the Office of the Account in mourning for a Keeper murdered by the Hold. You understand."

"I understand."

"You're the best I have. You knew her." He put his free hand on my shoulder, briefly, the way he had at the ferry port when I was twelve. "Find them."

He went. Bastian hovered.

I looked at Ottilie for a long time. Then I started counting.

The paint was Charter script. That was the first thing. The Hold has been painting *WE WERE CARGO* on walls across Tamar for six years, ever since the first Concordance survey ship came out of the Long Lane and the Houses began talking about integration, and they paint it in ice-script — the blocky, run-together letters miners use, that you can cut into a gallery wall with a lance tip in four strokes. Every proctor in Saltmarch knows that. This was neat, upright Charter capitals, the kind they teach you in the Office's schoolroom. The kind someone would write if they'd seen the slogan on a newsfeed and never on a wall.

The paint had been sprayed over her outflung left hand. There was red on her knuckles. But there were no drips under them on the cabinet face, and in zero gravity paint doesn't drip, it beads and drifts — and there were no beads in the air of aisle nine, not one, which meant the paint had dried long enough before anyone found her that the air scrubbers had cleaned out the overspray. Fine. That fitted. What didn't fit was that there were tiny beads of red, dried hard, caught in the weave of her coat on the *other* side of her body — the side against the cabinet. She'd been turned round after she was painted. Or painted first and tied up after.

Her fingers were clean.

I looked at them twice to be sure. Ottilie Brask wrote on paper. She'd written on paper every working day of her life, with a steel-nibbed pen she refilled from a bottle of blue-black ink she'd had sent up from a craftsman in Saltmarch, and in twenty-nine years I had never seen the tips of her right fingers without that faint blue stain on them. They were pink and clean as a child's. She'd scrubbed them. You scrub your fingers before you put on gloves, if you're careful about the gloves, if you're going to handle something you don't want to mark.

And her spectacles were folded in the breast pocket of her coat. Not on her face. Not on their cord. Folded, the way you fold them when you're not going to read anything for a while. When you're going to talk to someone instead.

"Sir?" said Bastian.

"Get me the ferry manifest," I said. "The real one, not the proctors' summary. And the Index access log for the last three days. And find out who cleans aisle nine."

"Yes, sir. Sir — do you think it's the Hold?"

He was twenty-four. He was House-client stock, the son of a steward in House Temperance, and he'd been my junior for a year, and he was eager in the way the Crown makes its children eager — to be useful, to be seen being useful, to rise. I liked him well enough. He made good tea.

"I think," I said, "someone wants me to think so."

* * *

They'd put her in the Office's little medical bay, in a cold drawer, and after the proctors and the doctor and the photographers had all done what they did, I asked to be left alone with her for ten minutes, and because I was an Assessor of the Second Rank and Lucan Marsh's man, they let me.

I don't know what I meant to do. Say something, perhaps. People do. I'd watched them do it, in the galleries of Gallows Reach, over bodies that had come back from the Deep Ice wrapped in sacking: they'd lean over and say things in low voices, things they'd been meaning to say, and I'd stand in the doorway with my tally and wait to record the transfer. I'd always wondered what they said. I'd never thought to ask.

I pulled the drawer out and looked at her and couldn't think of anything.

She'd been dressed for the drawer in a plain grey shift, and her own clothes were in a bag clipped to the end of it. Her shoes were in the bag too. They were good Saltmarch shoes, soft leather, with a little heel she'd always said was her one vanity, and the left one was slightly heavier than the right.

I wouldn't have noticed if I hadn't been holding them. I'm not sure I'd have noticed then if I hadn't spent twenty years lifting things in other people's houses and wondering what was under them. I turned the shoe over. The heel had been hollowed out, very neatly, and plugged with a disc of leather that had been glued and then trimmed flush, and when I worked the disc out with the tip of my pocket knife a card fell into my palm.

An Index card. Standard film, standard size, folded twice. Not a printed card — a blank, of the kind Keepers keep for cross-references. On it, in Ottilie's handwriting, in blue-black ink, was written:

*D-0447*

Nothing else.

D for the dead accounts. Four-four-seven. A portion number. Somewhere at the end of the Index, in the old cabinets nobody opens, there was a closed account of a family long extinct, and that was its number, and Ottilie Brask had scrubbed her fingers and folded her spectacles and gone to meet someone in the middle of the night with that number hidden in the heel of her shoe.

I turned the card over. Nothing on the back.

I sat there for a while holding it. The number tickled at something. I couldn't think what. I've a memory for numbers, as I said — I remember three hundred and eight strikes, and every portion I've ever closed, and the overtime rates at the refinery belts — and this one sat at the edge of my mind like a word you can't quite reach, familiar the way a face on a ferry is familiar, someone you might have stood next to once. I turned it over and over. *Four-four-seven*. It wouldn't come.

My handset chimed. Not the Office tone; the personal one.

I nearly didn't answer. Then I saw the name and answered it at once, the way I always did, in case it was the one time it was bad.

"Da?"

"Hello, love."

"Are you on the ice? Mam says you were on the ice today. She says there was a default."

"I'm on the Hulk." I closed the drawer, quietly, so she wouldn't hear it. "I had to come up. Work."

"Oh." Wren was fifteen. She lived with her mother in Saltmarch, in a Clear-quarter flat I paid half the rent on, and she had a voice that still went up at the ends of things like a much younger child's when she was worried, and down like a much older woman's when she was angry, and right now it was doing the first. "I just wanted to ask. About the — you know."

"I know."

"Because it's eleven days to my birthday. And it's twelve days to the Burning, and Mam says if it's not done by the Burning it has to wait till after the new quarter, because of the holiday, and that's four months."

"I know, love."

"How much is it now?"

"Four hundred," I said. "Four hundred and six, if you want to be exact."

"Four hundred and six," Wren said. She said it the way you'd say the height of a mountain. Her mother's family had Cleared two generations back; Imke was born Clear and had never thought about it in her life. I wasn't. I'd been born with a portion, like everyone in Gallows Reach, and I'd paid it off myself by twenty-eight on an Assessor's wages, and I'd paid half Wren's since she was born, because a child of one Clear parent and one Cleared parent still inherits the Cleared parent's share of the founding Liberty. That's the law. It's very fair. Everyone owes for their own freedom; it's only right. And for fifteen years, every quarter, I'd paid double into Wren's portion, so that she would be Clear on her sixteenth birthday and never have to stand in a gallery with her son in the back room and listen to someone like me say a figure in a level voice. Four hundred and six marks. Two months' wages, if I didn't eat.

"Will it be done?" she said. "By my birthday? Da?"

I looked at the closed drawer, and at the card in my hand, and at the little steel bag with Ottilie's good shoes in it, one heavier than the other.

"It'll be done," I said. "I promise."

I didn't know yet that it was the first promise I'd make that month that I wouldn't be able to keep. I'd find out about the others in their turn. That's the thing about a ledger. You can't read the bottom line until you've written every entry above it, and by then, of course, it's far too late to change any of them.


# Chapter 3: Perigee

*The Salvage · CR 1317*

The Hulk is still hot when they go in.

It is always still hot. That is the joke the divers make, and like most jokes in the Gutter it is mostly true. Every three and a half days the Hulk swings down out of the dark on its long, failing ellipse and drags its belly through the top of Absalom's atmosphere, two hundred kilometres above the cloud deck, and the friction lights it up like a coal. From Tamar you can watch it happen — a red spark crossing the rust-coloured face of the giant, trailing a thin bright tail — and then it climbs away again, out toward apogee, cooling as it goes. The cold window is the day and a half near the top of the climb, when the outer hull has radiated enough of its heat that a shell can touch it without cooking the diver inside. Most crews wait for the middle of the window. Yara Tull goes in at the front of it, when the plates still glow faintly in infrared and the meltwater inside is warm enough to move, because that is when nobody else is there.

"Seven," says Mitya, over the suit band. "Did you see the bulletin? Assembly engineers say seven passes. Then she comes apart."

"They said nine last month."

"Last month she still had her Crown."

The sled drifts in on cold gas. Ahead of them the Hulk turns slowly end over end against the stars, black and enormous, eleven hundred metres of scorched iron with half its ring torn away. Whatever she was — and she was a great many things, Yara's mother used to say, a ship and a station and a seat of government and a prison and a church — she is now mostly a shape. Her leading face, the side that meets the air first at perigee, has been ablated smooth and glassy, the old hull plates fused into a single dark scab. Her trailing face is a ruin of torn superstructure, broken vanes, the stumps of docking arms, and from a hundred cracks in it, as she warms and cools and warms again, she bleeds thin white plumes of vapour that catch the sunlight and glitter and are gone. She looks, Yara thinks every time, like something dying of a fever. Which she is.

"Mouth's clear," says Mitya. "No lights."

"Good."

The Mouth is what the divers call the breach in the trailing hull near the old docks, a tear forty metres across where something hit her a long time ago and nobody ever patched it. Yara brings the sled in under its lip, matches the slow tumble with three short bursts, and clamps on. The vibration comes up through the clamps into her shell: a deep, irregular shuddering, as if the whole Hulk were breathing.

"She's loud today," says Mitya.

"She's always loud after perigee."

"She's louder." He unclips from the sled and pushes off toward the hull, a bulky shape in a patched orange shell with a cutter on his back and a coil of line on his hip. "Fen's restless."

"Fen's dead."

"Fen's in there somewhere, counting." He says it in the singsong all divers use, the words worn smooth with saying, and then he laughs, because he always does, and she can hear in the laugh that today, for once, he means it a little.

* * *

Inside, the water is everywhere.

It has taken three hundred years of slow freezing for the Hulk's interior to become what it is now, and thirty years of falling for it to start becoming something else. For most of the Long Dimming she was a sealed iron bottle full of ice — every flooded compartment, every burst pipe, every tank and conduit and drowned room frozen solid in the dark, with her air leaked away and her heat long gone. When her station-keeping engines finally failed and she slid off her old balance point between Tamar and the giant and began to fall, the ice began to wake. Every perigee heats her a little more. Every perigee, a little more of the ice turns to water, and the water does what water does in a hull that is falling freely through space: it clings. It forms skins on every surface. It fills the sealed rooms completely and trembles there, held in by doors that were shut three centuries ago. In the open corridors it drifts in globes the size of a fist or the size of a man, wobbling, merging, splitting, catching the beams of the divers' lamps and throwing them back in slow shivering patterns across the walls.

Yara loves it. She has never said so to anyone. It is the most beautiful thing she knows.

They go in through the outer ring, along a corridor whose walls are lined with water like the inside of a throat. Their lamps make moving rooms of light in the dark. At the first junction there is a hatch they rigged eleven days ago, a crude airlock of welded plate and a salvaged door seal that lets them into the flooded section beyond without draining it out into the void, and they go through one at a time, and on the other side the world becomes liquid.

Diving the Hulk is not like swimming. There is no up and no down; there is only the water, and the walls somewhere in it, and the line on your hip that goes back the way you came. The water is cold and grey with silt and full of things. Paper — real paper, from the Houses' time, drifting in pulped grey clouds. Fragments of furniture. Wiring. A child's shoe. At one turning, a portrait in a gilt frame, face down, slowly rotating. Yara pushes it aside with one gauntlet and it turns over as it goes, and for a moment her lamp catches the face of a woman in a high collar, painted very finely, with an expression of perfect contempt, and then it is gone into the dark behind her.

The walls in this part of the ring are panelled in some pale stone that has gone green in the wet, and carved into the panels, at intervals, in tall elegant letters, are words. CONSTANCE. CONSTANCE. CONSTANCE. The old House names. They are all through the ring. Divers use them for navigation: you go left at PRUDENCE and through the arch at TEMPERANCE and if you find yourself at CLEMENCY you've gone too far and you'll want to watch for the vent shaft. Nobody remembers what the Houses were, exactly. They were rich, and they lived up here in the turning ring with gardens and real stone while everybody else lived in the ice, and then they were gone. Some of the families in Saltmarch with good flats still claim them. Nobody believes them.

"Here," says Mitya.

The salvage tonight is copper. There is a junction room off the CONSTANCE corridor where the ring's old power bus came through, and the bus bars are solid copper as thick as a wrist, and copper is fetching forty-two the kilo at Gorse's scale this month because the refineries in Saltmarch are rebuilding their furnace coils. They found the room on the last dive. They have nine hours of window and four hours of air.

They work. It is slow work, and hard, and in the water every movement costs twice what it would in air. Mitya cuts. Yara braces the bar, and when it comes free she wrestles it into the bag on her back, and they move to the next. The cutter's arc boils the water around it into a cloud of hissing bubbles that tremble away into the dark, and every few minutes the Hulk groans around them — a long low iron sound that comes through the water and into the shell and into Yara's teeth — and they both stop, and wait, and when it passes they go on.

Between the third bar and the fourth, while Mitya is changing the cutter's tip, Yara drifts to the end of the junction room and finds a door.

It is not much of a door. Narrow, a service hatch, its seal long gone, so that it opens with a push into a little room beyond: a dozen square metres, flooded like everything else, with a desk bolted to the floor and a rack of empty shelves and a strongbox in one corner with its lid torn off. An office. There are a lot of little offices in the ring. Most of them have been stripped already, years ago, by crews who dived the Hulk when it was still mostly ice and you went in with a thermal lance and came out with whatever you could carry. This one has not been touched. Its hatch was hidden behind the bus housings. On its back wall, in brass letters fixed to the stone, are the words OFFICE OF THE ACCOUNT — RING ANNEX, and beneath them, in smaller letters, a motto she can't read for the green crust over it.

The desk has one drawer. It is stuck. She braces her boots against the desk and pulls, and the drawer comes out all at once in a cloud of silt, and something heavy tumbles slowly out of it into the beam of her lamp.

She catches it. It's a box. Brass, the size of a big book, with a lens of dull glass set into its face like an eye and a coil of fine wire wrapped around one side, and on its lid an emblem she half recognises: a sunburst, and under it a pair of scales. Under the emblem, stamped into the metal, are the same words as the wall. OFFICE OF THE ACCOUNT.

"What's that?" Mitya is in the doorway.

"Don't know. Brass."

"Brass is eleven the kilo."

"It's heavy brass."

"It's Office junk." He drifts closer and turns his lamp on it. "That's a seal-reader. My grandad had a picture of one. They used them to read the old wrist chips — your portion, your number, all that. Before the Dimming." He taps the coil with one finger. "That's a Charter-band aerial. They talked to each other on the old band. It's worth nothing, Yara. Nobody's used the Charter band in two hundred years except the Ash bell."

"It's worth eleven the kilo."

"It's worth eleven the kilo," he agrees, and laughs, and goes back to the copper.

She keeps it. She couldn't say why. She clips it to her harness, where it hangs against her hip like a second heart, heavy and cold, and she goes back to work.

* * *

On the way out, the Hulk tries to kill them.

It is not personal. The Hulk tries to kill everyone eventually. They are three bars up and two hours from the end of their air, working their way back along the line toward the airlock, when the long low groan comes again through the water, louder than before, and keeps coming, and the walls of the CONSTANCE corridor begin very slowly to move.

"Slosh," says Yara.

It happens when the Hulk's tumble shifts — when some unseen mass of ice deep in her breaks loose, or a compartment fails, and her spin wobbles, and all the free water in her responds at once. In the open corridors it's a current. In the flooded sections it's a wall. She sees it coming, a grey thickening of the silt at the far end of the corridor, a rolling cloud of paper and debris advancing on them like weather, and she has time to grab the nearest handhold — an old brass rail, worn smooth — and shout Mitya's name.

The current hits. It is like being taken by a hand. Her body is pulled out straight from the rail, her bag of copper dragging at her shoulders, the seal-reader slamming against her hip, the silt so thick that her lamp shows her nothing but a blind grey glow. Something big tumbles past her in it — the portrait, she thinks absurdly, the woman with the contemptuous face — and strikes her helmet and is gone. Her gauntlets are locked on the rail. She can feel the line on her hip go taut and then go slack, taut and slack, as Mitya is thrown about at the end of it.

Then it passes. The water slows, and settles, and the silt begins slowly to clear, and she is hanging from the rail with her arms on fire and her heart going like a pump.

"Mitya."

Nothing.

"*Mitya.*"

"Here." His voice is thin. "Here. I'm here. I'm caught on something. Hold on."

He is twenty metres down the corridor, wedged against a doorframe where the current threw him, his line wrapped twice around a broken stanchion. His shell is scraped bright along one side. He is laughing, in a high, unsteady way she doesn't like. "Did you see that? Did you see it? The whole ring moved. That was the whole ring."

"Are you hurt?"

"My pride." He unwinds the line. "And my bar. I lost a bar."

"We've got three."

"We had four." He pushes off from the doorframe and comes up the corridor toward her, slowly, and stops beside her at the rail. Through the faceplates, in the lamp light, she can see his face — the long Saltmarch face, the stubble, the eyes that are always laughing at something just behind you. They are not laughing now. "She's coming apart, Yara," he says quietly. "Seven passes. You can feel it."

"I know."

They go out by the spine.

It's not the fastest way, but the slosh has filled the ring corridor behind them with debris, and the spine route is clear. The spine is the old core of the Hulk, the long weightless shaft that runs from her bow to her stern, and it is the part of her that is most a ship. The Houses and the Office and the Dimming and the falling have changed her ring past recognising, but the spine is still Charter work: wide square corridors with hazard stripes on the walls in faded red and yellow, hatches with heavy dogged wheels, and everywhere the neat stencilled labels of a ship that once carried people across the dark. HOLD ACCESS. PURSER'S DECK. BALLAST 3. BALLAST 4.

The fourth ballast tank is sealed. Every diver knows it. It sits against the spine in the dark near the old Hold, and its access hatch is a great round iron door, dogged shut and welded at the rim with a bead of slag three centuries old, and riveted to the middle of it is a plate in the Office's hand: CONDEMNED — BY ORDER OF THE OFFICE OF THE ACCOUNT. Below the Office's plate someone has painted, much more recently and much more crudely, a skull, and beside the skull two names and a year. BRAM. OLE. '16.

Bram Osgood and Ole Varrick. They cut the weld on the Widow last spring, on a bet, at the end of a long cold window, and went in. They were good divers. Ole's line came out four days later on its own, cut clean. Nobody went in after them. Something about the Widow — the way she's built, the way the current runs in her when the ice thaws — makes her a trap. Gorse welded the hatch shut again himself and painted the skull. Divers say she's full of ice still, at the bottom, and the ice moves.

As they pass her, Yara hears it. She doesn't mean to stop. She stops. Through the iron, through the water in the corridor and the water in the tank, comes a slow, dull knocking — irregular, faint, like something heavy turning over and over in its sleep.

"Ice," says Mitya.

"I know it's ice."

"Come on. Air."

She goes. She doesn't look back. But she can feel it behind her for a long time, the knocking, coming up the line, until the airlock closes on it.

* * *

Gorse weighs the copper at forty-one the kilo, not forty-two, because the bars have been cut short and he says short bars are harder to sell. Nobody argues. You don't argue with Gorse on his own scale. He is a vast soft man who has not been inside a shell in twenty years, with a beard dyed the colour of rust and three rings on each hand, and he runs the biggest salvage concession on the Hulk under licence from the Warden of Tamar, and every crew that dives the Hulk dives it on his permit or not at all. He takes sixty per cent. He takes it first, before the weight is even off the scale, and then he takes what you owe him, and then he gives you what's left in Saltmarch scrip, which is printed on plastic film and is worth slightly less every week.

"And that," he says, tapping the seal-reader where it sits on the scale beside the copper.

"I'm keeping that."

"Brass. Eleven the kilo."

"I'm keeping it."

Gorse looks at her for a moment with his small wet eyes. Then he shrugs, an immense slow movement, like the Hulk shifting. "Keep it. Your mother kept junk too." He gives her a slip of film. "Mitya stays. We need to talk about his book."

Mitya's book is the ledger of what he owes Gorse, which is a great deal. Yara does not look at him as she goes. He does not look at her.

The Gutter is what everyone calls the cluster of habitation cans clamped to the underside of Ringfall dock, in low orbit over Tamar. Ringfall was built in the Houses' time to service ferries to the Hulk, and when the Hulk was abandoned the dock was too, and the divers moved in — first into its empty offices and stores, then into the cans they bolted to its hull when the offices filled up. There are perhaps four thousand people in the Gutter now. Divers, mostly, and their families, and the people who sell to divers: welders, shell-fitters, scrip-changers, the noodle cart on the main concourse, three bars, a clinic that is open on alternate days. It smells of scorched metal and fish sauce and too many people breathing the same air. Its corridors are lit by strings of salvaged bulbs. On its walls, layered over each other, are a hundred years of divers' marks: names, dates, tallies of dives, the House names copied out of the Hulk for luck, and everywhere the little scratched skull that means someone didn't come back.

There's a crowd at the concourse screen.

Yara notices it because there's never a crowd at the concourse screen. It shows the Warden's bulletins, the perigee schedule, the copper price. Today there are two hundred people packed in front of it, and nobody is talking, and on the screen is a ship.

It's not a ferry. It's not anything she's seen before. Long and narrow, black, with a hard bright sheen like oil on water, and along its flank in tall white letters a name: LAWFUL REMEDY. Under the name, smaller, a crest — a key crossed with a sword, inside a circle of words too small to read. It hangs in Tamar orbit against the stars, very still, very clean.

"What is it?" she asks a woman beside her.

The woman doesn't look round. "Bailiffs," she says.

The Warden's face replaces the ship. Corin Asch is sixty, heavy-jowled, with the high colour of a man who drinks; he has been Warden of Tamar for twenty-two years, which is longer than any Warden in living memory, and he has the trick of looking at a camera as though it has disappointed him personally. He reads from a slate.

"— from the High Chancery of Vey, under the Accords of Settled Space, a writ of execution in favour of the Paschal Recovery Company, assignee of record of the instrument known as the Account of Absalom." He pauses. He seems to be reading it for the first time. "The Recovery Company holds a certified copy of the said instrument, under the seal of its original issuers, and asserts that the obligations recorded therein were never discharged and have accrued interest under the terms of the instrument since the date of its last entry." He looks up. "The Wardenship has examined the writ and finds it in order. Registration of liable persons will commence on Tamar and Ringfall tomorrow at the eighth hour. All residents are required to present themselves. Further notices will follow."

The screen goes back to the ship.

For a moment, nobody says anything. Then someone laughs — a man near the front, an old diver Yara half knows, with one arm. "The Account?" he says. "The *Account*?" He looks round at them, grinning, inviting them to share it. "That's a story. That's a Burning story. They burned it. My grandmother told me. They burned it on the ice and danced on the ash."

"They burned the Book," says someone else. "Not the Account."

"Same thing."

"It's not the same thing."

Then everyone is talking at once.

Yara doesn't stay. She knows the stories as well as anyone — the Book and the Burning, the Purser who would have sold them, the Houses and their Liberty, the Account, Fen the Turncoat on the Night of the Hulk — and she knows that stories are what people tell each other when nobody can remember what happened. None of it has anything to do with copper. She pushes out of the crowd and down the concourse toward home, and as she goes she hears, faint and clear above the noise, the Ash bell ringing the hour.

It isn't a bell. It's a chime on the old Charter band, broadcast from the Ash Church's tower on the ice above Saltmarch, where someone two hundred years ago rigged a salvaged array to sound the hours for the faithful. Every radio in the system picks it up. Seven notes, falling. On Ringfall they come through the concourse speakers, and the divers' suit bands, and the cheap receivers in the noodle cart; and for once, tonight, everyone in the Gutter stops talking to listen to them, as if they might mean something.

At her hip, the brass box she took from the drowned office gives a single faint answering click.

She looks down at it. Nothing. Old brass, dull glass, a coil of wire. She keeps walking.

* * *

Pim is coughing when she gets in.

Their can is one of the old ones, eight metres by three, with two bunks and a galley and a curtain across the end where the toilet is. Their mother bought it the year Pim was born. It still has her diving marks on the inside of the hatch: two hundred and nine dives, scratched in fives, and then nothing, because the two hundred and tenth dive was the one she didn't come back from. Yara has never added hers below. Pim says she should. She says she will when she gets to two hundred and ten.

He's on the top bunk with a blanket round his shoulders and a slate on his knees, coughing into his sleeve, a long rough barking that goes on and on and leaves him grey. Rime-lung. Fourteen years of breathing Gutter air with ice-dust in it, the doctors at the alternate-day clinic say, though a doctor on Vey would probably say something else. On Vey they could fix it. On Vey there are clinics with machines that clean the scarring out of a lung like scrubbing a pot. The passage to Vey alone is eight thousand marks. The treatment is more than that. She's been counting it for three years.

"You're late," he says, when he can.

"Copper was heavy."

"You're bleeding."

She looks. There's a smear of blood on the inside of her wrist where the shell's seal chafed when the slosh hit. "Not mine," she says, which is a lie, and he knows it, and lets it go, which is one of the things she loves him for.

"Did you see?" He holds up the slate. On it, small and grainy, is the black ship. "Lawful Remedy. It's from Vey. It came through the lane yesterday and nobody said anything until today. Yara, they're saying it's the *Account*."

"I saw."

"But the Account was a — it was the Houses' thing. The Liberty Debt. That was three hundred years ago." Pim reads everything. He's read everything in the Gutter's little lending library twice, and everything the Ash Church will let him borrow, and he collects old words the way other boys collect salvage: songs and sayings and the names of things that don't exist any more. "How can anyone owe something from three hundred years ago?"

"Ask the bailiffs."

"I'm asking you."

She unclips the seal-reader and puts it on the galley shelf and starts peeling off the inner suit. "I don't know, Pim. Interest, I suppose. Someone always owes something." She looks at him. "Did you take your drops?"

"Yes." He's looking past her, at the door. "Yara. There's someone here."

She turns.

There's a woman sitting on the step at the end of the galley, in the curtain's shadow, where Yara didn't see her when she came in. She's perhaps fifty, with a plain broad face and greying hair pinned up, and she's dressed the way nobody in the Gutter dresses: in good grey wool that fits her, and soft boots, and a coat with a collar. She has her hands folded on her knees. She is entirely still, the way people are still when they've been waiting a long time and are used to it.

"I'm sorry," she says. "Your brother let me in. I told him I'd wait. My name is Sabeth Orrin."

Yara doesn't move. "What do you want?"

"To hire you."

"I dive for Gorse."

"I know. I've spoken to Gorse. He's agreed to lend you to me, for a fee." Sabeth Orrin smiles slightly. "He was very reasonable about it. I find people usually are, once they understand how much money is involved."

"How much?"

"Fifty thousand marks." The woman says it the way you'd say the time. "Ten now, forty on delivery. Vey scrip, not Saltmarch. Paid wherever you like."

On the top bunk, Pim has stopped coughing. Yara can feel him holding his breath.

Fifty thousand marks is Vey and the clinic and the machines that scrub the lungs clean, and passage back, and Gorse's book paid off, and the can, and the rest of their lives. Fifty thousand marks is more money than Yara's mother made in two hundred and nine dives. It's the kind of figure that only means one thing in the Gutter. Somebody wants something out of the Hulk very badly, and they want a diver who'll die getting it.

"For what?" she says.

Sabeth Orrin leans forward, out of the shadow, into the light of the galley lamp.

"You know the stories," she says. "Everybody does. The Night of the Hulk. The Houses and the Hold. Fen the Turncoat." She says the name carefully, as if it were a thing that might break. "On the Night of the Hulk, an Assessor of the Office of the Account named Hollis Fen stole something from the Office. Something the people who lost it would have killed to keep. It was never found."

"Fen's cache," says Pim from the bunk, very softly.

"Fen's cache," Sabeth agrees, without looking round. "The divers have a name for everything, don't they? Yes. That."

"It's a story," says Yara. "There are a hundred stories about the Hulk. Fen's cache, the Houses' gold, the Purser's ghost. Fen sold the Hold to the Concordance and ran. Everyone knows that. If he stole something he took it with him."

"Perhaps." Sabeth takes a folded film out of her coat and holds it out. "Or perhaps he didn't get the chance. If he'd taken it out of the Hulk, someone would have used it by now. Nobody has. So I think it's still there." She waits, holding out the film, until Yara takes it. "Traitors steal useful things, Miss Tull. That's what makes them traitors.
