And I need it found before the Hulk comes apart. Seven passes, the Assembly says. I'd like it in my hands in six."

Yara unfolds the film. It's a plan — old, copied and recopied, the lines gone soft and fuzzy: the spine of the Hulk, deck by deck, with the Office of the Account marked in red along the middle of it, and the old Index gallery, and the strongroom. Someone has annotated it, in a neat small hand, with the depths of ice and water as they were mapped thirty years ago.

"Why me?" she says.

"Because you go in at the front of the window when no one else is there," says Sabeth Orrin. "And because you need the money more than anyone else Gorse showed me." She stands up. She is shorter than Yara expected. "I'll come back tomorrow for your answer. Don't tell anyone what I've asked. Don't tell Gorse what it's for. He thinks I want copper." She nods to Pim, politely, as if he were an adult. "Goodnight."

She goes. The hatch closes behind her.

For a while neither of them says anything. Pim coughs, quietly, into his sleeve. The can hums around them — the air pump, the heater, the faint vibration of Ringfall's hull. Through the wall, from the can next door, someone is singing a diver's song, slow and out of tune, the way people sing when they've been drinking since the bulletin:

*Heave the iron, heave it slow,
down the Widow, down we go —*

"Are you going to do it?" says Pim.

Yara looks at the plan. She looks at the brass box on the galley shelf, with its dull glass eye. She looks at the inside of the hatch, at their mother's marks: two hundred and nine, in fives, and nothing under them.

"I'll think about it," she says.

She has already decided. They both know it. She folds the plan very small and puts it inside her shirt, against her skin, where she can feel its edges; and that night, lying on the bottom bunk listening to Pim breathe, she counts the passes the way her mother taught her to count dives. Seven. Seven passes, and then the Hulk comes apart, and whatever is in it goes down into Absalom forever and is never seen again.

Seven. She lies awake counting until the Ash bell rings the next hour, seven falling notes on the old band, and at the galley shelf, in the dark, the brass box clicks once more, softly, as if it were answering.


# Chapter 4: Article One

*The Crossing · CR 611*

On the third morning after the courier, Annick woke to silence.

It took her a moment to understand what she was hearing. The ring turned; the ventilation hummed; somewhere above her on the fourth deck a hatch banged and someone laughed. Everything was as it always was. But for eleven years, every morning, she had woken to the sound of Aurelio Benn coughing through the bulkhead — a long dry rattle that began at the change of watch and went on, with pauses, until he had dressed and taken his first strong tea — and this morning there was nothing on the other side of the wall but the faint, steady hiss of his concentrator.

She lay and listened to the hiss for perhaps ten seconds. Then she got up and went next door without dressing.

He was lying on his back on his bunk with the blanket pulled up to his chest and the oxygen line over his ears, and his eyes were half open, and his lips were the colour of a bruise. The concentrator was clipped to the rail beside him where he always put it at night. Its little indicator glowed green. It was working perfectly. It was hissing pure oxygen down the line into the nose of a man who had stopped breathing some hours before.

She did not touch him. She stood in the doorway in her sleeping shift, in the ring's light gravity, and she did a thing she would think about for a long time afterward: she counted. Not her breaths. His. She counted to sixty, watching his chest under the blanket, as if there were some figure it might reach. Then she went to the comm on the wall and called the doctor.

Yusuf Haddad came up from the infirmary in four minutes with his bag and his embarrassed face. He knelt by the bunk and did the things doctors do, briefly, and then he sat back on his heels and was quiet for a while.

"His lungs," he said at last. "It was always going to be his lungs. I told him a year. I'm sorry, Annick."

"In his sleep?"

"It looks like it." He reached up and took the oxygen line gently from the old man's face and held the end of it against the back of his own hand, feeling the flow. Then he unclipped the concentrator from the rail and turned it over and looked at its readout for a long moment. He frowned.

"What is it?"

"Nothing." He set it down. "The reservoir's full. That's all."

"Is that wrong?"

"It's—" Haddad hesitated. He was a careful man; he disliked saying things he could not prove. "It's unexpected. If he'd been drawing on it all night, it would be lower. Unless he stopped drawing on it early." He rubbed his face. "It doesn't mean anything. He might have taken the line off in his sleep and put it back. People do strange things when they can't breathe."

"Can you check it?"

"Check it for what?"

She did not know. She stood looking down at Benn's face — the long mournful face, the down of white hair lying flat against the pillow in the gravity — and found that she could not say it.

"I'll keep it," said Haddad, after a moment, quietly. He wrapped the concentrator in a towel from the rail and put it in his bag. "For the record. I'll look at it properly when I have time." He looked up at her. "You should get dressed. And you should take the key, before anyone else comes."

* * *

The master key hung on a cord around Benn's neck, under his shift. It was identical to her own except for the number on the shank, 0001, and the bead in its bow, which was larger and older and very slightly cracked. She lifted the cord over his head as gently as if he could feel it. Then she dressed, and put both keys inside her collar, and went down the spoke to the spine and along the dim weightless corridor to the strongroom, with the dead man's key lying cold against her breastbone beside her own.

The Ordinances were precise about the death of a purser, because the death of a purser was the most dangerous moment in the life of a ship. A Book with two keys and one purser was a Book with a spare. A key without a hand to hold it was a key that might be found, or bought, or taken. So the Charter required that when a purser died, his key went with him. The assistant's key became the master. The dead man's key was broken in the strongroom, before witnesses, and the pieces were committed with his body, so that there would never again be more keys than there were people entitled to hold them.

She had sent for witnesses: Haddad, who came, and the Master or his deputy, and the Factor. Lindqvist sent his apologies and the Third Officer, a nervous young man named Brandt who kept wiping his hands on his trousers. Quarry came himself. He floated in the strongroom doorway in his beautiful coat with his soft hands folded in front of him and watched her with polite attention, as he might have watched a clerk at a counter.

The bead press was bolted to the wall below the Ordinances: a small steel vice with a hardened jaw, designed for one purpose. Annick set Benn's key in it with the bead between the jaws. She turned the handle. The lattice resisted, and resisted, and then went with a sound like a knuckle cracking, and when she opened the press the bead was a little heap of dull brown grit. She brushed it into an envelope. She sealed the envelope. She put the envelope and the broken key into a second envelope, and sealed that, and wrote on it in her plain clerk's hand *A. Benn, Purser, Voyage V. Key 0001, broken*, and handed it to Haddad to put in the shroud.

Then she turned to the cradle.

It was only a box. She had lifted it a thousand times. She had dusted it and polished its hasp and checked its seals and opened it to make entries, and carried it in her arms down this corridor and back, the year they reshielded the strongroom at Callis. She knew its weight to the gram. But she understood, as she put her hands on it, that she had never before lifted it with no one else in the world entitled to lift it after her, and she found, absurdly, that her hands would not close.

*Article One*, Benn said, in her head, in his patient teacher's voice. *The Purser is whosoever holds the Key and the Book together.*

She lifted the Book out of its cradle. In zero gravity it had no weight at all, only mass, so that it moved in her arms like a sleeping animal, reluctant to be disturbed. She held it against her chest. Her key was at her throat. There was a moment — very short, nothing anyone in the room could have seen — when something passed through her that she had no name for, and would not have used if she had: a sense of the whole ship around her, the Lantern and the ring and the coil and the Hold, three thousand six hundred and twelve sleepers in their frost and two hundred and forty waking and a hundred and eighteen crew, every life aboard written somewhere in the block of crystal she was holding, every one of them owed or owing. The Charter had a phrase for it. *The office devolves.* She had read it in the clerks' manual at seventeen and thought it sounded like something mechanical, a valve opening. It did not feel like a valve.

"Purser," said Edwin Quarry from the doorway, gently, like a man greeting someone at a party.

"Factor."

"My condolences. He was a man of great principle." He let that sit for a moment, so that she would hear it. "I'll expect you in the captain's day room at the second bell. There are matters of the voyage we need to discuss."

He went. The Third Officer went after him, wiping his hands. Haddad stayed.

Annick set the Book in the cradle and opened it with her key and put her thumb to the bead and made the entry. She gave the date and the hour and her own name and her new office. She gave Aurelio Benn's name and the manner of his death, as the doctor had certified it: *failure of the lungs, in sleep.* He had no debt. He had been Clear for forty years. His estate — some savings, a few books, a box of letters, the bottle of Callisian brandy in the privy box that he had been too frightened to drink — passed under the Ordinances to his sister in the Callis hill district, who was eighty-four and might or might not be alive to receive it.

"Sealed," she said.

"Play it back," said Haddad softly from behind her.

She did not want to. She did. The Book gave her voice back:

*— failure of the lungs, in sleep —*

And it shook. Not much. Only on *sleep*, very slightly, a tremor so small that she might not have heard it if she had not been listening for it. But it was there, grown into the lattice now, permanent. Someday, someone listening would be able to tell.

She closed the lid.

* * *

The captain's day room was a small panelled cabin off the Lantern, in the forward hub where the spine met the ring, so that it had a little gravity and a great deal of light. It had a table of real wood that Lindqvist's grandfather had carried out of Callis, and a sideboard with a decanter on it, and a viewport looking out at the dull red coal of Pell. There were four chairs at the table. The captain was in one, and the Factor in another, and the First Officer was not sitting at all but standing at the viewport with her back to the room. Annick took the fourth chair because there was nowhere else to stand.

Lindqvist looked as if he had not slept since the courier. He nodded to her heavily. "I'm sorry about Aurelio. He was the best purser I ever sailed with."

"Thank you, Captain."

"You've been told nothing."

"No, Captain."

"No. He wouldn't have." Lindqvist looked at the Factor. "Go on, Edwin. You've been waiting three days."

Quarry smiled slightly. He took a sheet of film from his coat and laid it on the table and turned it round so that she could read it, the way she turned a bill round for a bonded colonist. It was the courier's order. She recognised the Receivers' seal at the foot: the sunburst of the Charter, struck through with a bar, and above it the black tower of the Bank of Vey.

She read it. It did not take long. The Receivers wrote plainly.

*The Meridian Charter Company being in liquidation, all property of the Company is vested in the Receivers of the Bank of Vey, to be realised for the benefit of the Company's creditors. The vessel* Merciful Increase, *with all her stores and the debts of her voyage, is property of the Company. The Master is directed to divert the vessel at the Fork of the Long Lane to the Shale system, where the Receivers have contracted with the Shale Concern for the sale of the vessel and the assignment of the debts of her voyage at a price agreed. The Purser is directed to seal the assignment in the Book at the Fork, and to record the Master's authority as subject to the Concern's pilots on arrival. The ship's company will be paid off at Shale with a bonus of six months' wages in recognition of their service. The Factor will see to the execution of these directions.*

Below that, in the Factor's own hand, was a figure: the price the Shale Concern had agreed to pay for the debts of the hold. It was not very much. It was a great deal less than the debts were worth on the face of the Book. It was, she understood, the price of a bulk lot of something that would not last.

She read it again. Then she laid it face down on the table.

"Shale," she said.

"A fine opportunity," said Quarry. "The Concern's very well capitalised. Heavy industry. There's a great demand for labour."

"It's two-point-one gravities."

"I believe it's two-point-one-three."

"There's no colony at Shale. There's a mine." She did not look at him; she looked at the captain. "The Concern buys bonded debt from bankrupt carriers. It doesn't settle anyone. It works them."

"It employs them," said Quarry, "until their obligations are discharged. Which is exactly what would have happened at Absalom, Purser. The only difference is the address." He spread his soft hands. "And, of course, that Absalom no longer has anyone to receive them. The Charter's colony office at Absalom was a desk in a building on Callis that has been, I understand, burned down. There's no one there. There's a gas giant and a moon made of ice. If we take these people to Absalom we'll be dropping them on the ice with nothing — no stores, no tools, no relief ship, no market for anything they produce — and leaving. That isn't mercy. That's murder with extra steps."

"How long do they live at Shale?"

He did not answer. Lindqvist did not answer. At the viewport, Nadia Szell said without turning round, "Ask the doctor."

"I'm asking you, First Officer."

Szell turned. In the light from the port her eyes were very dark. "Six to nine years," she said. "For unacclimatised adult labour under the Concern's contract terms. The children do better, oddly. They grow into it." She said it flatly, without any expression at all, as she might have read off a fuel figure. "Haddad has the tables. The Concern publishes them. It's a very honest company."

"Thank you, Nadia," said Quarry.

"You asked."

Annick looked at the captain. "Captain Lindqvist."

"It's done, Purser." He was staring at the table. "It's the order of the owners. It's the law. I've got a hundred and eighteen crew who haven't been paid since Callis, and some of them have families on Callis, and Callis is on fire, and the only money any of them will ever see for this voyage is at Shale." He looked up, and she saw that his eyes were wet, and that he was ashamed of it, and that it would not change anything. "I don't like it. Aurelio didn't like it. He said so, in this room, for five hours. And I'll tell you what I told him. I'm sixty years old. I've carried bonded for the Charter for thirty-four years. I've never once asked where they ended up. I'm not going to start pretending now that I'm the sort of man who would."

"Purser Benn refused to seal it."

"Purser Benn," said Quarry gently, "is dead."

The room was quiet. Through the viewport, the coal of Pell burned on, dull red, giving no heat to anything.

"The Fork is thirty-five days out, at the present rate of cooling," said Quarry. "There's no hurry. I don't expect you to be easy with it today. I'd think less of you if you were. But I do expect you to understand it." He leaned forward a little, and for the first time his soft voice had something under it, like a blade under silk. "You're the only purser this ship has, Purser Vell. The only key. If you die, the Book is locked, the debts can't be assigned, and the Concern won't pay — and then there's no bonus and no wages, and a ship full of very angry crew at Shale, with a hold full of bonded who can't be sold and can't be fed." He let that settle. "So you see, it's very important to all of us that you stay well. And that the people you care for stay well. I want you to know that I'll do everything in my power to see to it."

She looked at him. He looked back, pleasantly.

"Of course, Factor," she said.

* * *

Szell caught her in the passage outside.

"Purser." The First Officer fell into step beside her, one hand on the rail. "Drink with me."

"I'm on watch."

"You're the Purser. You don't stand watch, you stand next to it. Come and drink with me. There's real whisky in the officers' mess and nobody's touched it since Callis."

There was. Szell poured two glasses from a square brown bottle and pushed one across the little steel table, and sat back with the other held in both hands, and looked at Annick over the rim of it with her dark considering eyes. The mess was empty at this hour. The ring's gravity was light here, close to the hub, and the whisky moved in the glass in a slow, heavy way, like oil.

"Benn was a good man," said Szell.

"Yes."

"Good men are expensive. Did you know that? Everyone thinks they're cheap. They come with principles; principles look like they're free. But you have to pay for them somewhere. Usually someone else does." She drank. "He'd have cost all those people their wages. Lindqvist knew it. That's why he sat there for five hours letting the old man talk. He was hoping Benn would talk him into something. He didn't."

"Why are you telling me this?"

"Because you're not a good man," said Szell, "and neither am I, and I thought it would be pleasant for once to talk to someone who isn't pretending." She smiled. It was the first time Annick had seen her smile; it changed her face completely, made it younger and almost warm. "I've watched you for three years, Purser. You're the only person on this ship who does exactly what she's supposed to do, every day, and never once tells herself a story about it. I find that restful."

Annick turned her glass on the table. She did not drink. "What do you want, First Officer?"

"Want?" Szell looked at her for a moment. Then she said, as if it were an idle question, the kind you ask in a bar at the end of a long watch, "What would you do, Purser, if you could do anything at all?"

"My job."

"Yes," said Szell softly. "That's what I thought." She finished her whisky and stood. "Don't let it go to waste. It's the last of it. There's none at Shale."

* * *

Dace came for her after the second watch, when the ring had dimmed its lights for the ship's night.

She did not knock. She opened the door of the purser's cabin with a bonded tech's override, which she was not supposed to have, and stood in the doorway in her dun coveralls with her hair tied back with wire, and said, "Come with me. Don't bring anything that talks."

"Dace—"

"Leave your slate. Come on."

Annick left her slate. She went.

They went down the spoke into the spine and aft along the main corridor, past the strongroom door with its red letters, and through the great double hatch into the Hold.

The Hold of the *Merciful Increase* was the largest single space aboard her: a cylinder two hundred metres long and sixty across, running down the centre of the spine, and filled from end to end with cradles. They were racked in tiers on a lattice of black struts that rose from every surface toward the axis, so that wherever you floated in the Hold, cradles surrounded you — above, below, on every side — receding into a blue dimness lit only by the small cold glow of each cradle's status lamp. Three thousand six hundred and twelve of them. Each one was a coffin-shaped shell of white composite with a window of frosted glass over the face, and behind each window, if you went close enough, you could see a face: still, grey, rimed with frost on the lashes and the brows, like a face seen through ice on a pond. The air was cold enough here to make the breath smoke. It smelled of cradle-gel and coolant and, faintly, of something sweeter underneath that Annick had never let herself put a name to.

The bonded called them coffins. The crew called them cradles. The Charter's manifests called them berths, and charged for them by the cubic metre.

Dace led her through the struts hand over hand, deep into the racks, three tiers in and halfway down, where a gap in the lattice made a kind of hollow among the cradles, out of sight of the main hatch. There were perhaps a dozen people waiting there. All bonded, in dun, with the blue lamplight on their faces. Some of them she knew by sight from the Wake rotations: the gang chief with the burned face from the coil, a bonded electrician from the Lantern's maintenance crew, a slight young man with a shaved head and a knife scar on his chin whom Dace drifted over to stand beside, very close. And two she knew by name, because everyone in the Wake knew them by name, and because she had read their entries in the Book the day they came aboard.

*Ruck, Tobiah. V5-0319. Bonded. Quarryman.* He was enormous. Even in zero gravity, where size meant less, he took up space the way a bulkhead does: two metres tall, broad as a door, with a shaved scarred head and hands like shovels and a face that had been broken and set crooked at least twice. He had worked eighteen years in the Charter's granite quarries in the hills above Callis before he signed for Absalom. She had read his debt. It was very large; quarrymen's families borrowed against their wages for generations. And he had a voice — she heard it now for the first time — that did not belong to the rest of him at all: low and soft and unhurried, a voice for talking to animals, or children, or the dying.

"Purser," he said. "Thank you for coming."

*Osei, Marit. V5-2207. Bonded. Clerk.* Except that she had not been a clerk; that was what the manifest said, because the Charter had no category for what she had been, which was an advocate for the dockworkers' guild on Callis for twenty years, until the guild was broken and its officers bankrupted by suits and their debts sold to the Charter. She was fifty-six, small and upright, with grey locs tied back and reading lenses pushed up on her forehead, and she looked at Annick the way Annick looked at a column of figures she suspected had been added wrong.

"You know why you're here," said Marit Osei.

"Shale," said Annick.

"Yes." Marit glanced at the electrician. "Kofi was on the Lantern the night the courier came, re-running conduit behind the command ring. He has good ears. We've known for three days." She folded her arms. "The question is what you're going to do about it."

"I'm not going to do anything about it. I'm the purser."

"That's rather the point."

"Dace," said Annick, without looking at her sister, "you shouldn't have brought me here."

"I know," said Dace. "I did anyway."

Tobiah Ruck drifted a little closer. He moved carefully, as big men learn to, so as not to crowd. "Nobody's asking you to do anything you don't want to, Purser," he said. "Not yet. We just want you to hear us. That's all. Can you do that much?"

She could have said no. She could have turned around and gone back up the struts and out through the hatch and along the corridor to the Factor's cabin, and knocked, and told him that there were fourteen people in the Hold plotting mutiny, and given him their names; and she knew, with perfect clarity, as she floated there in the blue cold among the coffins, that Edwin Quarry would have thanked her and sent proctors and that by the next watch there would be fourteen fewer people in the Wake and her sister would be one of them. She knew that Dace knew it too. That was why Dace had brought her. It was the oldest bargain in the world: *I have put my life in your hands. Now what will you do?*

"I'll listen," said Annick.

They told her. Not everything — she could see them choosing what to say, see Marit's eyes flick to Tobiah's before each answer — but enough. There were three hundred of them in the Wake who would move when Tobiah said move. There were more in the Hold, in the cradles, who could be woken: a fast thaw was dangerous, but it could be done, if you had someone who knew the cradle systems and was willing to kill a few to wake the rest. There were twenty-four proctors and a hundred and eighteen crew, and most of the crew would not fight for the Factor, not for Shale, not if it came to it. And there was the Fork, thirty-five days off, where the ship would come out of fold and drift for its cooling, and where the Factor would come down to the strongroom with his order for the Purser to seal.

"And then?" said Annick.

"And then," said Tobiah, "we take her."

"And then what? You take the Lantern. You lock the Factor in his cabin. And then you have a ship you can't fly." She looked round at them. "Can any of you fly her? Can any of you jump her? The coil needs a pilot who's been trained on it, and a navigator who knows the Lane, and the Lantern's locks won't answer anyone the Book doesn't name as Master. Article Fourteen. You can take the bridge and sit in the captain's chair and she'll drift at the Fork until the air runs out."

"That's why we need you," said Marit.

"To do what?"

"To write one line." Tobiah's soft voice. "That's all, Purser. Not to fight. Not to carry anything. At the moment we take the Lantern, you're in the strongroom with the Book and the key, and you write one line that says who the Master is. And she flies."

"Who?"

He did not answer. Marit did not answer.

"Who do you want written in?" said Annick. "You?"

"We'll have help," said Tobiah after a moment. "When the time comes. Somebody who can fly her." He held up one great hand before she could speak. "I'm not going to tell you more than that. Not tonight. I'm not asking you to trust me tonight, Purser. I'm only asking you to listen."

"And if I won't write it?"

"Then you'll seal the Factor's assignment," said Marit Osei, "and we'll all go to Shale, and you'll keep the Book for the Concern for six to nine years, and enter us one at a time as we die." She unfolded her arms. "You've been keeping a Book of what we owe since the day we came aboard, Purser Vell. Every litre of air. Every meal. Every death. You're very good at it; everyone says so. I've only one question, and you needn't answer it now." She leaned forward slightly, into the blue light. "Have you ever, once, in your whole career, kept a Book of what's owed to *us*?"

Somewhere in the racks behind them, very softly, someone was singing. A man's voice, low, with no tune to speak of — one of the Wake's night watch, drifting among the cradles, checking the lamps, singing to himself the way people sing in the dark to know they're not alone.

*Heave the iron, heave it slow,
the Book is heavy, down we go.
What is written, that is owed,
and the dark's a long cold road —*

"I'll listen," said Annick again. It was all she could find to say. "That's all. I'll listen."

"That's all we asked," said Tobiah Ruck.

* * *

She did not go back to her cabin. She went to the strongroom.

It was the middle of the ship's night, and the spine was empty, and her own breath was the loudest sound in it. She let herself in through the iron door under the red letters and closed it behind her and floated for a while in the dark before she turned on the lamp. Then she took the Book out of its cradle and held it — just held it, against her chest, the way she had held it that morning with the Factor watching — and then she put it back, and opened it, and turned the key.

The page-glass filled with light. She scrolled back through the columns, past Benn's death this morning, past Emeric Saal three days ago, past a hundred and forty other entries of the voyage, births and deaths and transfers and fines, back to the beginning, and found what she was looking for. *Voyage the Fifth. Opened at Callis. A. Benn, Purser. A. Vell, Assistant Purser.* She touched it, and the Book gave her his voice:

*— in the name of the Meridian Charter Company and under the Ordinances, I open this Book, and bind myself to keep it truly —*

It was a much younger voice than she remembered. Not young, but younger; less air in it, less patience. He'd been sixty, opening this voyage. She had stood beside him in this room and heard him say it and thought nothing of it at all.

*— truly, and without fear or favour, so that what is written here be owed, and only what is written here —*

She stopped it.

She sat there for a long time with the Book open in front of her and the stylus in her hand. She did not write anything. There was nothing to write. There was the Fork in thirty-five days, and the Factor's order, and the six to nine years, and the fourteen people in the Hold whose names she knew now and could not unknow, and her sister standing very close to a young man with a knife scar on his chin, and the First Officer's dark eyes over a glass of the last whisky in the ship. There was a concentrator in a towel in Haddad's bag with its reservoir full.

She held her thumb above the bead and did not lower it.

At last she closed the lid. The page-glass went dark. In her head, quite without meaning to, she began to keep her other accounts, the ones she had never written anywhere; and for the first time in eleven years she found she did not know what column to put them in.


# Chapter 5: The Dead Accounts

*The Account · CR 929*

I went into the dead accounts at three in the morning, ten days before the Burning, because three in the morning is when the Index is empty and the cleaners have gone and the only people awake in the spine of the Hulk are the proctors on the vault door, who don't care where an Assessor goes as long as it isn't through them.

I'd never been that far in. Nobody goes that far in. The living accounts are near the hatch, where the light is good and the cabinets are new and the clerks come and go all day with their trays of cards; the Clear accounts are further back, in the older steel; and past them, past a bulkhead where the gallery narrows and the light panels are spaced so far apart that you swim from one pool of light to the next through the dark, are the dead. Three hundred years of them. Every family that ever held a portion and died out, or was extinguished — struck to the last surety, or wiped out in the fevers of the 700s, or simply stopped — closed and cross-referenced and filed away in cabinets so old that the steel has gone brown and the labels are handwritten. Nobody looks at them. There's nothing in them anyone can collect.

D-0447 was in the sixth cabinet, eleventh row, a drawer at knee height that stuck when I pulled it and then came out all at once with a squeal I felt in my teeth. I held my breath and listened. Nothing. The scrubbers hummed. Somewhere far back toward the hatch, a proctor coughed.

The card at the front of the drawer was what it should have been. *D-0447. Portion of MATTOCK, Gallows Reach. Closed CR 744 — extinction of line (fever).* A family of ice-cutters, dead for nearly two hundred years, with a closing balance of nil. There were a few dozen cards behind it — the Mattock portion's history, quarter by quarter, payment by payment, strike by strike, down to the last of them. I thumbed through them, slowly, by the light of my handset. Nothing. Births and deaths and quarters paid. A strike in 731. A Clearance refused in 738.

Then, right at the back of the drawer, where the cards stopped and there was a finger's width of space before the steel, something that wasn't a card. A fold of paper. Thin, almost translucent, the kind Ottilie bought in Saltmarch by the ream for her notes, and on it, in her blue-black ink, in her small precise hand, rows and rows of numbers.

I'd have known it anywhere.

When I was twelve and a new scholarship boy, frightened of everything in the Hulk and sure I'd be sent back down to the ice any day for some failure I didn't understand yet, Ottilie used to set me puzzles. I think she did it to keep me from crying. She'd write a message in numbers and leave it on my bunk, and I'd have to work out what it said, and when I did there'd be a treat waiting somewhere — a sweet, a book, once a little clockwork bird she'd had made in Saltmarch that sang four notes. The key was always the same. It was the Catechism of Mercy. *Because it's the only text*, she told me, *that every person on Tamar knows by heart and nobody ever listens to.* Each number was a word: the line of the Catechism, then the word in the line, then the letter in the word. *Three-one-one* was *W*, from *What*. *Seven-four-two* was *U*, from *our*. It was a child's cipher. It would have taken the Office's code clerks about four minutes.

But the Office's code clerks don't open the dead accounts.

I didn't decode it there. I folded it into my coat and closed the drawer, slowly, so it wouldn't squeal, and swam back down the dark gallery from pool to pool of light, and at the bulkhead where the dead accounts end I found I was holding the paper against my chest through my coat with my hand flat, the way you'd hold a wound.

* * *

It took me most of what was left of the night, in my quarters, with the door locked. Not because it was hard. Because my hands kept stopping.

She hadn't written much. A dozen fragments, some only a few words long, as if she'd been writing them over days, a line at a time, whenever she had a moment alone. Some of them I couldn't make sense of. Some of them I could make sense of and wished I couldn't.

*The Account was not copied. It was composed.*

That was the first. Then:

*Founding page claims true copy of the Book's last state at Landfall, sealed by the Purser. Seal is not a purser's seal.*

And:

*The Account is silent. Every Charter book keeps the purser's voice. Listened to the founding page twice. Nothing.*

And, by itself, underlined twice:

*Ask the rings.*

I sat looking at that one for a long time. *You read it like the rings of a tree.* Every entry grows a layer in the lattice; look at the layer and you can see when it was written. Every Keeper knew it. Every Keeper who'd ever been trained by Ottilie Brask knew it twice over, because she wouldn't let you forget. But you can't read the rings of the master Account without being in the vault with it, and nobody goes into the vault but the Chief Assessor and the Keepers of the Account and, on the first day of every quarter, the Speaker of the Houses, who comes to watch the quarter's strikes and Clearances grown into the lattice and says a prayer over it. And even if you got in, you'd need a reader much finer than anything the Office has — fine enough to count rings three hundred years old, one from the next, the way you'd count the rings on a pine stump with a lens.

The last fragment was the shortest.

*H — if you are reading this, I was right to be frightened. I'm sorry. Burn this.*

I didn't burn it. I sat there with it in my hands until my handset told me it was six o'clock, and then I washed my face and shaved and put on a clean shirt and my grey coat and my brass pin, and went to work.

I remembered, while I was shaving, the one time I'd been in the vault.

I was thirteen. It was the day of my first Burning on the Hulk, and Ottilie, who had no business doing it, had taken me in for a minute while the Keepers were at the ceremony in the Hall and the proctors on the door were drunk on House wine. She'd wanted me to see it. *You should know what you're serving*, she said. I remember the cold, and the dark iron walls, and the smell — old metal, old air, something like cloves. I remember the Account itself: a great cube of crystal the colour of smoke, a metre on a side, lying in a cradle that was much too small for it, a padded iron bracket built for something a tenth its size, so that the Account sat on top of it rather than in it, strapped down with steel bands. *That was the Book's cradle*, Ottilie said. *They put the Account in it after the Burning. For continuity.* She'd said *continuity* in a way I didn't understand then.

I remember a brass plaque on the wall, covered in tiny writing. I remember a little steel safe set into the wall beneath it, a hand's breadth square, with a keyhole and no handle. *The purser's box*, she said. *Nobody's opened it in three hundred years. Nobody has the key. The Houses tried drills on it once and it ate the drills.* And I remember a shelf beside the cradle, with three iron boxes strapped to it in a row, each the size of a big book, each with a lead seal across its hasp and a number stamped on its cover. VOYAGE I. VOYAGE II. VOYAGE IV.

*Where's Three?* I asked her.

*Lost*, she said. *Before Landfall. Nobody knows how.* She shrugged. *Things get lost on a voyage. Come on, before the proctors sober up.*

I hadn't thought of it in twenty-eight years. I thought of it now, with the razor at my throat, and I didn't know why, and it made my hand slip, and I bled into the basin for a while and watched it go round.

* * *

"You look terrible," said Lucan Marsh.

"I didn't sleep."

"No. None of us did." He poured me tea. The Chief Assessor's office is in the old Charter quartermaster's cabin, near the vault, in zero gravity, and he takes his tea from a bulb with a sipping straw like everyone else in the spine; but he serves it from a real pot, a squat brown glazed thing with a magnetic base that he'd had made on Tamar, because he says a man ought to be able to pour something for his guests. In the corner of the office, floating in a glass sphere the size of a head, was his juniper — a bonsai, two hundred years old, which he said had come up from the ice in the time of his great-great-grandmother and which he watered every morning with a mist from a little brass bulb. It had grown in zero gravity all its life, and so it had no idea which way was up, and its branches went everywhere at once, twisted and dense and very beautiful, like something drawn by a child who'd never seen a tree. He told me once it was the only thing in the Hulk he'd save in a fire.

He looked old this morning. Older than I'd ever seen him. He'd known Ottilie longer than I had. They'd come up through the Office together, forty years ago, and there'd been a story, in my time as a junior, that once they'd been more than colleagues. I'd never asked.

"Tell me," he said.

I told him about the ferry passengers. The proctors had traced the two ballast tickets in the night: false names, bought for cash at a Saltmarch kiosk that didn't keep faces. I told him about the paint, Charter script and not ice-script, and the beads on the wrong side of her coat. I told him about her fingers, and her spectacles.

I didn't tell him about the shoe. I don't know why. I'd meant to. I'd come into the office meaning to lay the card on his desk and the paper beside it and say *Look what she left me*, the way I'd have laid my sums in front of him at twelve. I opened my mouth to do it, and then I looked at the juniper going every way at once in its sphere, and I closed my mouth again.

"So you don't think it was the Hold," he said.

"I think someone painted the Hold on it."

"The Hold paints itself on everything." He sighed. "Hollis. I want you to listen to me, because I'm going to say this once and then I'm going to let you get on with it. Ottilie had—" he searched for the word — "enthusiasms, these last months. She'd been working very late. She'd been going into the dead accounts, which she had no reason to do, and into the old Charter manifests, which she had less reason to do. She'd started saying things at Keepers' meetings about the founding of the Account that made the Keepers of the Account uncomfortable. She was sixty-three. Some people become antiquarians as they get older. It's a kind of grief, I think. You start looking back for something you've lost and you think it's in the archives." He looked at me over his tea. "I don't know what she found, if she found anything. I don't want to know. I want whoever killed her struck, and I want it done before the Burning. Don't let her ghosts pull you down with her. Find the killer. Leave the rest alone."

"Yes, sir."

"Good." He put down his bulb. "There's something else. The verifier docks at eleven."

"I thought he was due tomorrow."

"He made good time. The Concordance always makes good time; it's one of their charms." He said it lightly, but there was something under it. "Speaker Constance will meet him at the dock. The Office will provide his liaison for the duration of his visit — escort him, show him whatever he asks to see, answer his questions. I want it to be you."

I stared at him. "Sir, I'm on Ottilie."

"You can be on both. He'll be in the vault with the Keepers most of the time; he won't need you there." He smiled slightly. "And frankly, Hollis, I want someone with the verifier whom I trust to tell me what he looks at. Not what the Speaker's people tell me he looks at. What he actually looks at. Do you understand?"

I understood. I understood that the man who'd lifted me out of the ice at twelve had just asked me, in the kindest possible voice, to spy on the Concordance for him; and I understood, a moment later, with a coldness I can still feel, that it put me beside the one person in the system who'd be allowed into the vault with a reader fine enough to count rings.

"Yes, sir," I said.

* * *

The Concordance survey cruiser *Probity* came in to the station's long dock at eleven exactly, and I'd never seen anything like her.

Our ships are ugly. The Mercy Guard's cutters are squat armoured wedges studded with sensor blisters and gun ports, and the ferries are boxes, and even the Speaker's courier yacht, which is the prettiest thing in the system, is only a sleek grey dart with a gilded stripe down its side. The *Probity* was four hundred metres long and white — white all over, smooth as an egg, without a seam or a port or a weapon showing anywhere on her — and she slid up to the dock on thrusters so fine I couldn't see their flames, and touched, and was still. Through the dock windows, the crowd of House stewards and Office clerks and Mercy Guard officers in dress blue went silent, the way a crowd goes silent when it sees something it can't afford.

Severine Constance was at the front of the welcoming party, of course.

I'd seen the Speaker of the Houses perhaps a dozen times in my life, always at a distance: at the Burning, in the Hall, reading the Mercy; once, in the Office, coming out of the vault after the quarter's prayer with her face very still. She was fifty-four. She was tall and thin and grey-eyed, with silver hair cut close to her skull in the Crown fashion, and she wore the Speaker's robe of plain unbleached linen — the Houses dress plainly; it's part of the Mercy — so well that it looked like the most expensive thing in the room. House Constance was the first of the Seven. Its Speakers had led the Houses for most of three centuries. You didn't say that, in the Crown, any more than you said the names of the Founders. You only knew it, the way you knew where the light was coming from.

The *Probity*'s lock opened. One man came out.

I'd expected — I don't know what I'd expected. An admiral. A delegation. The Concordance of Worlds is two hundred and eleven settled systems and seven hundred billion people and the largest navy in the history of the human species, and it had been six years since its first survey ship came out of the Long Lane after three centuries of silence and told us we weren't alone any more. I'd expected trumpets. What came out of the *Probity* was a lean, middle-aged man in a plain black uniform with a narrow band of silver at the collar, carrying a document case, with close-cut grey hair and a long, patient, rather tired face, and in his left eye socket, where an eye should have been, a lens.

It was the size of an eye and the shape of an eye, and it moved like an eye, but it was faceted crystal, pale gold, and when the dock lights caught it, it threw back tiny sparks like a cut stone. It moved independently of his other eye. As he came down the ramp toward the Speaker, the right eye looked at her and the left looked at the room — swept it, end to end, quickly and thoroughly, like a clerk running a finger down a column — and then, for no more than a second, stopped on me.

"Verifier-Commander Isaak Thorne," he said to the Speaker, and bowed very slightly. "Bureau of Titles, Concordance of Worlds. Thank you for receiving me."

There were speeches. I don't remember them. I stood at the back of the welcoming party with my hands behind my back and watched the crystal eye, and when the speeches were over Marsh caught my sleeve and brought me forward and said, "Commander, may I present Assessor Hollis Fen, of the Office of the Account, who'll be your liaison during your visit," and Thorne looked at me with both eyes at once for the first time.

"Assessor," he said.

"Commander."

"You're the one investigating the murder of the Keeper."

I didn't look at Marsh. I didn't look at the Speaker. "Yes, Commander."

"I was told about it on approach. I'm sorry." He didn't sound sorry. He didn't sound anything. He had a flat, even voice with no accent I could place, as if it had been planed smooth. "I'd like to begin in the vault this afternoon. Is that possible?"

It was possible. Everything was possible for him; the Speaker had made that clear. I walked him from the dock up through the spoke to the spine, while his luggage followed with two silent Concordance ratings in white, and I showed him where the handholds were and how to move in zero gravity, which he already knew, and he asked me questions in his planed-smooth voice. How many portions were held. How many Clear. How many struck in the last year. How long the Index had been kept on film and why. He listened to every answer without comment. At the Office hatch he stopped and looked up at the red letters over the vault door at the end of the passage.

"*What is written is owed*," he said. "Article Four of the Purser's Ordinances. Did you know that?"

"No, Commander."

"Charter law. The Ordinances were the Charter's code for ship's pursers. The Concordance inherited the Charter's legal code when it absorbed the old Charter worlds, and the Bureau inherited the Ordinances along with everything else." He looked at the letters with his crystal eye, and the eye made a tiny sound, a click, like a lens focusing. "Your Account claims title under them. That's why I'm here. Not to judge your society, Assessor; I want to be clear about that, because people always assume. I'm not here to decide whether the Liberty Debt is just. I'm here to decide whether the instrument by which your Houses claim to hold the debts of this system is what it says it is."

"And if it is?"

"Then it is," said Thorne. "Paper is paper."

His crystal eye flicked down. Not at my face — lower. At my chest, where under my shirt, on its long chain, my mother's key hung against my breastbone.

"You're carrying lattice," he said.

I'd forgotten it was there. You do, with things you've worn for twenty-nine years. I put my hand to it, through my shirt, without meaning to.

"A family thing," I said.

"Charter work," said Thorne. "Old. Very old." The eye clicked again. "Is it Office property?"

"It was my mother's."

"Ah," he said, and looked at me for a moment longer with both eyes, and then nodded and went on through the hatch, and never mentioned it again.

* * *

That evening, after I'd left him in the guest quarters the Speaker had given him in the Crown — a suite of rooms with real windows and a garden — I took the ferry down to Saltmarch and went looking for the Hold.

Saltmarch hangs from the underside of Tamar's ice shelf like a wasps' nest from the eaves of a roof. The top of it, near the shelf, is the Clear quarter, where Imke and Wren live: domes and terraces and real glass, lit from above by panels that imitate a sky nobody under the ice has ever seen. Below that are the working districts, the refineries and the belts and the stacks of ballast flats, tier on tier, getting narrower and darker as they go down. And at the bottom, where the city meets the black sea under the ice, is the Sump.

The proctors don't go into the Sump at night. The Office doesn't go into it at all, if it can help it. I'd been there twice in twenty years, both times with an escort of six, and both times I'd come out with fewer people than I went in with. I went in that night alone, in a ballast coat over my grey, with my pin in my pocket and my sidearm under my arm and no idea at all what I was doing except that I'd run out of other things to do.

It took four hours and most of the money in my pockets. A tea-seller who knew a woman who knew a man. A back room behind a fish-gutting shed that smelled so strongly of the sea under the ice that my eyes ran. A hood over my head, and two sets of hands on my arms, and a long walk down a passage that sloped and turned and sloped again, and a door, and a chair, and then the hood came off and I was sitting in a little room carved out of the ice with a single lamp, and across a table from me was Calla Dray.

I knew her face. Everyone in the Office knew her face; it was on every proctor's wall in Saltmarch. Thirty-four, dark, wiry, with cropped black hair and a long white scar from her left ear to the corner of her mouth, and two fingers missing from her left hand — the third and the fourth, gone at the knuckle — where the Deep Ice had taken them. She'd done four years down there, for her father's portion. She'd come back up and organised the Hold in the six years since. The Office had a price on her I'd have needed to be an Assessor for another century to earn.

She looked at me for a long time without speaking. Then she said, "Hollis Fen."

"Yes."

"Second Rank. Lifted out of Gallows Reach at twelve. Marsh's boy." She said it flatly, as if reading off a card. "Three hundred and eight strikes. Am I right? Three hundred and eight."

"Three hundred and nine," I said. "There was one this week."

"Haskell. The refinery boy. Fourteen months." She leaned forward. "You struck my brother, Assessor. Do you remember? You must; you remember them all, they say. Jory Dray. Gallows Reach thirty-one. Seven years ago. Two years for six hundred marks."

"I remember."

"He died in the eighth month. A lance blew out. They brought him up in a sack." She was very calm. "I want you to know that I know that. Before we talk. I want you to know that I know, and that you're sitting in my room, and that nobody in the world knows you're here."

"I know it," I said.

She spat. Not at me, quite. On the floor between us, deliberately, the way you'd mark a line.

"Ottilie Brask," she said.

"Yes."

"You think we killed her."

"No," I said. "I think someone wants me to think you did. I want to know why."

She looked at me with her dark eyes for a long moment. Then she sat back.

"She came to us," Calla said. "Two months ago. Through a laundress in the Crown who carries for us. Said she was a Keeper of the Office and she'd found something and she didn't know who else to trust. We thought it was a trap. Of course we did. We watched her for a month. Then we met her — once, down here, in the dark, the way you've met me." She shrugged. "She said she'd found something that would end the Account. Those were her words. Not *hurt*. Not *embarrass*. *End.* She said she needed a little more time to be sure, and then she'd bring it to us, and we'd know what to do with it. She was supposed to come down on the twenty o'clock ferry, three nights ago. We sent two of ours up on the same boat to bring her back safe."

"The ballast tickets."

"The ballast tickets. They got to the Office and found it full of proctors and the Index sealed. They heard she was dead. They ran. They're in the ice now; you won't find them, so don't try." She looked at me. "We didn't kill her, Assessor. We needed her alive more than anyone in the system."

"Do you know what she found?"

"No." She said it bitterly. "She wouldn't say. She said it was safer if only one person knew until she was certain." Calla's mouth twisted. "She was wrong about that."

I sat there in the cold little room with the lamp and the spit on the floor. After a while I said, "Why are you telling me this?"

"Because you came down here alone," said Calla Dray. "Nobody from the Office has ever come into the Sump alone. Either you're stupid, or you're frightened of something worse than us. I'd like to know which." She stood up. "I'll give you one thing, Assessor. One. Not because I like you; I'd cut your throat for Jory and sleep well. Because she trusted you."

I stared at her. "She told you that?"

"She said if anything happened to her, there was an Assessor called Fen who might come looking. She said he was a good boy who'd done bad things, and that he'd know her handwriting." Calla's face did something complicated. "The laundress. Mags Tiller, in the Crown laundries, lower ring. She carried Ottilie's notes. She might have seen something. Ask her, if you can find her before the proctors do. That's all you get." She nodded to someone behind me. "Put his hood on. Take him up."

* * *

I got back to the Hulk on the last ferry, at two in the morning, with the hood-marks still red on my neck. My quarters are in the lower Crown, where junior House families and senior Office staff live in a kind of polite proximity — two rooms, a window onto the dark, a bed with real gravity under it. I let myself in. I took off my coat. I was halfway across the room toward the bed before I stopped.

Everything was where it should have been. That was what stopped me. My quarters are never exactly where they should be. I'm untidy; Imke used to say I lived like a man waiting to be evicted. There are always papers on the table and a shirt on the chair and my second boots kicked under the bed at an angle. The papers were on the table. The shirt was on the chair. The boots were under the bed. But the papers were squared to the edge of the table, and the shirt was folded over the chair-back, and the boots were side by side, toes out, like a soldier's.

Someone had been through every inch of the place. Someone careful, who'd put everything back exactly where it had been, and hadn't known that *exactly* was the one thing my rooms had never been.

I stood very still in the middle of the floor. Ottilie's paper was inside my shirt, against my skin. It had been there all day. I put my hand flat on it.

My handset chimed. The personal tone.

"Da?" Wren's voice was small and wide awake. "I'm sorry. I know it's late. Mam's asleep. I couldn't—"

"It's all right, love. What is it?"

"There was a man today. From the Office. He came to the flat after school, when Mam was at work." She swallowed. "He was very polite. He had a pin like yours. He said it was routine. He wanted to know about my portion — how much was left, and whether you were still paying it, and when my birthday was." A pause. "He asked when my birthday was twice, Da. He wrote it down. Why would they want to know when my birthday is? They know when my birthday is. It's in the Account."

I looked at my boots, side by side under the bed, toes out.

"What did he look like?" I said.

"Young. Nice-looking. A Crown voice. He said his name but I didn't—" She stopped. "Is it something bad? Did I do something?"

"No," I said. "No, love. You didn't do anything. It's routine." I made my voice level. It's the one thing they train you for. "Go back to bed. I'll come down and see you soon. I promise."

That was two.


# Chapter 6: The Ash Case

*The Salvage · CR 1317*

The Ash Church is cut from the ice itself.

It hangs from the underside of the shelf at the top of Saltmarch, in the oldest part of the city, where the galleries are narrow and the lamps are oil and the walls sweat. You go in through a low door under a carved lintel and down nine steps, and then the ceiling lifts away from you all at once into a great blue vault, thirty metres high, and you are standing inside a bubble of ice so old and so pure that the lamplight goes into the walls and doesn't come out. Everything glows. The pillars glow, and the pews, and the floor under your boots, all the same deep cold impossible blue, like being inside an eye.

Yara hasn't been here since her mother's ash. She remembers the blue. She'd forgotten the cold.

There are perhaps forty people in the pews this morning, more than she expects. Old women, mostly, in diver's wool, and a few men, and a scatter of children kneeling on the ice with their parents' hands on their shoulders. They aren't praying, exactly. They're waiting. At the front of the church, on a low altar of ice, under a column of clear glass, is the Ash Case, and one by one the people in the pews get up and walk to it and stand in front of it for a moment and then dip a finger in a bowl of grey ash at its foot and touch the ash to the inside of their left wrist.

Yara watches them do it. Then, because she's here, she goes up too.

It's smaller than she remembered. A box the size of a big book, iron, black and blistered, sitting on a cushion of grey felt. Whatever was once on its cover — there's the ghost of a pattern, a circle with lines coming out of it — has melted and run and set again in drips, like candle wax. Its hasp is fused into a lump. There's a crack along one side where the iron split in the heat, and through the crack, if you lean close to the glass, you can see darkness and a glint of something underneath, like burnt sugar. That's all. A burnt box. People have walked across the ice for three hundred years to look at it.

*They burned the Book*, says the brass plate on the column. *And the ash was our freedom.*

"You're Hana Tull's girl."

Yara turns. There's an old woman at her elbow, in the grey robe of the Ash with a diver's harness belt cinched over it, of all things — a short woman, broad and bent, with white hair cropped close, one eye milky with cataract and the other sharp and pale as a gull's. Her hands are huge, knotted, scarred across the knuckles in the way only shell gauntlets scar you. Yara knows her by sight. Everyone in the Gutter knows her by sight.

"Mother Quell," she says.

"I gave your mother her ash." Quell looks at her with the good eye. "You were sixteen. You didn't cry. Your brother cried enough for both of you." She nods at the bowl. "Are you going to take it?"

"I don't—"

"Nobody does, these days, until they need to." The old woman dips her own thumb in the ash and, before Yara can move, takes Yara's left wrist in one enormous hand and turns it over and presses the thumb to the inside of it, where the skin is thin and the blue veins show. It's cold. It leaves a grey smudge. "There," says Quell. "Liberty's mark. Now you've paid. What do you want?"

* * *

Quell's room is behind the altar, down a short passage cut through the ice — a cell three metres square with a cot and a stove and a table covered in papers, and on the wall, pinned up with ice-nails, a drawing of the Hulk.

It's an old drawing. Hand-done in pencil on brown paper, gone soft at the folds, with notes in a neat cramped hand all over it — depths, temperatures, *ICE HERE*, *CLEAR TO HERE 1281*, *DON'T*. It shows the spine in section, deck by deck, from the Lantern to the stern, the way Sabeth Orrin's film plan showed it, but in a great deal more detail. Yara stands looking at it while Quell makes tea on the stove.

"You did that," she says.

"Forty years ago. When I was a diver and the Hulk was a block of ice and we went in with lances and came out with chilblains." Quell pours. "Sit down. You're making the room nervous."

Yara sits. The tea is bitter and salt, the old Gutter way. She holds the cup and tries to think how to begin.

"You want Fen," says Quell.

Yara nearly spills it.

"Oh, don't look at me like that. Everyone in the Gutter knows a Vey woman with good boots came to your can three nights ago, and everyone in the Gutter knows what Vey women with good boots want with divers this month. And you come in here on the first morning after, smelling of the Hulk, and look at the Ash Case like you've never seen it, and then look at my wall." The old woman sits down across from her. "So. Fen."

"I want the stories," says Yara. "That's all. What people say."

"What people say." Quell sips her tea. "All right. I'll tell you what the Church says, and what the songs say, and what the divers say, and you can work out for yourself which of them is lying. Most likely all three."

She tells it the way Yara has heard it a hundred times, at the noodle cart and the bars and her mother's knee, but she tells it better — slowly, in a low, level, practised voice, the voice of someone who has told it from an altar for thirty years.

"In the Crossing," says Quell, "our people were carried in the dark. They lay in the cold like the dead, and they woke to work, and they owed for every breath they took. The Purser kept the Book of what they owed, and she would have sold them. That's the first thing. The Purser who would have sold us." She sets down her cup. "But at Landfall, before all the people, the Book was burned. Thrown in the fire, and the debts went up in the smoke, and what was left was ash. And the ash was our freedom. That's what the Church says. That's what the Case is."

"And the Houses?"

"The Houses said the fire was theirs." Quell's mouth thins. "They said they'd lit it, and we owed them for it. Liberty, they called it. The Liberty Debt. Every child born owing for the freedom of the ones before. They wrote it in a new book of their own, the Account, and kept it in the Hulk, and lived up there in their ring with their gardens while we cut the ice to pay them." She shrugs. "For three hundred years. Then came the Night of the Hulk, and then the Dimming, and then they were gone, and nobody cared about the Account any more, because there was nobody left to collect it. Until this week."

"And Fen."

"And Fen." Quell looks at her for a moment with the good eye. Then she sings — not well; her voice is old and cracked, but she carries the tune — the verse every child in the Gutter knows, the one they sing skipping rope on the concourse:

*Fen was an Assessor, grey was his coat,
he counted the Hold and he cut every throat;
he went out on the tide when the Concordance came
with the Hold's blood on his hands and the Houses' gold to his name.*

"That's the song," says Quell. "Fen the Turncoat. Assessor of the Office of the Account, on the Night of the Hulk. Sold the Hold to the Houses, or the Houses to the Concordance, or both to each other, depending which verse you sing. Ran off with the Concordance ships and lived like a lord on some world with three suns." She picks up her cup again. "Songs lie in rhyme, girl. It's easier to remember that way."

"What do the divers say?"

"The divers say," says Quell, "that Fen's still in there. Counting." She smiles slightly. "You've heard it. Every time the Hulk groans. I said it myself, forty years ago, every dive. And there's another one, older. The Purser's ghost. A woman in a grey coat, with a lamp, going through the spine. Looking for her Book. I never saw her. I knew a man who said he did. He was drunk at the time and he drowned the next spring." She drinks. "And there's Fen's cache, which you know about, or you wouldn't be here."

"Did anyone ever look for it?"

"Everyone looked for it. For two hundred years every diver who went into the Hulk was looking for Fen's cache, or the Houses' gold, or the Speaker's jewels, or whatever they'd heard about in a bar that week." Quell gets up, stiffly, and goes to the drawing on the wall and puts one huge knotted finger on it, near the middle of the spine. "When I dived, the Office was all ice. Solid. We lanced our way into the upper decks — here, and here — and found nothing but film and paper and the Assessors' lockers. And this." Her finger moves down to a square shape drawn in heavy pencil, deep in the spine, right beside a long chamber labelled *OLD HOLD*. "The Office vault. The strongroom, the old Charter people called it. Iron door, a metre thick. We cut at it for two windows, five of us, with lances, and got about a thumb's depth in. Then the ice shifted and took one of us, and we gave up." She looks at Yara over her shoulder. "Nobody's been into the vault since the Night of the Hulk. Not in the ice, and not since the thaw. If there's a cache, that's where I'd look."

Yara stands up and goes and looks at the drawing close. Under the square, in Quell's cramped hand, someone forty years ago has written *VAULT — DOOR STILL SOUND — DON'T BOTHER*. And beside it, between the vault and the long shape of the Old Hold, there's another, smaller shape, round, with a line through it. *BALLAST 4*, it says. And under that, in a different pencil, darker, as if it were added later: *WIDOW*.

"The Widow was sealed when you dived," she says.

"The Widow's always been sealed. Office sealed her, long before the Night. Some sickness in the water, they said, back in the Houses' time." Quell goes back to her cot and sits. "Leave the Widow alone. Gorse was right about that, for once in his greasy life. She killed two good divers last spring and she'll kill more."

On the way out, Yara stops in the passage behind the altar, where a narrow stair cut into the ice goes up toward the shelf. There's a hum coming down it, faint and steady, and a smell of hot metal.

"The bell tower," says Quell behind her. "Do you want to see?"

The tower is a shaft bored up through the ice shelf to the surface, sixty metres, with a spiral stair around its wall and, at the top, under a dome of thick glass, a machine. It's old, and patched, and bigger than Yara expected — a squat steel cabinet the size of a ferry seat, studded with dials and switches, cabled up through the dome to an array of slender rods and dishes standing on the ice outside, black against the stars and the great rust face of Absalom. A clockwork thing on top of the cabinet ticks toward the hour.

"Charter-band array," says Quell. "Came out of the Hulk two hundred years ago, in the first years of the Dimming. It was the old Office's — they used it to talk to their seal-readers, so the story goes, and to the Concordance when there was a Concordance. Some priest with more sense than faith dragged it out and brought it down here and hooked a chime to it." She pats the cabinet like a horse. "Every radio in the system hears it. Seven notes, every hour, for two hundred years. It's the only thing on Tamar still talking on the old band."

"Does it only send?"

Quell looks at her oddly. "It sends what you give it. It hears what's sent to it, if anything ever were." She shrugs. "Nothing ever is."

The clockwork clicks. Below them, through the cabinet, something heavy engages; and from the array on the ice the chime goes out — seven falling notes, on the old band, to every receiver in the system. Yara feels it in her teeth. And at her hip, on her harness, where she's brought it without quite deciding to, the brass seal-reader from the drowned annex gives a single soft click.

Quell's good eye goes to it at once.

"Where did you get that?" she says.

* * *

Mitya is waiting for her on the ferry dock when she comes back up to Ringfall.

He's leaning against a bollard with his hands in the pockets of his diver's jacket, looking at nothing, and he doesn't straighten up when he sees her, just watches her come along the dock with the expression he always has when he's about to ask for something: half rueful, half amused, as though the two of them were sharing a joke about him.

"You went to Quell," he says.

"Who told you?"

"The whole Gutter told me. You were seen. Hana Tull's girl, taking ash." He takes her left wrist and turns it over and looks at the grey smudge on it, and something in his face goes still for a moment. Then he lets go. "You're doing Fen's cache."

"I'm doing copper."

"You're doing Fen's cache for the Vey woman in the good boots. Fifty thousand marks, Gorse's boys are saying. Gorse thinks it's copper. Gorse is an idiot." He looks at her. "You can't do the spine alone, Yara. Nobody can. You need someone on your line."

"I'll find someone."

"You've found someone." He spreads his hands. "Me. I'm someone. I'm the best line you've ever had and you know it."

She does know it. That's the trouble. Dmitri Saar has dived with her for six years, and for three of those years they slept in the same bunk, and she's never in all that time seen him lose his head in the water. Out of it, he loses it every week. He's Saltmarch Clear-quarter stock, fallen; his grandmother had a flat with a window and his mother had a flat without one and he has a can in the Gutter and a book with Gorse that's longer than his arm. He gambles. He doesn't drink, which is somehow worse, because it means he's sober when he does it. He's lost their dive money twice, once on a fight and once on a ferry race, and the second time it was money she'd put aside for Pim's clinic, and she'd moved her things out of his can that night and hasn't been back.

"A third," she says.

"A third's fine."

"I mean it. A third, and you don't gamble any of it until Pim's on the ferry to Vey."

"A third, and I don't gamble any of it until Pim's on the ferry to Vey." He holds up one hand, palm out, the way children swear on the concourse. "On your mother's ash."

"Don't."

"On mine, then." He grins. "When do we go?"

* * *

They go in at the front of the next window, when the Hulk is still glowing.

Six passes now. Yara can see it from the sled. The last perigee has taken more of the Crown — a whole section of the old ring hangs loose by a few twisted spars, swinging slowly as the Hulk tumbles, and the leading face is darker and glassier than before, and the plumes of vapour coming off her trailing side are thicker and whiter and don't stop. The vibration coming up through the sled clamps is a constant shudder now, not a breathing. It's like holding on to a dying animal.

"She's loud," says Mitya.

"Shut up."

They go in through the Mouth and down the ring to the nearest spoke and through it, hand over hand, into the spine. Sabeth's film plan is clipped to Yara's forearm; Quell's drawing is in her head. The spine route is longer than the ring, but it's open, mostly — the slosh hasn't reached here — and the water in the great square corridors is clear and still and so cold she can feel it through the shell like a hand on her back. Their lamps go a long way. The hazard stripes on the walls flicker past, red and yellow, red and yellow, faded almost to grey. HOLD ACCESS. PURSER'S DECK. The stencils are three hundred years older than the House names in the ring, and they look newer: plain Charter letters that nobody bothered to carve.

The Old Hold is a chamber so big their lamps can't find its far end.

Yara has heard about it all her life, and she still isn't ready. It runs down the middle of the spine for two hundred metres, sixty across, and it is full of water and full of cradles. They rise from every surface on a lattice of black struts — tiers and tiers of them, above, below, on every side, receding into a darkness the lamps can't reach — and every one is a white shell the shape of a coffin, with an empty window where a face once was, and its lid standing open. Three thousand of them. Four. The water moves among them very slowly, and the open lids move with it, all together, a few degrees one way and a few degrees back, like the gills of something breathing. In the Houses' time the Old Hold was a memorial, Pim told her once — a place you came on Burning Day to stand among the empty cradles and remember that your ancestors had been carried here in them like freight. There are names scratched on some of the shells. Hundreds of names, in every kind of hand, some of them so old they've worn nearly smooth. People came here for three hundred years and wrote their names on the cradles of the dead.

"Don't," says Mitya softly, over the band, when she slows.

"I'm not."

"You are. I can see you. Come on."

She comes on. They go through the Old Hold on a line strung between the struts, hand over hand, in the slow moving dark, with the open lids nodding all round them. At the far end there's a hatch, and beyond the hatch the passage turns, and beyond the turning there's the Office.

The Index is what makes her stop again.

It's a long gallery lined on both sides and down the middle with steel cabinets, floor to ceiling, rows and rows of them with narrow aisles between, and every cabinet has burst. Every drawer is open or gone. And the water in the gallery is full of cards. Millions of them. Little rectangles of film, each the size of a palm, drifting in the cold clear water in a slow endless snowfall that the lamps light up white, that turns and glitters and parts around her helmet as she swims into it and closes again behind. It's like swimming into a cloud. It's the most beautiful thing she's ever seen, and she's seen the Hulk at perigee from the sled, burning.

She catches one. She can't help it. It's soft with three hundred years of wet and the print is smeared, but she can read some of it in her lamp: a name, HASKELL, and a place, *Gallows Reach 40*, and further down a line that says *struck* and a figure and a date that might be 929 before the film tears in her gauntlet and comes apart into grey threads.

"Yara."

"I know."

"What's it say?"

"Nothing." She lets the pieces go. They drift away into the snow. "Someone owed something."

The vault is at the end of the Office, past the Index, past a warren of smaller rooms with their doors standing open on drowned desks; and they don't reach it, because between the Office and the vault the passage is full of ice.

Not all of it. The perigees have melted the ice from the outside in, so that the passage is a tunnel of water ending, twenty metres along, in a wall — a great pale grey face of old ice, cloudy and fissured, filling the passage from wall to wall. There are things frozen in it. Papers. A chair. A grey shape that might be a coat or might be something wearing a coat. Yara puts her gauntlet flat against it and feels, through the shell, the cold of it, and under the cold a faint, deep, continuous creaking, like a ship at anchor. The ice is moving. It's melting, slowly, from the face back, and as it melts it's shifting, settling, finding new ways to lie.

"Two passes," says Mitya, behind her. "Three, maybe. Then it's clear."

"We haven't got three."

"We've got six."

"We've got six before she comes apart. That's not the same thing." She takes her hand off the ice. "We can cut it."

"Through that?" He laughs, high. "There's a hundred cubic metres of ice there, Yara. We'll be cutting till she burns."

They're arguing about it — quietly, the way they argue in the water, in short flat sentences with long gaps — when the Hulk moves.

It's not a slosh. It's worse. It comes through the walls, not the water: a great iron groan that goes on and on and rises in pitch until it's almost a shriek, and the whole passage shudders, and a row of cabinets in the Index gallery behind them tears loose from the floor with a sound like a gunshot and comes tumbling down the gallery toward them through the snow of cards in a slow, enormous, unstoppable fall.

Yara sees it coming and can't move. That's what she'll remember afterward. Not the fear — she's had the fear before, plenty of times, the Hulk is full of it — but the strange slow stillness of watching a wall of steel come at her through the water, end over end, scattering cards, and knowing exactly how long she has and being unable to use any of it.

Then a hand closes on her harness from behind and yanks, hard, and she's pulled sideways and down into the doorway of one of the little drowned offices, and the cabinets go past a metre from her faceplate and hit the ice wall at the end of the passage with a sound she feels in her spine.

Silence. The snow of cards swirls and settles. Somewhere, far off, the Hulk groans once more and is quiet.

Mitya is wedged in the doorway beside her with one arm still locked through her harness. Through his faceplate, in the lamp light, his face is grey.

"You froze," he says.

"I know."

"You never freeze."

"I know." She can hear her own breath, loud in the helmet. "Thank you."

"A third," says Mitya, and lets go of her harness, and laughs — high and shaky, the way he laughed after the slosh — and then stops laughing, all at once, and looks past her, up the passage toward the Old Hold.

There's a light.

Not theirs. A long way back, at the far end of the Index gallery, where the passage turns toward the Hold: a glow in the water, yellowish, moving. Then a second. Lamps. Two divers, or three, coming slowly along the gallery through the snow of cards, the way Yara and Mitya came.

"Who's on the Hulk?" says Mitya very quietly. "Nobody dives the front of the window. Nobody but us."

Yara kills her lamp. After a moment, he kills his. They hang in the dark of the drowned office, side by side, and watch the yellow lights come on down the gallery toward the ice wall, swinging, searching, until they're close enough that she can see the shells behind them, and the colour of the shells.

Yellow. Gorse's yellow. Every crew on Gorse's permit dives in yellow but theirs, because Yara's mother refused to repaint her shell and Yara has never repainted it either.

"He knows," says Mitya.

"Somebody told him."

"Not me."

She doesn't answer. In the dark beside her she can feel him looking at her, and she doesn't answer, and after a while the yellow lights reach the ice wall and stop there, and swing back and forth across its face, and across the tangle of steel cabinets now lying against it, and then turn, slowly, and go back the way they came.

* * *

When they come up into the Gutter, eleven hours later, with no copper and their air nearly gone, there are bailiffs on the concourse.

Yara hasn't seen one before. They're tall, all of them, or seem tall: men and women in long black coats of some material that shines faintly under the salvaged bulbs, with the key-and-sword crest of the *Lawful Remedy* on their sleeves and narrow black helmets with no visors, only a band of dark glass where the eyes should be. There are twelve of them. They're standing in a line in front of the concourse screen, very still, while two more go along the walls with sheets of film and a heat-gun and fix the sheets to the plating over a hundred years of divers' marks.

Yara stops in front of one. It's printed in the old Charter letters, very clear.

*WRIT OF EXECUTION. HIGH CHANCERY OF VEY. In the matter of the Account of Absalom, the Paschal Recovery Company, assignee. ALL PERSONS of the lines of portion-holding families recorded in the said Account are LIABLE for the obligations of those lines as recorded, with interest accrued. Registration is MANDATORY. Persons failing to register will be held in DEFAULT and may be taken in satisfaction.*
