Under it, in smaller print, there's a rate of interest, and a date of last entry, and a formula. Yara reads the formula twice. She's not good with figures, not the way Pim is. But she understands that it's a figure that has been doubling for three hundred and eighty-eight years.

There's a line along the concourse wall, two hundred people long. Divers, divers' families, welders, the noodle-cart man. At the head of it, under a canopy of black film, three bailiffs sit at a table with a machine on it, and one at a time the people in the line hold out their wrists, and the machine pricks them, and a bailiff reads something off a screen and says something, and the person moves away. Mostly they move away. Yara watches a man she knows — Tobin Rusk, an old welder from the can three down from hers — hold out his wrist and wait and listen and then stand there, not moving away, until two of the bailiffs from the line come and take him by the elbows, quite gently, and walk him off down the concourse toward the dock where the *Lawful Remedy*'s shuttle is berthed.

Tobin's wife is standing by the wall. She doesn't scream. Yara has heard people scream in the Gutter, when the Hulk takes someone. This is worse. Tobin's wife stands by the wall with her hands pressed flat against the plating, against the divers' marks and the new white sheet of the writ, and makes a sound like the Hulk groaning, low and long and steady, and doesn't stop.

"Default of registration," says someone in the crowd. "He didn't register yesterday. He was on a shift."

"He's registering now."

"Too late. Read the writ."

Mitya's hand is on her arm. "Come on," he says quietly. "Home. Pim."

She goes. She goes home along the concourse past the line and the bailiffs and the writs, and she doesn't look at the shuttle dock, and she doesn't look back at Tobin's wife; and all the way home she can feel, under her skin, on the inside of her left wrist where the old woman pressed her thumb, the grey cold smudge of the ash.

Six passes.


# Chapter 7: Air Debt

*The Crossing · CR 611*

Breathing was billable.

It had been billable all her life, so Annick had stopped noticing it, the way you stop noticing the hum of a ship. In the stacks of Callis there had been meters on the walls of every corridor, little brass dials with red needles that crept up through the day as the air went bad, and the Company billed each stack for its share of the scrubbing; and when she was very small her mother used to make a game of it, on the long nights when the needles were in the red, of seeing who could breathe the slowest. Four in. Hold. Four out. Annick had won every time. She had not understood until she was nine that it was not a game.

On the *Merciful Increase*, the meters were on the bonded. Each one wore a band on the left wrist, just above the number, with a sensor that counted every litre of air its wearer drew and sent the count to the Book, where it was multiplied by the voyage rate and added to that person's debt at the end of every shift. A big man working hard breathed perhaps four thousand litres a shift. A small woman sitting still breathed perhaps fifteen hundred. The difference, over a three-month Wake, was about two hundred marks. Over a lifetime of Wakes it was a great deal more. The bonded knew the figures better than Annick did.

So they rationed. Everybody did. You learned it in your first Wake or someone taught you: breathe shallow, move slowly, sleep as much as you can, because a sleeping body draws less. The coil gang breathed through their teeth. The cradle techs in the Hold, where the cold made you want to gasp, wrapped their faces in scarves and breathed through the wool. And every rotation, sooner or later, someone took it too far.

It was Ama Brook this time. A wiry, middle-aged woman from the galley crew, with grey in her braids, who collapsed at the slab vats at the end of the second watch on the fourteenth day after the courier and lay on the deck with her lips blue and her eyes rolled back, while the rest of the galley stood round her and nobody touched her, in case it counted against them somehow.

Annick was in the mess when it happened, eating, because she had found that if she ate in the mess the bonded talked in front of her, a little, and she was learning things. She got to Ama first. She did not know why. She knelt on the deck in the ring's light gravity and turned the woman on her side and held her jaw open, the way Haddad had shown the crew in a drill, and looked at the meter on her wrist. It read eleven hundred and four litres for the shift. Ama Brook had been at the vats for nine hours, stirring vats of protein paste with a paddle as long as she was, and she had breathed eleven hundred litres. She had been holding her breath.

"Get the doctor," said Annick, and nobody moved, and she looked up at the circle of faces round her and said, in the voice she used at the moment of the bill, "*Now*," and two of them went.

Haddad came. Ama Brook woke up in the infirmary an hour later with an oxygen mask on her face — free oxygen, medical, which by the Ordinances could not be billed — and looked at it with horror and tried to pull it off, and Haddad held her hands down gently and said something to her in a low voice until she stopped.

"They all do it," he said to Annick afterward, in the passage. "Every rotation. I see three or four a Wake. Mild hypoxia, headaches, fainting. Two years ago a man on the coil gang died of it. Not directly. He was lightheaded and he missed a handhold." He rubbed his face. "There's a line in the clerks' manual about it, did you know? *The voyage rate for air is fixed to encourage economy of respiration among the bonded.* Encourage economy. I've always admired that."

"I know the line."

"Of course you do." He looked at her with his tired, careful face. "You've been in the Hold a great deal lately, Purser."

She held his gaze. "I'm reconciling the cradle manifests."

"Yes," said Haddad. "That's what you told Sergeant Kolar too. She asked me about it." He hesitated. "I'm not asking you anything. I'd like you to know I'm not. But if I were reconciling cradle manifests at two in the morning, I'd want to know that the sergeant had noticed."

* * *

She had been giving them the rosters.

It was the first thing they had asked for, and it had been easy, which frightened her more than anything else about it. The purser's office kept the payroll. The payroll required the duty rosters. The duty rosters showed where every member of the crew was at every hour of every watch, including the twenty-four proctors of the Factor's guard, who were paid by the Charter like everyone else and whose posts and shifts and leave were recorded in the Book's annex alongside the bonded's air. Annick had only to open the annex and read. She did not copy anything. She did not write anything down. She read it, as she read everything, quickly, with her mouth shut, and then she went down to the Hold in the middle of the ship's night and told Dace, and Dace told whoever Dace told.

The second thing they had asked for was harder.

"The Lantern's locks," said Marit Osei. "How they work. Exactly."

They were in the hollow among the cradles, three tiers in, with the blue lamplight on their faces and the frost on the windows all round them. It was the fourth time Annick had come. It had begun to feel like a habit, which frightened her too.

"You know how they work. Article Fourteen."

"I know what the Ordinance says. I want to know what happens. When you seal a new Master. What do the locks do, and how long do they take, and what happens to the old Master's authority while they do it?"

So she told them. She told them that when the Purser sealed a change of Master in the Book, the Book sent the entry to the Lantern along a hard line that ran through the spine, and the Lantern's locks — the command ring, the coil controls, the navigational array, the weapons lockers on the bridge deck — dropped the old Master's codes and took the new one's, and that the whole process took about forty seconds, during which no one at all was Master and the ship would answer to nobody. She told them that the line could not be cut without the Lantern knowing. She told them that a Master's authority could be sealed only to a named person who was physically aboard, and that the Book would check the name against the crew manifest and refuse anyone not on it.

"Anyone not on the *crew* manifest," said Marit slowly.

"Yes."

"Not the bonded manifest."

"No. A Master has to be crew." Annick looked at Tobiah, who was floating a little way off with one great hand resting on the window of a cradle, as he often did, as if to keep its sleeper company. "That's why you need a friend on the bridge."

Tobiah didn't look round. "Yes," he said.

"Who is it?"

"Purser," said Marit gently, "you know we're not going to tell you that."

"Then how am I supposed to seal them?"

"When the time comes," said Tobiah, "you'll know." He turned then, and looked at her across the hollow with his broken, crooked face. "I'm sorry. I know it's hard. I know we're asking you to do a thing in the dark. But you understand why. If you know the name and someone asks you, it's in you to tell. If you don't know it, you can't."

She understood. She did not say so.

* * *

Proctor-Sergeant Vashti Kolar stopped her in the spine two nights later, outside the strongroom.

Annick had come down from the ring the long way, by the aft spoke, because she had begun to vary her routes; and she turned the corner by the Hold hatch and found the sergeant waiting there in the dim corridor in her grey armour, with her helmet under her arm and one boot hooked casually through a handhold, as if she had been there for some time. She was a tall, rawboned woman of about forty, with pale cropped hair and a face like a fist. She had commanded the Factor's proctors for nine years. Annick had entered the fines she levied. They were large.

"Purser," said Kolar.

"Sergeant."

"Late to be up."

"I'm going to the strongroom."

"I can see that." Kolar looked at the strongroom door, at the red letters over it, and then at the great double hatch of the Hold beside it. "You've been in the Hold a great deal."

"I'm reconciling the cradle manifests."

"At night."

"The cradle techs are on day shift. I can't hear myself think."

"No," said Kolar. "I expect not." She unhooked her boot and drifted a little closer, not quite threatening, the way a dog drifts closer. "You're ballast-born, aren't you, Purser? Callis stacks. I looked it up. Mother died in the Company's mines at Gethen. Sister in the Wake." She smiled, without anything behind it. "Must be strange. Keeping the Book on your own people."

"They're not my people. I'm crew."

"Yes," said Kolar. "That's what I'd say." She put her helmet on, and her voice came out of the speaker grille, tinny and flat. "Goodnight, Purser. Mind how you go in the Hold. It's very cold in there. People get lost."

* * *

The next time she went down, Tobiah was alone.

She had not expected that. She came in through the great hatch and up through the struts, three tiers in, and found the hollow among the cradles empty except for him, floating with his back to her and his hand on a window, and for a moment she thought she would turn round and leave. Then he said, without looking round, "Dace is on the coil. Marit's sleeping. I told them I'd wait for you."

"Why?"

"Because I wanted to talk to you without anyone listening." He turned. "Is that all right?"

She did not know if it was. She came the rest of the way into the hollow and caught a strut and held it.

He did not talk about the rising. She had expected him to; she had been ready for it, with every Ordinance she could think of. Instead he asked her about the Book — not what it said, but what it was. How the lattice grew. How long it lasted. Whether she had ever seen one destroyed, and what happened to a Book at the end of a voyage. She told him about the dead shelf, the four old Books strapped side by side with their lead seals, and about the law that said a Book could never be destroyed.

"Never?"

"Never. It can be closed. It can't be burned."

"What if a ship's taken? Pirates?"

"Then it goes in the ballast." She surprised herself by smiling. "Article Nine. There's a chute in the strongroom floor, a lead sleeve, a lever. If a ship's taken by anyone not lawfully in command, the purser drops the Book into Tank Four, and it sinks, and whoever took the ship can't write themselves into it."

He was staring at her. Then, slowly, he began to laugh.

It was a deep, soft laugh, like his voice, and it went on for some time, and he put his hand over his face while he did it. "*Down the sea*," he said.

"What?"

"The song. The coil song. *Heave and heave and we'll be free, when the Book goes down the sea.* You've heard it."

"I've heard it."

"I always thought it was just a rhyme. Something to sing. My father sang it in the quarry, and his father, and nobody I ever met knew what it meant." He took his hand away. His eyes were wet, from laughing or from something else. "It's spacers' talk, isn't it? From the old ships. *The Book's gone down the sea.* The ship's taken. The ship's ours."

"Yes," said Annick. "It's from the Ordinances. Nobody's used a drop in a hundred years. But the old crews used to say it. When a ship was lost, or mutinied. *Her Book's gone down the sea.*" She hesitated. "Benn told me. He said he'd heard old spacers say it on Callis when he was a boy."

"So the ship's ours when the Book's in the ballast."

"That's what it means."

He looked at her for a long time. Then he sang it — properly, in a low true voice that filled the hollow and went out among the cradles and the frost, all of it, the whole verse, the way the coil gang sang it scraping salt:

*Heave the iron, heave it slow,
the Book is heavy, down we go.
What is written, that is owed,
and the dark's a long cold road.
Heave and heave and we'll be free
when the Book goes down the sea.*

When he finished, the Hold was very quiet. Somewhere far off in the racks a cradle pump cycled, a soft sigh.

"My father died under a slab," said Tobiah. "In the quarry above Gethen town. I was nine. A block of granite as big as this hollow came off the face in the rain and he was under it, him and four others, and they didn't get them out for a week because the Company wouldn't stop the cutting. When they did, the foreman came to our house with a man from the Company's office, a clerk, with a slate and a little stylus, and the clerk read out what my father owed, and said it was ours now." He looked at her. "He had a voice exactly like yours. Very level. I hated him for twenty years. Then I met him again, in a bar in Callis, when I was thirty, and he was old and drunk and he didn't remember me, and I couldn't hate him any more. He was only the man who said the number."

Annick said nothing. There was nothing she could say.

"I don't hate you, Purser," said Tobiah. "I want you to know that. Whatever happens. I know what you are. I know what it costs."

"You don't know what it costs."

"No," he agreed. "I suppose I don't." He put his hand back on the cradle window beside him. "This is Lise Saal. Emeric's wife. Did you know? I come and sit with her sometimes. And the boys are just there, the next two." He nodded at the cradles above. "I thought someone should. Since nobody's told her."

She looked at the frosted window under his hand. Behind it, through the rime, was a woman's face — thin, young, with a straight nose and a mouth that turned down a little at the corners, and frost on her lashes. Annick had entered her name. She had never looked at her.

"What happens at Absalom?" she said.

He didn't answer at once.

"Quarry's right," she said. "That's what I can't get past. He's a liar and a thief and I think he'd sell his own mother at a discount, but he's right. There's nothing at Absalom. There's no colony office. There's no relief ship. There's no market for anything. If you take this ship to Absalom, you'll land four thousand people on a moon made of ice with what's in the holds and nothing else, and the nearest help is eleven jumps back the way we came, at a port that's on fire." She heard her voice beginning to rise, and stopped it. "How will you feed them? Who'll decide who works and who eats? Who'll keep the air running? What will you do when the first people start to die?"

"We'll work it out."

"That's not an answer."

"No," said Tobiah Ruck. "It's the only one anyone's ever had." He looked at her across the hollow, steadily. "You want a Book for it, don't you. A plan. Figures. Who owes what to whom, and when it's paid. I understand. That's how you're made. But there isn't one, Annick. There's just people, and the ice, and whatever we do next. That's what free means. It means nobody's written it yet."

It was the first time he had used her name. She noticed. She thought he noticed that she noticed.

"I have to go," she said.

"I know."

She went down through the struts toward the hatch. At the bottom she looked back, once. He was still floating in the hollow with his hand on Lise Saal's window, a great dark shape among the cradles in the blue light, and he was singing again, very softly, under his breath, to the sleeper behind the frost.

* * *

Haddad found her in the strongroom the next morning.

He came in without knocking, which he never did, and shut the iron door behind him, and stood with his back against it for a moment as though he expected someone to try it. He had the concentrator with him, still wrapped in its towel.

"I looked at it," he said.

She set down her stylus.

"There's a valve on the output side," said Haddad. "Between the reservoir and the line. It's a safety valve; it closes if the line's pulled out, so you don't waste oxygen into the room. It has a manual override, a little lever under the casing. You'd need a tool to reach it." He unwrapped the towel. The concentrator lay in his hands, small and grey and ordinary, with its green light dark now. "The override's been moved. Recently. There are scratches on the casing where someone got a blade under it. If you closed that valve while a man was asleep, the concentrator would go on running — the light would stay green, it would go on hissing, it would sound exactly the same — but nothing would come down the line. He'd be breathing ship's air through a tube. With his lungs, in his sleep, he'd be dead in an hour. And the reservoir would stay full." He looked up at her. "And then if you came back later and opened the valve again, it would go on hissing pure oxygen down the line into the nose of a dead man until somebody found him."

The strongroom was very quiet.

"Is that proof?" said Annick.

"No." Haddad wrapped the concentrator again, carefully, as if it were something alive. "Valves stick. Casings get scratched. He might have knocked it himself, getting into his bunk. A tribunal would laugh at me." He held the bundle against his chest. "There's a log on these. A service log — every time the casing's opened, it records the time and the code of whoever opened it. It's for the maintenance crews. I haven't been able to read it. It needs an engineer's reader, and I can't ask an engineer for one without telling them why."

"Can I read it?"

"Can the Book?"

She thought about it. "Not directly. But if you bring it to me with the right cable, I can open the annex and pull its log as a maintenance record. All ship's equipment reports to the annex eventually."

"Then I'll find a cable." He hesitated at the door. "Annick. If it's what I think it is, it was someone who could get into his cabin at night. Someone who knew about the valve. Someone who knew he'd refused."

"I know."

"That's not a long list."

"No," she said. "It isn't."

* * *

Edwin Quarry invited her to dinner that evening.

He did it properly: a card under her door, in his own elegant hand, *The Factor requests the pleasure of the Purser's company at the second bell*, as though they were in a hill house on Callis and not on a transport at the end of the world. She considered not going. She considered it for perhaps four seconds, which was how long it took her to understand that not going would tell him more than anything she could say at his table.

The Factor's cabin was the largest aboard after the captain's. He had made it beautiful. There was a carpet on the deck, a real one, Gethen wool in deep reds, and pictures on the bulkheads — little old oil paintings of the Callis hills in gilt frames — and a table laid with white linen and silver, and on the table, under covers, food she had not seen since Callis: smoked fish, preserved figs, a small round cheese with a black rind, a bottle of wine. In the light gravity near the hub the wine moved slowly in the glass, like Szell's whisky.

"Please," said Quarry. "Eat. I know what they give you in the mess."

She ate. She was hungry; it would have been stupid not to. Quarry talked. He talked well, lightly, about the hills above Callis and the house where he had been a boy, about a painter he admired, about the theatre in the port. He asked her nothing at all. He refilled her glass. It was only when the figs were gone and the cheese was half gone that he set down his own glass and looked at her across the table with his pleasant pricing eyes and said, "We're the same, you know, Purser."

"I don't think so, Factor."

"No? I was born in the Lowfield debtors' quarter at Callis. My father was a cooper. He owed the Charter for his shop, and his father owed it before him, and when I was eleven he defaulted and they took the shop and the tools and sold the debt on to a collector, and I went to work on the docks to pay my share." He smiled at her surprise. "I don't tell people. It's not interesting. But I tell you, because I think you'll understand it. I climbed out on the ledger. I learned the law. I learned that debt is the only honest relationship there is — everyone owes, Purser, everyone, the only question is whether it's written down — and once you understand that, you understand everything." He turned his glass. "You climbed out the same way. You learned the Book. You're the only person aboard this ship who reads it the way I do."

"And how is that?"

"As a fact," said Quarry. "Not a judgement. A fact. Like the weather. Like the coil." He leaned back. "Benn read it as a judgement. That was his trouble. He thought that because a thing was written, it had to be *just*. It doesn't. It only has to be written."

She set down her fork.

"The courier carried a second packet," said Quarry, as if he were changing the subject. "You saw me take it. You're wondering what was in it. I'll tell you: my commission. Two per cent of the Concern's price for the debts of the hold, payable at Shale on the Purser's seal." He spread his soft hands. "I'm not ashamed of it. Everyone aboard will be paid at Shale. The crew their wages and their bonus. The captain his pension. I my commission. It's written down."

"And the bonded?"

"Will be employed." He reached for the bottle and refilled her glass. "There's another thing I wanted to tell you. The coil's cooling faster than projected — something to do with the radiator fins, the First Officer says. She's very clever about these things. We'll be able to make the jump to the Fork in seventeen days, not twenty-one." He watched her face. "I'd like to see the assignment entry beforehand. Drafted. So we can go over the wording together. I want there to be no difficulty on the day."

"Seventeen days," said Annick.

"Seventeen days." He smiled. "Plenty of time."

The alarm went as he said it.

Not the whoop of an accident; something harder and higher, a stuttering two-tone that Annick had heard only in drills. *Security. Security.* And then the ship's voice, flat and urgent in every compartment: *Proctor detail to Ballast Two access. Proctor detail to Ballast Two access. Armed response.*

Quarry did not move. He sat at his white table with his glass in his hand and looked at her, and she understood, with a cold drop in her stomach, that he was not surprised.

"Excuse me, Factor," she said, and stood.

"Of course," said Quarry. "I expect you'll want to see."

She went. She went down the spoke to the spine so fast she hit the hatch rim with her shoulder and did not feel it, and along the main corridor past the strongroom and the Hold, aft, toward the long dim stretch where the access hatches for the ballast tanks opened off the spine — Tank One, Tank Two — and she turned the last corner and the corridor was full of grey.

There were eight proctors. Their visors were down and their flechette carbines were up, and the access hatch to the crawlway behind Tank Two was standing open, its cover plate bent back, and they were pulling things out of it. Lengths of pipe. Bundles wrapped in sacking. Cutting tools from the coil gang's lockers, their safeties filed off. A crude gun, a thing made of tubing and a gas bottle and a firing pin, which one of the proctors held up for the others to see, and somebody laughed. And against the far bulkhead, with their hands on their heads and stingers held to their necks, four bonded in dun.

One of them was the young man with the knife scar on his chin. He was bleeding from the nose and smiling, very slightly, the way some people smile when they are terrified.

The one beside him was Dace.

Vashti Kolar turned from the hatch and saw Annick and lifted her visor. Her face like a fist did not change. "Purser," she said. "Good. You'll want to take their numbers."


# Chapter 8: The Sale

*The Account · CR 929*

The Crown laundries are in the lowest deck of the ring, under the gardens, where the spin is strongest and the water falls hardest and nobody from the Houses has ever been. You go down to them by a service stair behind the House Patience kitchens, and the air gets warmer and wetter with every step until by the bottom it's like breathing soup. Then there's a long low hall full of steam and noise, and machines the size of ferry cabins churning the Houses' linen in soapy water drawn up from the cisterns in the spine, and two hundred ballast women in rubber aprons feeding them.

Mags Tiller was at the end machine. She saw me before I saw her. I knew because she stopped feeding sheets into the drum, and stood very still with a sheet in her red hands, and every woman on that side of the hall stopped too and looked at me, and then looked away, all together, the way a shoal of fish turns.

"Mrs Tiller."

"I don't know you."

"No. Calla Dray sent me."

She was a heavy woman of sixty or so, with forearms like hams and a face boiled pink by forty years of steam. She looked at me — at my grey coat, which I hadn't thought to change, and at the place on my lapel where my pin would be if I'd been wearing it — and I watched her decide whether to scream.

"You're the second," she said.

"The second what?"

"Assessor. This week. Asking." She fed the sheet into the drum without looking at it. Her hands were shaking. "The other one came Tuesday. Young. Nice manners. Crown voice. Asked had I ever carried anything for the Keeper. I said I carried sheets." She fed in another. "He said if I remembered anything else I should come and see him. He said his name. I didn't keep it."

Tuesday was the day after Ottilie died. The day before I'd gone into the dead accounts. Someone had been a day ahead of me the whole way.

"Did you carry anything for her, Mrs Tiller?"

She didn't answer. She fed sheets. After a while she said, very low, under the noise of the drums, "Calla really sent you?"

"Calla said Ottilie trusted me. She said I'd know the handwriting."

Mags Tiller looked at me sideways through the steam for a long moment. Then she reached into the pocket of her rubber apron and took out something small and folded and pressed it into my hand, fast, palm to palm, the way you pass a coin to a beggar so the Houses won't see.

"She gave me that the morning she died," she said. "To take down to Calla on my day off. And then she was dead and the proctors were all over and I didn't dare. I've had it in my apron since." She turned back to her machine. "Don't come back. Don't ever come back here. I've got grandchildren."

I didn't come back. I went up the service stair and out into the Crown gardens, where the light panels in the ceiling were doing a very good morning and House children were feeding fish in an ornamental pool, and I sat down on a bench under a fig tree with my back to the trunk and unfolded the paper.

Ottilie's blue-black ink. Ottilie's numbers. More of them than before — two sides, close-written, the last thing she ever wrote. I took out my handset, called up the Catechism, and began.

* * *

It took an hour. When I'd finished I sat under the fig tree for another hour and didn't move.

I'll give it to you as she wrote it. I've had a long time since to think about whether to soften it, and I've decided not to. She didn't.

*C. — Found it. In the Chief's correspondence. I index everything the Chief signs, it's my job, and he forgot. Conveyance of the Account of Liberty to the Ostrava Labor Trust, a Concordance contractor. Every un-Clear portion in the Account. One point six million. Price agreed with Speaker C. Drafted by M. in his own hand.*

*Concordance Bureau of Titles to verify the Houses' title to the Account. If verified, Account passes to Ostrava. Ostrava may then take satisfaction of the portions under Concordance labour law. Transport to Ostrava projects on worlds I cannot pronounce. Contract labour. Terms of satisfaction: the portion as written, with interest, at Ostrava rates. They will not be coming back.*

*Signing set for Burning Day, in the Hall. Before the Ash.*

*In return the Seven Houses receive Concordance peerage, citizenship and estates on Concordance worlds, and a share of the price. The Office's senior Assessors are promised citizenship. I was promised citizenship. I was asked to prepare the Account for verification.*

*That is how I found the other thing. You know what I mean. I have told you. I am nearly sure now.*

*Lots by district. Copied below. Gallows Reach is lot one. They are going to take the ice first.*

And then, below, in a tight column of numbers that took me twenty minutes on its own, she'd copied out the lot list — the order in which the portions of Tamar would be satisfied, district by district, with the number of people in each lot and the transport that would carry them. Gallows Reach, lot one, eleven thousand four hundred. The Deep Ice, lot two, all of it. The Saltmarch refinery districts, lots three to seven. The Sump, lot eight.

Lot nine was the Saltmarch Clear quarter. Not the Clear — the Clear were Clear, they had no portions to sell. The un-Clear living in it. The partials. The children of one Clear parent and one Cleared, with their small inherited shares of Liberty still outstanding, waiting for a sixteenth birthday or a last payment. Ottilie had copied out the names in lot nine in full, because there were only three hundred and six of them. I found Wren's on the second side, eleven lines from the bottom. *Fen, W. Saltmarch, Upper Terrace 4. Portion outstanding: 406 m.*

I read it, and then I read it again, and I found I was counting the lines below it, the way you'd count the steps down to a door you don't want to open. Eleven. There were eleven lines below her.

*M. in his own hand.*

There are a lot of people in the Office of the Account whose names begin with M. That's what I told myself, sitting under the fig tree. There's Morrow in Clearances and Maddox in the strike office and Merriam the Keeper of the Account, who'd been Ottilie's rival for twenty years. There's the Chief Assessor, who drafts every conveyance the Office makes, by right of his office, because that's what the Chief Assessor is for. It didn't mean anything. A man can put his hand to a thing he didn't choose. I'd done it three hundred and nine times.

I folded the paper and put it in my shirt with the other one. Then I went to find the Verifier.

* * *

Thorne was in his garden.

The Speaker had given him one of the best suites in the Crown, in House Constance's own section of the ring, and the best thing about it was a walled garden open to the light panels, with gravel paths and beds of Tamar ice-moss and a little fountain that ran with real water. When I came in through the gate he was sitting on a stone bench beside the fountain in his plain black uniform with a slate on his knee, reading, and he didn't look up.

"Assessor," he said. "You're not on my schedule until fourteen."

"No, Commander."

"Sit down." He touched the slate and it went dark. "You look as though you've been told something."

I didn't sit. I stood in front of him on the gravel with my hands in my coat pockets, and I could feel Ottilie's papers against my skin under my shirt, and I said, "Ostrava."

He looked up at me then, with both eyes. The crystal one clicked.

"Ah," he said.

"You knew."

"Of course I knew. I've read the conveyance. It's the reason I'm here." He gestured at the bench beside him. "Sit down, Assessor. I'm not going to have this conversation craning my neck."

I sat. The fountain ran. Somewhere beyond the garden wall, House children were laughing.

"Your Houses approached the Concordance six years ago," said Thorne, "when our first survey ship came through the Lane. They wished to negotiate the integration of the Absalom system into the Concordance on terms favourable to themselves. Integration takes many forms. The Concordance has absorbed two hundred and eleven systems; it has a procedure for most of them. One of the procedures concerns systems whose economies are founded on bonded labour — there are more of those than you'd imagine; the Charter was very busy, once. In such cases the Concordance's law allows the bonds to be assigned to a licensed labour trust, on verification of title, and the trust may then take satisfaction of them under our own labour code." He spoke as he always did, in that flat, planed voice, like a man reading out a timetable. "Ostrava is the largest such trust. It works on terraforming projects in the outer systems. The conditions are hard. The contracts are long. They are, under our law, entirely legal."

"They're going to ship a million and a half people off this system and work them to death."

"They are going to take satisfaction of a debt," said Thorne, "if the debt is valid. That's all I'm here to determine."

"And if it's valid, you'll certify it."

"Yes."

"Knowing what it's for."

"Knowing what it's for." He looked at me. "I verify title, Assessor. That is my office. What title is used for is not my office. If I began deciding which valid debts ought to be enforced and which ought not, I would not be a verifier any longer; I would be a judge, and the Concordance has a great many of those, and they're not very good, because they all think they're being merciful." He turned his slate over in his hands. "You think I'm a monster."

"I think you're a clerk."

"Yes," said Thorne. "That's worse, isn't it?" Something moved at the corner of his mouth that on another man might have been a smile. "But I'll tell you something about clerks, Assessor, since you are one too. A clerk has exactly one power, and it's the power to say *this is not what it says it is*. Nobody can make a clerk certify a false instrument. Not a Speaker, not an admiral, not the Concordance itself. If the Houses' instrument is false, I will not certify it. I will say so, in writing, under my seal, and the Bureau will stand behind me, and the conveyance will be void." He said it in exactly the same tone as everything else. "That is the only power I have. I use it without fear or favour."

*Without fear or favour.* I'd heard the phrase before. Every Assessor takes an oath in it. I'd sworn it at twenty-one in the Hall, in front of the Ash, with Lucan Marsh's hand on my shoulder.

"The signing's set for the Burning," I said.

"Your Speaker felt the symbolism was appropriate. The Burning freed your people from the Charter's Book; the Sale frees your Houses from the burden of the Account." Thorne's crystal eye moved — to the gate, to the wall, back to me. "I begin the founding page in three days. I proceed in order, Assessor. I've read the Account's structure and its current entries. Next I read its origin. Its first entry. The page on which the whole instrument rests." He paused. "If you have something to tell me about that page, now would be a good time."

I sat on the bench beside the fountain with my hand flat on my chest, on Ottilie's papers. *That is how I found the other thing. You know what I mean.*

I didn't know what she meant. Not all of it. I knew *composed*. I knew *silent*. I knew *ask the rings*. I didn't know what the rings would say, and I didn't know who'd killed her for asking, and I had a list in my shirt with my daughter's name on it eleven lines from the bottom.

"No, Commander," I said. "Nothing yet."

"Then I'll see you at fourteen," said Thorne, and turned his slate back on.

* * *

They tried to kill me that evening in the spoke.

The spokes are the lifts that run from the Crown in toward the Hulk's spine, eight of them, like the spokes of a wheel. You get in at the rim, where the spin gives you nearly a full gravity, and the car runs inward along the spoke toward the hub, and as it goes you get lighter and lighter, until by the time you reach the spine you're floating. It takes about four minutes. Most people stand at the rail and read their handsets. I'd ridden the spokes perhaps eight thousand times in twenty-nine years. I knew the feel of every one: the shudder at the half-way point on Spoke Three, the grinding sound in Spoke Six. I was in Spoke Five. It was seven in the evening. There was nobody else in the car.

It stopped between levels at about the forty per cent mark. Half a gravity. The lights flickered and held.

I knew. I'd like to say I'd been expecting it — after the boots, after Wren's call, after Mags Tiller's *you're the second* — but I hadn't, not like this, not in a lift I'd taken eight thousand times. What I knew was the count. The car had been running for one minute fifty-one seconds. It had never in my life stopped at one minute fifty-one, and the lights should have gone to emergency red if the stop was a fault, and they hadn't, and that meant somebody had stopped it on purpose from the maintenance panel, and that meant they were coming in.

They came in through the roof.

There's a maintenance hatch in the ceiling of every spoke car. It came open with a bang and a man dropped through it — not fast; at half a gravity nothing's fast; he came down through the hatch like a diver into a pool, feet first, slow and deliberate, with one hand on the hatch rim and the other holding a knife the length of my forearm. He wore a plain grey coat. He had a soft round face and very pale eyes, and he was smiling a little, the way some men smile at work.

I had my sidearm. I had it out of its holster, even. But in half a gravity, in a car two metres square, with a man coming down on you from above, a sidearm is very nearly useless; you can't get your arm up, and the recoil will throw you back against the wall, and if you miss, the round will go through the car's skin and into the spoke shaft and you'll be lucky if all it does is breach the car. He knew that. That was why he was smiling. He landed on me with his knees and his weight and drove me down against the floor of the car, and the sidearm went skittering away across the deck in long slow bounces, and the knife came down.

I caught his wrist. That was all. I caught it with both hands and held it and the point of the knife hung a hand's breadth above my left eye and quivered there, and he leaned on it, and I held it, and neither of us said anything at all.

I'm not a fighter. I want you to understand that. I'm an Assessor. I've struck three hundred and nine people to the ice and I've never in my life struck one with my hand; I have proctors for that. This man was a fighter. I could feel it in his wrist — the patience of it, the way he wasn't straining, just leaning, letting gravity do the work, letting me tire. In a full gravity he'd have had me in ten seconds. In half a gravity it was going to take a minute.

I counted. I don't know why. It's what I do. I lay on the floor of Spoke Five with a knife over my eye and counted his breathing, in, out, in, out, slow and even, a man at his ease; and on the twenty-third breath I noticed that every time he breathed in, his weight came up off me a fraction, a few hundred grams, the way it does in low gravity when your chest lifts.

On the twenty-fourth breath, as it came up, I let go of his wrist with my right hand and hit him in the throat.

Not hard. I didn't have the leverage for hard. But the knife came down when I let go, and I turned my head, and it went into the deck beside my ear, deep, and stuck there; and he was choking from the throat punch, and in half a gravity a man who's choking floats up off you a little whether he wants to or not. I got my knee between us. I pushed. He went up and back, slowly, turning, toward the far wall of the car, coughing, reaching for the knife; and I rolled the other way and found my sidearm under the bench where it had bounced, and turned onto my back with it in both hands, and he came off the far wall toward me with his arms out like a man diving into water, and I shot him in the chest.

The recoil threw me back against the bench. The round didn't go through the car's skin. It didn't go through him either. He hung in the middle of the car with his arms still out, looking surprised, and then very slowly he came down to the floor in the half gravity, as if he were lying down to sleep.

I sat on the deck of Spoke Five with my back against the bench and the sidearm in my lap and listened to the lift hum. After a while I found I was counting again. His breaths. They stopped at nine.

He had nothing in his pockets but a slip of film. No identity tag, no handset, no money. A slip of film, folded once, and on it in a neat clerk's hand, two lines.

*Fen, H. Lower Crown 7, 14.*
*Fen, W. Saltmarch, Upper Terrace 4.*

Under his collar, on a thin chain, he wore the small blue enamel shield of the Mercy Guard. They all wear it. It's issued at enlistment. Most of them never take it off.

I reported it. Of course I reported it. I sat in Spoke Five with a dead man until the proctors came, and I told them a man had attacked me with a knife in an attempted robbery and I had defended myself, which was true, and I didn't tell them about the slip of film, which was in my sock. They took the body away. The next morning the proctors' summary identified him as an unemployed ferry hand from the Saltmarch docks with a record of petty theft. There was no mention of the enamel shield. When I asked the proctor-captain about it, very politely, he looked at me for a moment with his face perfectly still and said there had been no shield, Assessor; I must be mistaken; it had been a very distressing experience.

* * *

The next morning I went to Clearances with four hundred and six marks.

I'd sold my second pair of boots, and my good coat, and a set of Charter-era brass instruments I'd bought in a Saltmarch junk shop at twenty and kept on my shelf for nineteen years because I liked the way they caught the light. I'd emptied my savings. I'd borrowed eighty marks from Bastian, who'd lent it to me at once without asking why, and who'd made me tea afterward and asked if I was all right, because I looked terrible. It came to four hundred and six exactly. I had it on a payment chip in my hand. I'd counted it eleven times.

The Clearances counter is in the forward Office, near the Hall, a long brass rail with grilles and clerks behind them, where people come up from Tamar on the ferry with their last payments and stand in line and hand them through and are made Clear. It's the happiest place in the Hulk. People cry there. I'd seen a woman of seventy dance there once, by herself, at the end of the line, with her arms out in the air.

There was no line. The grilles were closed, all but one. Behind the open one was a young clerk I didn't know, reading a slate.

"Clearance," I said. "Portion of Fen, W. Saltmarch Upper Terrace Four. Final payment." I put the chip on the counter.

He didn't take it. He looked at it, and at my pin, and then up at me, and his face did something careful.

"I'm sorry, Assessor," he said. "All Clearances are suspended."

"Since when?"

"Pending the Speaker's review." He touched his slate and turned it round so I could see the order on it, and the seal at the bottom, and the name above the seal. *By order of the Chief Assessor.* "No portion may be Cleared until the review is complete. Payments may be lodged against the portion but the portion remains open."

"When was this issued?"

He checked. "Six weeks ago, Assessor."

Six weeks ago. Before Ottilie died. Before any of it. Six weeks ago someone had sat down in the Speaker's rooms and worked out the price of one point six million portions, and someone else had understood that every portion Cleared before the Burning was a portion that couldn't be sold; and so for six weeks nobody on Tamar had been made free. I thought of all the people who'd stood in this line in those six weeks, with their last payments on chips in their hands, and been told *pending review*, and gone home. I thought of the woman dancing. I thought of Wren's voice going up at the end, the way it did when she was worried. *Will it be done? By my birthday?*

"Lodge it," I said.

"Assessor?"

"The payment. Lodge it against the portion."

He looked at me with something like pity. "It won't Clear her, sir. Not until the review."

"I know," I said. "Lodge it anyway. I want it written down."

He lodged it. The Account took it — four hundred and six marks, against the portion of Fen, W., outstanding four hundred and six — and grew it into the lattice under the clerk's seal, and the portion stood at nil and remained open, and my daughter was exactly as owned as she'd been the day before. I watched him do it. Then I went out into the forward Office and along the passage to the Hall.

* * *

The Hall of the Account is the old cargo bay of the Hulk, in the spine, near the bow — a great iron cavern a hundred metres long with ribs like a whale's, where the *Merciful Increase* once carried the stores of four thousand colonists. It's where the Founders burned the Book. There's a disc of black iron set into the deck at the centre of the Hall, two metres across, scorched and pitted, and the Houses say it's the very plate the crucible stood on; and on the disc, under a column of glass, is the Ash Case.

I went and stood in front of it. People do, in the Hulk, at odd hours. Nobody looks at them.

It's a box the size of a big book. Iron, black and blistered, its cover melted and run and set again, its hasp fused into a lump. There's a crack along one side. If you lean close you can see, through the crack, something dark and glassy underneath, like burnt sugar. That's the lattice, they say. That was the Book. Every debt the Charter ever wrote on us, grown into the crystal and burned to slag in the Founders' fire before all the people, three hundred and eighteen years ago, on the first Burning Day. *They burned the Book. And the ash was our freedom.*

They were hanging the banners for the Burning. Long strips of grey silk, ash-coloured, let down from the ribs of the Hall on cords, so that by the day itself the whole cavern would be hung with them like a forest of smoke. Ballast workmen on long poles, floating in the zero gravity, guiding the silk into place. One of them was singing, under his breath, the way ice miners sing on a long shift, and I caught a line of it as I stood there. Not the Houses' version. The other one.

*— heave and heave and we'll be free,
when the ash is paid, says he.*

Eight days.

I stood in front of the Ash Case and counted them. Eight days to the Burning. Seven to Wren's birthday. Two to the founding page. Three hundred and nine strikes. One man in a lift, nine breaths. Four hundred and six marks lodged against a portion that wouldn't close. Eleven lines below my daughter's name.

And at the bottom of it all, where I couldn't see it yet, the figure I hadn't reached. The one that every entry above it was adding up to, line by line, whether I liked it or not.


# Chapter 9: First Satisfaction

*The Salvage · CR 1317*

The Common Assembly of Tamar meets in a room that used to be a fish market.

Everyone knows it. The Assembly would rather they didn't. There's a carved stone over the door that says ASSEMBLY HALL in the old Charter letters, and benches in a horseshoe, and a dais with a long table and the Warden's chair, and a gallery up under the roof for the public; but the floor slopes to a drain in the middle, and there are hooks in the ceiling beams where the carcasses of ice-sharks used to hang, and on warm days, when the city's heat comes up from the refinery districts and the ice of the walls sweats, the whole room smells faintly of brine and gutting. Yara's mother used to say it was the most honest room on Tamar.

The gallery is full this morning. It's never full. Yara has come down from Ringfall on the early ferry because Pim wanted to come, and Pim wanted to come because he's read about the Assembly in the lending library and never seen it, and because, he said, standing in the can's hatch with his coat on and his face set, *somebody should watch*. So they're wedged into the back row of the gallery between a refinery woman and two old divers, and Pim is leaning over the rail with his slate in his hand, writing things down.

The Factor of the Paschal Recovery Company stands at the foot of the dais with his hands clasped behind him, and he's the most reasonable man Yara has ever heard.

He is perhaps forty-five. Tall, with a straight back and greying hair cut short, in a suit of dark grey cloth so plain it must have cost more than her shell. He has the bearing of a naval officer and the voice of a priest: low, warm, unhurried, a voice that seems to be taking you into its confidence. His name is Aurel Kade. He's been speaking for twenty minutes, and he hasn't once raised that voice, or used a word Yara doesn't understand, or said anything that isn't, as far as she can tell, perfectly true.

"No one wishes this," he says. "I want to be very clear about that, here, in your Assembly, before your Warden and your people. The Company takes no pleasure in it. I take no pleasure in it. I've read your histories on the voyage out. I know what the Account was. I know what it meant, here, for three hundred years." He looks up at the gallery, as if he can see each one of them. "But the obligation exists. It was written. It was sealed by the authority that governed this system. It was never discharged. It passed, by sale and inheritance and the ordinary operation of law, into the estate of a trading house on Vey that failed eleven years ago; and the Chancery of Vey, in liquidating that estate, offered it at auction, and the Company bought it, in good faith, at a fair price." He spreads his hands. "We didn't create this debt. We only hold it. And under the Accords of Settled Space, which govern every system in this region of the galaxy, including — since your Warden signed them forty years ago, when the Lane reopened — this one, a debt that is held must be honoured."

"Three hundred and eighty-eight years," says a voice from the benches.

It's a woman. Small, sixty, sharp-faced, with white hair scraped back and a coat too big for her, sitting at the end of the front bench with a stack of slates in her lap. Yara knows her from the bulletins: Mireille Dunn, Advocate of the Assembly, who's been the Assembly's lawyer for twenty years and has never, as far as anyone can remember, won anything.

"Three hundred and eighty-eight years," says Dunn again, standing. "That's how long your obligation has been sitting in a drawer, Factor. Nobody on Tamar has paid a mark on the Account since the Night of the Hulk. There's nobody alive who remembers it. There's nobody alive whose *grandmother* remembers it. And you come out of the Lane with a copy of a copy of a ledger nobody's seen since the Dimming and tell us the people of this system owe you their children."

"I tell you they owe what is written," says Kade gently. "With interest. As the instrument provides."

"The instrument was a fraud from the start. Everyone knows that. The Book was burned. Burned before all the people. Every debt the Charter ever wrote on us went into that fire, and the Houses dug up the ash and called it Liberty and sold it back to us for three hundred years. Ask anyone in this room."

"I don't doubt it," says Kade. "But your Book was the Charter's instrument, Advocate. The Account was the Houses'. The burning of one doesn't discharge the other — indeed, as I understand your own Church's teaching, the Account was raised *after* the Burning, precisely because the Book was gone." He inclines his head to her, as if she's made a fair point that he's sorry to have to answer. "I've no doubt the Houses were tyrants. Most founders are. But a tyrant's seal is still a seal. If the instrument was a fraud, show me. Show the Clerk."

Every head in the room turns to the end of the long table on the dais.

The Clerk-Notary of the Chancery of Vey has been sitting there all morning without a word. He's a narrow man of no particular age, thirty or fifty, with a long pale face and pale hair cut very short, in a high-collared robe of grey that falls straight to the floor, and on his chest, on a chain, a bead of dull crystal the size of a thumbnail. He has a slate in front of him and a stylus in his hand and he's been writing, steadily, through everything, without looking up. His name, the bulletins say, is Ansel Rhee. Without him, the bulletins say, the writ is only paper; the Company can register, and count, and post notices, but it cannot take a single person in satisfaction until the Clerk has certified it. The Chancery sends a Clerk with every writ, to see that it's executed as written. Not more. Not less.

He looks up now. He has pale eyes, almost colourless.

"The writ," he says, "stands upon an ancient instrument under seal." His voice is dry and very clear and has no warmth in it at all. "The Chancery has examined the seal and finds it to be what it purports to be: the seal of the issuing authority of the Account of Absalom. The Chancery makes no finding as to the justice of the instrument. The Chancery is not competent to make such a finding." He sets down the stylus. "An instrument under seal may be defeated only by a certified contrary record of equal or greater standing. That is: by another instrument, under seal, of prior date or superior authority, which discharges the obligations of the first or impeaches its validity. Absent such a record, the Clerk is bound to certify satisfaction of the writ as written." He looks at Dunn. "Have you such a record, Advocate?"

The fish-market room is very quiet.

"No," says Mireille Dunn.

"Then the writ stands." The Clerk picks up his stylus. "First satisfaction will commence at the eighth hour tomorrow. Forty thousand persons of the liable lines, of working age and sound health, in order of the Company's schedule, to be held at the Company's charge pending transport. Persons in default of registration will be taken first." He writes something. "The Clerk is obliged to the Warden for his cooperation."

Up on the dais, in his chair, Corin Asch shifts his heavy body and says nothing. He's been saying nothing all morning. Yara watches him not say it.

"Tell them the fee," says Dunn.

The Factor turns to her politely. "I'm sorry?"

"The fee. Tell the Assembly what the Company is paying the Wardenship for its cooperation. Per head." Dunn holds up one of her slates. "I have the schedule. I'd rather you read it."

There's a pause. Then Aurel Kade smiles, very slightly, and for the first time Yara sees something under the warmth of him — something flat and patient and cold, like the water at the bottom of the Hulk.

"Forty marks," he says. "Per satisfaction. For the costs of administration. It's quite standard."

The gallery makes a sound. Not words. Just a sound, the way the Hulk makes a sound when something deep in her gives way.

Beside Yara, Pim writes it down.

* * *

They register that afternoon, because there's no choice, because if they don't they'll be in default by morning and the defaulters go first.

The line on the Ringfall concourse is shorter now. Most of the Gutter registered in the first two days. There are perhaps forty people ahead of them, and the bailiffs at the table under the black canopy work fast, and nobody talks. When it's their turn, Yara holds out her left wrist, and a bailiff in a narrow visorless helmet takes it in a gloved hand — not roughly; they're never rough — and presses it to a plate on the machine, and something stings, and a light goes green.

The bailiff reads the screen. His voice comes out of a grille in the helmet, flat and pleasant. "Tull, Yara. Diver, Ringfall. Line of TULL, Gallows Reach twelve, portion of record. Liable." A pause. "Outstanding at last entry: one thousand one hundred and four marks. Accrued at the instrument rate: four million, two hundred and sixty thousand, eight hundred and twelve marks." Another pause. "Scheduled for satisfaction: lot fourteen. You'll be notified. Next."

She doesn't move. "Four million."

"Accrued at the instrument rate," says the bailiff. "Next."

"For eleven hundred marks."

"The figure's on your registration, ma'am. You can request a copy at the Company office. Next."

Pim holds out his wrist. He's very pale. The machine stings him and he flinches and coughs, and the bailiff waits for him to finish coughing, patiently, and then reads the screen.

"Tull, Pim. Minor, Ringfall. Line of TULL. Liable." A pause, a little longer than before. "Medical flag. Respiratory: rime-lung, chronic, treatable." The helmet tilts slightly, as if the person inside it were looking at Pim more closely. "Scheduled for satisfaction pending medical assessment. Medical satisfactions are processed aboard the *Lawful Remedy*. You'll be notified."

"What does that mean?" says Yara.

"It means he'll be assessed," says the bailiff. "The Company has medical facilities aboard. Treatable conditions are treated before transport." The pleasant voice doesn't change. "He's an asset, ma'am. The Company looks after its assets. Next."

They walk home along the concourse without talking. Past the writs on the walls, past the line, past the place where Tobin Rusk's wife stood making her sound. She's not there now. Someone has scratched a skull on the plating beside the nearest writ, small and neat, and under it *T.R.*

In the can, Pim sits on the bottom bunk and looks at the inside of his wrist, where the machine stung him. There's a tiny red mark, already fading.

"They'll fix my lungs," he says.

Yara doesn't answer.

"That's funny, isn't it? You've been trying for three years. Eight thousand for the passage. And they'll do it for free." He laughs, and it turns into a cough, and he coughs for a long time. When he's done he wipes his mouth and says, in a different voice, "Before they take me. So I'll last longer. So I'm worth more."

"They're not taking you."

"They're taking forty thousand people, Yara. In order of the Company's schedule. I wrote it down." He holds up his slate. "Somebody should."

She takes the slate out of his hand and puts it on the shelf and sits down beside him. They sit there. After a while, because she doesn't know what else to do, she reaches up to the galley shelf and takes down the brass box from the drowned annex and puts it in his lap.

"Quell says it's a seal-reader," she says.

He looks at it. Then he looks up at her, and she can see the thing in his face that's always there under the cough, under the fear, the thing that makes him read everything in the lending library twice. "What did she say?"

"She said the old Office used them to read the wrist chips, and to read seals. Proper Charter seals. She said if you put a seal to it, it would tell you if the seal was true. And it talks on the old band. To the bell." Yara touches the coil of wire on its side. "She asked where I got it. I told her. She went very quiet. Then she said, *Keep it dry.*"

Pim turns it over in his hands. He finds a catch on the underside that she never noticed, and presses it, and a panel slides back to show a row of tiny brass contacts and a socket. He looks at it without blinking.

"It's got a cell," he says. "Look. A cold-cell. They last forever." He presses something else, and the dull glass eye on the front of the box glows, very faintly, amber, like a coal; and then goes out. "It's not dead," says Pim softly. "It's just asleep."

* * *

Five passes. The Hulk at the front of the window glows like a forge.

They can't go back the way they went before. The ice wall between the Office and the vault has melted back perhaps five metres since last time, and the tangle of cabinets that came down on them has frozen into what's left of it, so that the passage is blocked by steel as well as ice. But Quell's drawing shows a second way. From the Hall of the Account — the great drowned cavern near the bow, where the Houses held their ceremonies — a service passage runs aft along the spine on the far side of the Old Hold, and comes back round to the vault level from the other direction. Quell never took it. It was solid ice in her time. *Might be clear now*, she said, with her finger on the line. *Might not. If it's clear, it'll be the only clear thing in there.*

They go in through the Mouth and along the ring to the forward spoke and down it into the spine, toward the bow, into water so cold their lamps fog at the edges.

The Hall is the biggest room Yara has ever been in.

It's bigger than the Old Hold. It's bigger than the Ash Church. It goes on and on in the dark ahead of them, a long iron cavern with ribs arching overhead like the bones of some enormous animal, and it's full of water, and it's full of rags. They hang from the ribs on cords, hundreds of them, long rotted strips of something that was once silk and once grey, and in the water they sway — slowly, all together, the way the cradle lids swayed in the Old Hold — like kelp in a current, like smoke. The lamps go into them and get lost. It's like swimming into a forest that's burning very slowly in the dark.

"Burning banners," says Mitya, low, over the band. "For the Burning Day. They hung them every year, Pim said."

"I know what they are."

At the centre of the Hall, where the rags are thickest, there's a disc of black iron set into the deck, two metres across, scorched and pitted, and a broken column of glass standing up out of the middle of it, empty, its top shattered off. Yara swims down to it without meaning to. She puts her gauntlet on the iron. It's colder than the water.

"This is where they burned it," she says.

"It's in the Church now."

"I know. But this is where."

She doesn't know why it matters to her. It doesn't. She takes her hand off the iron and swims up again, and that's when the line goes slack.

Not slack the way it goes when Mitya moves toward her. Slack all at once, completely, the coil on her hip suddenly loose and light. She turns and pulls it in, hand over hand, and it comes and comes with no weight on it at all, and then the end comes out of the dark between the rags, and it's been cut clean.

"Mitya."

"I see them."

Three lamps. Yellow shells. They come out of the forest of rags between Yara and the forward hatch, the way they came in — cutting her off from the spoke, from the Mouth, from air. One of them has a cutter in his hand, the arc still hissing, the water boiling white around its tip.

"Back off," says a voice on the open band. A man's voice, thick, Gutter accent. "Back off out of it, Tull. This is Gorse's. Warden's licence. You're on it without a permit."

"I'm on Gorse's permit."

"Not for this you're not. Gorse says you've been lent out. Gorse says that loan don't cover the spine. Back off and go home and nobody's hurt."

She knows the voice. Kell Amory. He's dived for Gorse for fifteen years. He came to her mother's ash.

"You cut my line, Kell."

"You'd have done the same."

She wouldn't. She doesn't say so. She hangs in the water among the rags with the cut end of her line in her hand and the empty column below her, and she can hear her own breath, loud and slow in the helmet, and she counts it. Her air gauge says three hours twenty. The spoke is two hundred metres behind them, through Kell and his crew. Without a line, in this dark, among these rags, she might find it and she might not.

"Yara," says Mitya on the suit band, very quietly. "Left."

There's a fourth lamp, coming in from the left, low, along the deck. Slowly. While Kell talks.

She doesn't think. She'll think about it later, for a long time, in the can at night. Now she just moves — down and left, fast, kicking off the iron disc with both boots, straight at the fourth lamp, with her own cutter coming off her back and into her hand and her thumb finding the trigger. The diver behind the lamp sees her coming and tries to turn, and in the water you can't turn fast, nobody can, and she's on him before he's half round, and she puts the cutter's arc against the seal at the back of his helmet and holds it there.

Not long. Two seconds. The water boils white between them. The seal goes. She feels it go, a jolt through the cutter, and a gout of silver bubbles erupts from the back of his helmet and streams away up through the rags, and he's thrashing, both hands at the back of his neck, and the bubbles keep coming.

She lets go and kicks back.

"*Ferro!*" Kell's voice on the open band, high. "Ferro, seal it, seal it—"

The man is spinning slowly in the water, clawing at his helmet, the bubbles pouring out of him in a long silver rope. He'll have a patch kit. Every diver has a patch kit. If he finds it in the next minute he'll live. If his crew stop and help him, they'll have to stop doing anything else.

They stop. All three yellow lamps converge on him, fast, swinging, and for a moment the four of them are a knot of light and limbs and silver bubbles in the middle of the Hall, and the rags swirl round them, and nobody is between Yara and anything.

"Go," she says to Mitya. "*Go.*"

They go. Not back toward the spoke — the yellow lamps are too near it — but aft, down the length of the Hall, through the rags, toward the far end where Quell's drawing shows the service passage. Mitya first, then Yara, kicking hard. Behind them Kell is shouting on the open band, a long stream of words she stops hearing after the first few. Her heart is going like the Hulk at perigee.

The passage is there. A square black opening in the aft wall, half hidden behind a curtain of rotted silk. Mitya pulls the silk aside and they go through, and on the other side the water is still and clear and empty, and their lamps go a long way down a straight Charter corridor with hazard stripes on the walls, red and yellow, red and yellow, and no ice in it at all.

* * *

It's clear the whole way.

They follow it for two hundred metres, aft and then round, past hatches marked STORES and HOLD AFT and a long stretch of blank wall where the Old Hold is on the other side, until the corridor turns again and they find themselves in a passage Yara recognises: the one from the Office, the one that ended in the ice wall. They're on the other side of it now. Behind them, ten metres back, the ice is a cloudy grey face full of frozen debris, and the tangle of cabinets that came down at them is a dark shape inside it, like something seen through fog. Ahead of them, at the end of the passage, there's a door.

It's iron. It fills the passage from wall to wall and floor to ceiling, black and smooth and enormous, with a wheel at its centre the size of a sled and a heavy frame of riveted plate around it, and above the frame, on the iron of the wall, there are letters. They were painted once. Red, Yara thinks, from the flakes that are left. Most of them have gone in the wet. What's left says:

WHAT IS WRIT — — — OWED

She stares at it.

"Vault," says Mitya, behind her. He sounds very tired. "That's it. That's the vault."

There are marks on the door. Old ones — a row of shallow scorches across the iron at chest height, where someone, a long time ago, cut at it with a thermal lance and gave up. Quell's crew, forty years ago. A thumb's depth in. Yara puts her gauntlet on them.

"Air," says Mitya.

"I know."

"Two hours ten. And no line. And Kell's crew between us and the spoke."

"I know, Mitya."

She takes her hand off the door. She looks at it for a long moment — the wheel, the frame, the ruined letters — and then she takes the marker spray from her harness and puts a small yellow cross on the wall beside the frame, the divers' mark for *here*, and under it the number five.

"Next pass," she says.

They go out the long way, round through the service corridor and the Hall and its rags, slowly, with their lamps down, listening. There's no sign of Kell's crew. There's a patch on the deck near the iron disc where the water is cloudy with something dark, and a little cluster of silver bubbles still trapped against one of the ribs overhead, trembling. That's all.

* * *

They come up into the Ringfall shuttle dock at the end of the window with forty minutes of air left, and the dock is full of black.

The *Lawful Remedy*'s shuttle is berthed at the far end, where the big ferries used to come in during the Houses' time: a long sleek black wedge with the key-and-sword crest on its flank, its ramp down, a line of bailiffs on either side of it. And coming down the ramp, between the bailiffs, are two people.

The first is the Factor. Aurel Kade, in his plain grey suit, walking with his hands clasped behind his back and his head bent a little toward the person beside him, as if listening carefully to something she's saying.

The second is Sabeth Orrin.

She's in the same good grey wool, the same soft boots. Her hair is pinned up. She's talking to Kade in a low voice, and he's nodding, and at the foot of the ramp he stops and puts his hand on her shoulder — lightly, briefly, the way you'd touch a colleague you trust — and says something, and she smiles.

Yara stands at the other end of the dock in her mother's unpainted shell, with her helmet under her arm and the cut end of her line still coiled on her hip and the smell of the Hulk coming off her in waves, and watches them.

"Yara," says Mitya, behind her, very quietly.

She doesn't turn round. She's watching Sabeth Orrin's face. And so she sees the moment, at the foot of the ramp, when Sabeth's eyes travel along the dock, across the divers and the cargo and the bailiffs, and find her.

Sabeth doesn't look away. She doesn't look surprised. She looks at Yara across the length of the shuttle dock for perhaps two seconds, with her plain broad face perfectly still; and then she turns back to the Factor and says something else, and they walk away together, toward the lift.

Five passes.


# Chapter 10: The Price of a Name

*The Crossing · CR 611*

The proctors' brig was on the second deck of the ring, aft, behind the armoury: six cells in a row along a short corridor, each two metres by one and a half, with a bunk and a drain and a door of steel mesh so that the sergeant on duty could see into all of them at once from her desk at the end. Annick had been there perhaps a dozen times in eleven years, always with her tally, to enter a fine or a confinement. She had never seen more than two of the cells occupied at once.

All six were full.

She took the numbers. Kolar had asked her to, and it was her job, and she had found, standing in the corridor behind Tank Two with the stingers at her sister's neck, that she could think of nothing else to do but her job. So she went down the row of cells with her tally and her stylus, and the proctor on duty opened each mesh door in turn, and she held the tally to each wrist and read the name and spoke the confinement. *Held pending the Factor's inquiry, on suspicion of mutiny.* The gang chief from the coil, Hal Dremmer, with his burned face, who looked at her as she took his wrist as if she were a wall. Two bonded she knew only by their numbers. The electrician, Kofi, who had good ears. Dace.

And the boy with the knife scar.

He was in the last cell. He was lying on the bunk on his side with his knees drawn up, and when the proctor opened the door he did not get up, and Annick saw why. His face had been beaten until it was a different shape. Both eyes were swollen shut. His nose was flat to one side. There was blood dried black in his ears and down his neck and soaked into the collar of his coveralls, and when she knelt by the bunk and took his wrist he made a sound she had never heard a person make, a thin whistling moan through his broken mouth, and tried to pull his hand away.

*Imre, Joss. V5-3011. Bonded. Coil gang.* Nineteen.

"He resisted," said Kolar from the end of the corridor.

Annick spoke his confinement. She did not look round.

"I'll want the doctor for him," she said, when she had finished.

"He can have the doctor in the morning."

"He'll have the doctor now, Sergeant, or I'll enter in the Book that a bonded colonist in your custody was denied medical care contrary to Article Six, and the Factor can explain to the Shale Concern why one of the assets he's selling them arrived with a fractured skull." She stood up. Her voice did not shake. She would hear it in her head later, that level clerk's voice, and wonder at it. "It's billable. They'll deduct it."

Kolar looked at her for a long moment with her face like a fist. Then she nodded to the proctor, who went.

Dace was in the fourth cell, sitting on the bunk with her back to the wall and her knees up and her arms round them. She had a cut over one eye and a bruise darkening along her jaw, and her hair had come loose from its wire, and she watched Annick through the mesh the whole time Annick was taking the other numbers, without moving, without blinking, the way a cat watches through a window.

When the proctor opened her door, she held out her wrist without being asked.

Annick took it. Her sister's skin was cold. She could feel the pulse under her fingers, fast and light, and she held the tally to the number and read it and did not speak at once.

"Go on," said Dace quietly. "Say it."

"Vell, Dace," said Annick. "V5-2231. Held pending the Factor's inquiry, on suspicion of mutiny."

"Sealed," said Dace. And then, lower, so that the proctor at the door could not hear: "Don't you dare. Whatever he asks you. Don't you dare."

* * *

Quarry was waiting in the sergeant's office at the end of the corridor.

It was a small square room with a desk and two chairs and a locker of stingers, and he was sitting in the sergeant's chair with his legs crossed and his soft hands folded on one knee, looking at nothing. He had changed out of his dinner clothes. He wore a plain dark coat now, buttoned to the throat, and he looked, for the first time since Annick had known him, like what he was: a lawyer, at work.

"Sit down, Purser."

She sat.

"You'll have seen what was found," he said. "Forty-one weapons, of one kind or another. Some of them quite ingenious. A store of food taken from the galley over several weeks — I gather it's been coming out in the slab buckets. Eleven coil-gang cutting tools with their safeties removed. A map of the Lantern deck, very good, drawn from memory by someone who'd worked behind the command ring." He paused. "It's a mutiny, Purser. I don't think either of us is going to insult the other by pretending otherwise."

She said nothing.

"Under the Ordinances," said Quarry, "a bonded colonist taken in mutiny against the Company's officers forfeits the protection of the voyage. Article Seventeen. You'll know it." He waited, and when she still said nothing, went on pleasantly. "The debt is called in full. The person is held to be Company property in satisfaction of the debt, and may be disposed of by the Factor as the Company's interest requires. In practice, on a voyage, that means one thing. We haven't room to keep them. We haven't food to feed them. And the Concern does not buy mutineers." He tilted his head. "In practice, it means the forward lock."

"You can't," said Annick.

"I can. I'd rather not. It's wasteful. Every one of those people in the cells is worth something to the Concern, and I'm paid a percentage." He smiled slightly. "But I'll space every one of them before the Wake rotation at the next watch, in front of the rest of the Wake, if I have to. Including the boy. Including your sister. It's the only thing that will stop this. You understand that. You've read the Book; you know what happens on ships where the Factor doesn't." He uncrossed his legs. "Or."

He let it hang.

"Or you can give me a name," he said.

"I don't know any names."

"Of course you don't." Quarry sighed, as if she had disappointed him in some small way. "You've only been in the Hold on eleven nights out of the last fourteen. Sergeant Kolar keeps excellent notes. You've been reconciling the cradle manifests, I understand. Very conscientious." He leaned forward. "I don't care, Purser. I want you to understand that. I don't care what you've been doing in the Hold. I don't care whose side you think you're on. I care that on the day we reach the Fork, there's a purser in the strongroom with the key, who'll seal what I put in front of her; and I care that between now and then this ship stays quiet. Those are the only two things I want. I'll pay a great deal for them."

"And the price is a name."

"Not the boy's. Not one of those six. They're nobody; they're hands. I want a head." He sat back. "Somebody planned this. Somebody chose where to hide forty-one weapons and how to get food out in the slab buckets and who to send behind the command ring with a pencil. Somebody who's still free, and will try again, and will try better next time. Give me that person, and your sister walks out of that cell tonight and goes back to the coil gang and nobody says another word about it. The others too, if you like. I'll be generous. It's a good price."

Annick looked at him. He looked back, pleasantly, his pricing eyes on her face.

"You have until the morning watch," said Edwin Quarry. "Six hours. After that I'll assume you don't know any names after all, and I'll do what the Ordinances require." He stood up and buttoned his coat. "Goodnight, Purser. I'm sorry. I truly am. It's a very hard thing to be asked."

* * *

She went to the strongroom because she did not know where else to go.

She did not turn on the lamp. She floated in the dark beside the cradle with one hand on the Book's iron lid and her back against the cold of the bulkhead — on the other side of which, she knew, was the black water of Tank Four, twelve metres deep, under the ship's shielding — and she did what she had always done when there was a thing she could not bear. She kept accounts.

She began with what she knew.

If she gave no name, Quarry would space six people at the morning watch, and one of them would be Dace. She did not doubt it. She had watched his face. He had not been bluffing, because a man like Quarry did not bluff; he made offers, and if they were refused he did what he had said, so that the next offer would be believed.
