If she gave Tobiah Ruck, the rising was over. He was its heart. Without him the three hundred in the Wake would not move, and the ship would go to the Fork and through it to Shale, and the bonded would be sold, and she would enter them one at a time for six to nine years as they died. And Quarry would let Dace go, and Dace would go to Shale, and Dace would die there too, slower.

If she gave Kofi, or Dremmer, or one of the others in the cells, Quarry would know she was lying. They were hands. He had said so.

If she gave Marit Osei—

She stopped.

She made herself go on. That was the discipline of it; that was what Benn had taught her, at that desk, in this room, for eleven years. You did not stop at the line you did not like. You wrote it, and then you wrote the next one.

If she gave Marit, Quarry would have his head. Marit planned. Everyone in the Wake knew it. It was Marit who had chosen the crawlway behind Tank Two, Annick was nearly certain; Marit who had worked out the slab buckets; Marit who had sent Kofi behind the command ring. Quarry would believe it. Quarry would space Marit, and only Marit, and let the others go, and the ship would be quiet. And the rising — the rising would lose its mind but not its heart. Tobiah would still be free. The three hundred would still be there. And the friend on the bridge, whoever it was. In seventeen days the ship would reach the Fork, and there would still be a chance.

Marit was fifty-six. Marit had been an advocate for twenty years. Marit had said to her, in the blue cold among the cradles, *Have you ever, once, in your whole career, kept a Book of what's owed to us?* Marit would understand it, Annick thought. Marit would do the same sum. Marit would get the same answer.

That was what she told herself. She told herself it several times, in the dark, with her hand on the Book. She did not believe it any of the times. She understood perfectly well what she was doing. She was taking the life of a woman who had done nothing to her and putting it in the column against her sister's, and finding her sister's heavier, and calling it arithmetic.

She thought of going down to the Hold. Now, tonight. Finding Marit in her cradle bay and telling her to hide. There were places on the ship a person could hide for seventeen days — in the Hold itself, among the frozen, if you had a cradle tech to help you. She thought it through. Then she thought through what would happen at the morning watch, when Quarry came for his name and there was none, and she understood that there was no version of it in which Marit hid and Dace lived. He had made sure of that. That was what he was good at.

She did not go down to the Hold.

At five hours and ten minutes, by the strongroom clock, she opened the Book.

She did not know why. She had nothing to enter. She turned the key and the page-glass woke, blue-white, trembling, and the voyage's columns filled it, and she scrolled back through them without reading, past the confinements she had entered tonight, past Benn and Emeric Saal and a hundred and forty others, and stopped at an entry from two years ago that she had not thought of since. *Osei, Marit. V5-2207. Bonded. Clerk.* Boarded at Callis. Debt assumed under the guild settlement: thirty-eight thousand marks. Dependents in cradle: *Osei, Abena. V5-2208. Granddaughter. Age seven.*

She touched it. The Book gave her the voice of the clerk who had entered it, a young man from the Callis office who had made the boarding entries and was not aboard. It was a bored voice. It read Marit's name the way you read the name of a crate.

She closed the lid.

At five hours and forty minutes she went back to the brig, and knocked on the door of the sergeant's office, and when Quarry opened it — he had not gone to bed; he was still in his dark coat; he had been waiting — she said it.

She said it level. That was the worst of it. Afterward, of everything, that was what she would remember: that her voice did not shake, not on the name, not on anything. The Book would have kept it if it had. She had always thought there would be some sign.

"Thank you, Purser," said Quarry gently. "I know that was difficult."

* * *

They spaced Marit Osei from the forward lock at the change of the morning watch, in front of the Wake.

Quarry had the whole rotation brought up — two hundred and thirty bonded, out of their bunks and their shifts, marched by proctors down the spine to the forward hub and packed into the long staging bay behind the lock, where the shuttles were loaded at port. They stood shoulder to shoulder in the zero gravity holding the rails and each other, in their dun and their sleeping greys, and the proctors stood along the walls with their carbines, and nobody spoke. The six from the brig were at the front, released, under guard. Dace was among them. Annick saw her and did not look at her again.

She was there because the Ordinances required it. A death in forfeiture was entered by the Purser at the place where it occurred, before witnesses. She stood by the lock with her tally and her stylus, beside Quarry and Kolar and two proctors, and she did not look at anyone.

They brought Marit up from the brig with her hands bound in front of her.

She had been arrested an hour before, in her cradle bay, while she slept. She had not resisted. She was in her sleeping greys, with her locs loose round her shoulders and her reading lenses still pushed up on her forehead, as if she had been interrupted in the middle of something and meant to get back to it. She looked smaller than Annick remembered. She looked, Annick thought, like what she was: a woman of fifty-six who had been woken in the night and told she was going to die.

They put her in the lock. The inner door was open. She stood in it, in the little chamber with the outer door at her back, and looked out at the Wake.

"Has the condemned anything to say?" said Quarry. It was in the Ordinances. He said it as he said everything, pleasantly.

"Yes," said Marit Osei.

She did not raise her voice. She did not need to; the bay was so quiet that every one of the two hundred and thirty could hear her breathe. She looked along the rows of them, slowly, face by face, the way Annick had seen her look at a column of figures.

"They'll make you choose," she said. "Between each other. That's all they've got. That's the only trick they know. They'll put your sister on one side and your neighbour on the other and tell you to pick, and you'll pick, because anyone would, and then they've got you, because you've done their work for them." She paused. "Don't let them. That's all. When they make you choose between each other, don't. Choose them. Choose to make them pay for it." She looked down at her bound hands, and then up again. "I'm not angry with whoever gave them my name. I want you to know that. Whoever you are. They'd have had someone. It may as well be me."

She turned her head. She looked at Annick.

It was not a long look. Perhaps two seconds. There was nothing in it that anyone else in the bay could have read: no accusation, no reproach, nothing at all except a kind of tired attention, the look of someone checking a figure and finding it was what they'd expected. Then Marit Osei said, quite quietly, in a voice meant only for her:

"Write it truly, Purser."

Quarry nodded to Kolar. Kolar touched the panel. The inner door closed.

There was a viewport in the inner door, a square of thick glass, and nobody was made to look through it. Annick looked. It was her job. She had to enter the hour of death, and the manner, and she could not do it truly unless she saw. So she watched through the glass as the light over the outer door went from green to amber to red, and the air went out of the lock in a single white gasp of frost, and the outer door opened on the dark and the dull red coal of Pell, and Marit Osei went out into it with her bound hands in front of her and her locs streaming, turning slowly, small and then smaller, until she was a speck against the coal and then nothing.

Behind her, in the bay, someone began to sing. A man's voice, cracked and low. *Heave the iron, heave it slow.* A proctor said something sharply, and the singing stopped. Then another voice took it up, a woman's, further back. *The Book is heavy, down we go.* And another. And another. They did not sing loudly. They did not need to. Two hundred and thirty people in a steel bay, singing under their breath, without moving, without looking at the proctors, until the proctors stopped telling them to stop.

Annick put her thumb to the bead of her key and made the entry.

"Marit Osei," she said. "V5-2207. Forfeit for mutiny under Article Seventeen. Executed by order of the Factor at the forward lock, at—" she looked at the clock — "six hours and four minutes, ship's time. The balance of her debt, thirty-four thousand two hundred and eleven marks, passes under the Ordinances to her household. To Abena Osei, V5-2208. Her granddaughter."

It shook.

Not much. On *granddaughter* — one word, the last one. A tremor so small that nobody in the bay could have heard it, under the singing. But she heard it, and she knew that the Book had heard it too, and taken it, and grown it into the lattice where it would stay forever: a woman's voice, very level, saying a child's name, and then, at the end, not.

*Write it truly.*

"Sealed," she said.

* * *

They released Dace and the others at the second bell.

Annick did not see it. She was in the strongroom, entering the release, and the order came down from the Factor's office with the six names on it and the word *discharged from confinement* and Quarry's seal, and she sealed it under her own, and that was all. It was a very short entry. It took eleven seconds. She timed it.

Dace found her in the spine an hour later, outside the strongroom door.

She had washed the blood off her face. The bruise along her jaw had come up purple and green. She had tied her hair back with a new length of wire, very tight, and she floated in the corridor with one hand on a rail and looked at Annick, and Annick looked back, and for a long moment neither of them said anything at all.

"They let us go," said Dace.

"Yes."

"All of us. Even Joss. Haddad's got him in the infirmary. He'll keep the eye, Haddad says. Maybe." She swallowed. "Kolar came to the cell and opened it and said *out*. That was all. *Out.* And an hour after they took Marit."

Annick said nothing.

"Why did they let us go, Annick?"

"I don't know."

Her sister's eyes were very dark in the dim corridor. They went over Annick's face slowly, feature by feature, the way they had gone over it through the mesh of the cell door; and Annick held still under them and let them, because there was nothing else she could do.

"Marit looked at you," said Dace. "At the end. I saw. Everybody saw. She looked right at you."

"She looked at everyone."

"She said something to you."

"She said what the condemned always say to the purser. To write it truly." Annick heard her own voice, level, level. "It's a formula. It's in the Ordinances."

"Is it?"

"Yes."

Dace looked at her for a long time. Then she said, very quietly, "I don't believe you." She let go of the rail. "I don't know what I believe. But I don't believe you."

She went. Annick watched her go down the corridor toward the spoke, hand over hand, without looking back; and she understood that she had paid for her sister's life with Marit Osei's, and that the price had been a good one, as Quarry had said, and that what she had bought with it was a woman who would never again believe a word she said.

* * *

She ate in the mess that evening because she had always eaten in the mess, and not eating would have been noticed.

It was very full. The whole Wake seemed to be there, at the long tables in their dun, eating slab and talking low; and when Annick came in with her tray the talk did not stop, exactly, but it changed, the way the sound of the coil changes when a segment closes. She sat at the end of a table by herself. Nobody looked at her. She ate.

Quarry passed behind her on his way out. He did not stop. He leaned down as he passed, as if to pick something up from the deck, and said in his soft voice, very close to her ear, so that nobody else could hear:

"Watch the First Officer, Purser."

Then he was gone.

She sat very still with her spoon in her hand. *Watch the First Officer.* It made no sense. It made a great deal of sense, and she could not see how. She thought of Nadia Szell at the viewport with her back to the room, saying *Ask the doctor*. She thought of the last of the whisky, and *What would you do, Purser, if you could do anything at all?* She thought of a concentrator wrapped in a towel, and a valve with scratches on its casing, and a list that was not long.

She looked up.

Across the mess, at the far table, Tobiah Ruck was sitting with the coil gang. He was not eating. He had his great scarred hands folded on the table in front of him and he was looking at her — not with anger, not with anything she could name, only looking, steadily, across the whole width of the room, the way Marit had looked at her through the lock door. He did not nod. He did not look away. He did not say anything at all.

After a while she looked down at her tray. When she looked up again, he was gone.


# Chapter 11: Rings in the Lattice

*The Account · CR 929*

The vault smelled the way I remembered. Old iron, old air, and something underneath like cloves.

I hadn't been inside it for twenty-eight years. I'd walked past its door perhaps ten thousand times, and touched the frame, and read the red letters, and never once gone through. You don't, if you're an Assessor. The vault is for the Keepers of the Account and the Chief Assessor and, once a quarter, the Speaker, and for the master lattice itself, which doesn't go anywhere. But the Speaker had told the Office that the Verifier was to see whatever he asked to see, and the Verifier had asked to see the founding page, and the Verifier's liaison went where the Verifier went. So at nine in the morning, six days before the Burning, I floated through the black iron door behind Isaak Thorne and two silent Concordance ratings in white, and the proctors closed it behind us, and the cold came up round me like water.

It hadn't changed. I don't know why I'd thought it would. The same dark iron walls, the same dim lamps in their cages. The same brass plaque on the wall, covered in tiny writing I'd never read. The same little steel safe beneath it with its keyhole and no handle. The same shelf, with three iron boxes strapped to it — VOYAGE I, VOYAGE II, VOYAGE IV — and the gap between the second and the fourth where the third should have been.

And in the middle of it all, on its cradle, the Account.

It's bigger than you'd think. A cube of crystal a metre on a side, the colour of smoke, so dense that the lamps go into its surface a little way and stop. It sits on the old Charter cradle in the middle of the floor — the padded iron bracket built for the Book, sized for something a tenth as big — and it's strapped down with steel bands so that it can't drift, and it looks, as it looked when I was thirteen, like a great grey animal crouched on a stool too small for it.

Every portion on Tamar is written in there. Every child born owing. Every quarter paid and every strike and every Clearance, three hundred and eighteen years of them, grown into the lattice layer on layer. My strikes. My mother's portion and her mother's. Ewan Haskell. Jory Dray. Wren.

"Keeper," said Thorne.

The Keeper of the Account was Constantia Merriam: a tall, heavy, nervous woman of sixty with a House Patience accent and a habit of touching the chain of her office as if to make sure it was still there. She'd been Ottilie's rival for twenty years. They'd hated each other the way only two people who care about the same thing can, and Merriam had wept at the funeral louder than anyone. She floated at the reading console beside the cradle — a panel of page-glass on an arm, connected to the Account by a bundle of fine cables — and she looked at the Verifier as though he were a fire she'd been told to stand next to.

"The founding page, Commander," she said. "As you requested."

She touched the console. The page-glass woke, and filled, and there it was.

I'd seen it before. Everyone has. It's printed in the front of every schoolroom copy of the Catechism, in facsimile: the first entry of the Account of Liberty, the founding text of the Houses, the page on which everything rests. I could have recited it. I read it anyway, there on the glass, because I'd never seen the real one.

*This Account is raised upon the last state of the Book of the* Merciful Increase, *Voyage the Fifth, at Landfall upon the world Absalom, as sealed by the Purser of that Book under the Ordinances, by which entry the patent of Absalom and all obligations of the hold were vested in the Council of the Founders in trust for the people of the hold; and whereas the said obligations were by the Founders' mercy remitted, saving only the Liberty owed by every person freed for the cost of that freedom, the Council hereby records that Liberty in this Account, to be held and kept by the Houses of the Founders until it be paid. And the Book was burned.*

Below it, a seal. Not a Charter sunburst; something else. A circle of seven small stars, and inside them a pair of open hands. The Council's seal. The Houses still use it.

"The voice," said Thorne.

Merriam hesitated. "Commander?"

"Every Charter instrument keeps the voice of its sealing purser. Article Twenty-two of the Ordinances. I'd like to hear the voice of the founding entry."

"The founding page is silent, Commander." Merriam touched her chain. "That's — it's the tradition. The Founders are not named. Their voices — they wished their entries to be silent. In humility." She looked at me for some reason, as if I might help. "It's in the Mercy."

"The founding page," said Thorne, "claims to be raised upon an entry sealed by the Purser. A purser's entry has a voice. A copy of a purser's entry carries the voice with it; that is what makes it a copy. A silent copy of a purser's entry is not a copy. It is a description." He didn't look at her. He was looking at the Account. "Thank you, Keeper. I'll read the lattice directly now, if you'd be so kind as to stand back."

She stood back. The two ratings took up positions at either side of the cradle. And Isaak Thorne floated forward until his face was a hand's breadth from the surface of the crystal, and put both hands flat on the steel bands, and leaned in, and looked.

I'd never seen it done. I don't suppose anyone in the system had. His right eye closed. His left — the crystal one, the faceted gold lens — moved forward a little in its socket, which I hadn't known it could, until it was almost touching the Account; and it began to make sounds. Clicks. A rapid, fine, whirring tick, like a clock with its back off, and then a pause, and then the ticking again, slower. I watched the lens turn. It turned the way a jeweller turns a stone under the glass, a fraction at a time, catching the light.

It took eleven minutes. I counted. Nobody spoke. Merriam touched her chain thirty-four times.

When he leaned back from the crystal, his right eye opened, and he looked at nothing for a moment, the way you do when you've been reading for a long time and the room comes back.

"The seal," he said, "is not a purser's seal."

Merriam made a small sound.

"A purser's key carries a lattice signature," said Thorne, in his flat timetable voice. "Every Charter key ever issued. It's grown into the bead of the key at the Charter foundry, and it marks every entry sealed with that key, the way a signet marks wax. I've read perhaps four thousand of them. This is not one. This is a corporate seal — a council seal, a company seal. It's a perfectly valid seal for a council's own instruments. It cannot seal a purser's copy, because it's not a purser's key." He turned slightly toward the Keeper. "Did the Houses never ask?"

"The — the Founders' seal is the Founders' seal, Commander. It's never been—"

"No. I see." He turned back to the crystal. "Second. The growth layers."

He held up one hand, flat, and with the other made a gesture over it, layering, as if stacking invisible cards.

"A lattice grows in a bath," he said. "A solution of mineral salts in water. Every entry is a layer; every layer takes its substance from the bath it's grown in. The water leaves a mark. Its isotopes — the ratio of heavy hydrogen to light, the oxygen — are fixed in the crystal as it grows, the way a tree's rings remember the weather of the year they grew." He looked at me then, quite deliberately, with both eyes. "The founding layer of this Account was grown in water with the isotopic signature of Tamar ice. Not Callis water. Not ship's water. Tamar."

I didn't say anything. I couldn't.

"At Landfall," said Thorne, "there was no Tamar water. There was nothing on Tamar at all; the Founders had not yet set foot on it. The only water in this system was the water the *Merciful Increase* carried in her ballast, from Callis, eleven jumps away." He set his hands back on the steel bands. "Third. The layers above the founding layer show the drift you'd expect in a bath used continuously for some years — the salts settle, the ratios shift, very slowly, very regularly. I can count back along that drift. I make it about nine years. Possibly ten. The founding layer of this Account was grown approximately nine years after Landfall, in Tamar water, under a council seal, without a voice." He paused. "It is not a copy of anything sealed at Landfall. It describes an entry it does not contain."

The vault was very quiet. Somewhere far off, through the iron, I could hear the station's ventilation, and under it, fainter, the slow deep sound the Hulk always makes, like a sleeper breathing.

"I note it," said Thorne. "I don't conclude." He turned to the Keeper, who was white to the lips. "Thank you, Keeper. That will be all for today. I'll resume the remaining entries tomorrow, in order."

* * *

He waited until we were in the passage outside, with the vault door shut behind us and the proctors twenty metres off, before he spoke again.

"You knew," he said.

"I suspected."

"The Keeper who was murdered. She found it."

"Some of it."

"Yes." He floated beside me with one hand on a rail, looking at the red letters over the door. WHAT IS WRITTEN IS OWED. "It's interesting, Assessor. I want you to understand that I find it genuinely interesting. Your Houses' founding instrument is a forgery. Not a clever one. A council of frightened people, nine years after Landfall, needing a paper for something they'd already been doing, wrote down what they wished had happened and sealed it with the only seal they had." His crystal eye clicked. "It's interesting. It's not sufficient."

I stared at him. "Not *sufficient*?"

"No."

"You just said it was a forgery."

"I said it was not a copy of an entry sealed at Landfall. Those aren't the same thing." He turned to face me. "Listen carefully, because I'll only explain this once and then you'll be on your own with it. The Bureau has a rule for late-made instruments. Many founding papers are late. People land on a world and spend nine years trying not to die, and then someone thinks to write down how it all began. That doesn't make the writing false. If the Council in year nine wrote down truly what the Purser sealed at Landfall, then the Account is a late copy of a true entry, and the obligation stands, and I verify it — with a note in the margin, deploring the irregularity." He paused. "To refuse verification, I need one of two things. Either a sealed contrary record — the original entry itself, or a sealed testimony of what it said — showing that the Account misdescribes it. Which, the Book being ash, nobody has. Or a lodging."

"A lodging."

"A sworn impeachment. An officer of the issuing office — an Assessor, a Keeper, someone with standing in the institution that keeps the instrument — swears under oath, before me, that the instrument is false and that the issuing office knows it to be false. Then the doubt is no longer mine. It belongs to the institution. And an institution that doubts its own founding paper cannot ask the Bureau to verify it." He looked at me steadily. "The Keeper might have lodged. I think she was preparing to. That may be why she isn't here."

"I'm an officer of the issuing office."

"You are." He didn't smile. "You'd need to be prepared to swear it. Before me, formally, under your name. And you'd need to be the kind of witness whose oath the Bureau can accept — someone uncompromised. Not a man with an interest. Not a man under a cloud." He reached into his coat and took out a small flat case, and from the case something the size of a coin: a wafer of crystal, pale gold like his eye, sealed in clear resin. "My reading. Under my seal. For your records, Assessor. It isn't evidence of anything by itself. It's what I saw." He held it out. "I give it to you because you're my liaison and I'm obliged to share my working with the issuing office. That's all."

I took it. It was cold. I put it in the inside pocket of my coat, against Ottilie's papers.

"The signing's in six days," I said.

"Yes," said Isaak Thorne. "I proceed in order." He turned toward the spoke. "I'll see you tomorrow at nine, Assessor. Unless I don't."

* * *

I should have gone straight to the Sump. I know that. I've had a long time to know it.

I went to Lucan Marsh.

I want to tell you why, and I find I can't, not in a way that makes sense. I'd known since the fig tree that *M.* drafted the conveyance. I'd known since the boots under my bed that someone in the Office was a day ahead of me. I'd killed a Mercy Guardsman in Spoke Five with my name in his sock, and the proctor-captain had looked at me with his face perfectly still and told me I was mistaken. I knew all of it. And I went to Marsh, because he'd crouched down beside me in the dirt in Gallows Reach when I was twelve, in his good coat, and checked my sums, and asked me my name. Because for twenty-nine years whenever I'd found something I didn't understand, I'd taken it to him. Because I thought — I did think this, I'm ashamed to tell you, I thought it all the way along the spine — that if I put it in front of him he'd look at it the way he'd looked at my sums, and frown, and then look up at me and say *Well, Hollis, what are we going to do about this?* and it would be all right.

His office door was open. He wasn't there.

The juniper was there, in its sphere, going every way at once. The brown teapot was on the desk with its magnetic base, still warm. And Bastian Oduya was there, standing by the desk with a stack of slates in his arms, and when he saw me in the doorway his face went the colour it had gone in aisle nine, the morning we found her.

"Sir," he said. "Assessor Fen. I didn't — the Chief's not — he's with the Speaker."

"I'll wait."

"Sir, I don't think—" He stopped. He looked past me, into the passage. He looked back at me. And I saw it in his face, very clearly, the way you see the figure on the tally before you've read it: that he was sorry. That he was very, very sorry about something, and that the something had already happened.

"Bastian," I said. "What have you done?"

The proctors came round the corner of the passage behind me.

There were six of them, in black, with stingers, and the proctor-captain from Spoke Five at their head, and they weren't hurrying. They came along the passage hand over hand, the way everyone moves in the spine, and they stopped a little way off in a loose half-circle, and the captain said, quite formally, as if reading it off a card:

"Assessor Hollis Fen. You're under arrest for the murder of Keeper Ottilie Brask."

I didn't say anything. I'd like to tell you I said something clever. I didn't. I hung in the doorway of the Chief Assessor's office with my hand on the frame and stared at him.

"Her pen was found in your quarters this morning," said the captain. "In a search ordered by the Chief Assessor, after information received. A steel-nibbed pen of Saltmarch make, with her initials on the barrel and her blood on the nib." He moved a little closer. "And a quantity of blue-black ink of the kind she used, on the cuff of your second-best coat. The one you sold in Saltmarch two days ago. The dealer kept it. He was very helpful."

"I didn't—"

"You'll have every chance to explain, Assessor. To the Chief." He nodded to the proctors on either side of him. "Hands where I can see them, please."

They came forward. And behind me, in the office, Bastian said in a small high voice, "Sir, I'm sorry, he said it was the only way to keep her safe—"

"Keep *who* safe?"

But I knew. I knew before the proctor-captain said it, almost kindly, with his hand out for my wrist.

"Your daughter's in protective custody, Assessor. In the Crown. Since an hour ago. For her own safety." He looked at me with his still face. "There's been a fire in Lower Crown Seven. Your quarters. Very sad. The Chief was concerned that whoever's behind all this might go after the girl next. Given her father's — situation." His fingers closed on my wrist. "She's quite safe. You can see her once you've given the Chief his account."

I hit him.

I don't remember deciding to. One moment his hand was on my wrist and the next it wasn't, and he was going backward along the passage with blood coming out of his nose in a slow red string of beads, and I'd pushed off the doorframe with both feet and gone straight through the gap where he'd been. A stinger cracked past my ear. Someone grabbed my coat. I twisted out of it — out of the coat, all of it, the sleeves stripping off my arms — and left it in his hands, and I was through them, and going down the passage toward the main shaft, hand over hand over hand, faster than I've ever moved in the spine in my life.

The coat. The coat had Thorne's wafer in it.

I'd moved it. I'd moved it on the way to Marsh's office, without thinking, the way you move a thing you're afraid of losing — out of the coat and into my shirt with Ottilie's papers, against my skin. I'd done it at the bend by the Index, with my back to the passage. I'd felt foolish doing it. I felt it there now, as I went, the hard little disc of it pressed against my chest under the shirt, beside the key on its chain.

Behind me the captain was shouting. Ahead of me was the main shaft, the great square tunnel that runs the length of the spine, with the Office opening off it on one side and the old Charter decks on the other, and I came out into it and didn't stop, didn't think, just turned aft and pushed off the wall and *dived* — down the shaft, away from the Office, away from the bow and the Hall and the Crown and everything I'd ever known in the Hulk, toward the stern, toward the dark.

* * *

The spine of the Hulk is eleven hundred metres long, and most of it is empty.

People forget that. They think of the Hulk as the Office and the Hall and the Crown, because that's where people live. But the Founders built the station onto the old ship from the bow backward, and they never got very far, and aft of the Office, past the Old Hold, past the stores, the spine is still mostly what it was three hundred years ago: Charter corridors with hazard stripes on the walls, Charter hatches, Charter stencils. Sealed decks that no one's opened since Landfall. Machinery nobody understands. Cold, because nobody heats it. Dark, because nobody lights it. The proctors patrol as far as the Old Hold and not a metre further, because there's nothing back there to steal.

I went past the Old Hold. I went past the great double hatch that leads into it, open as it always is, with the empty cradle racks going up and up inside into the blue memorial dimness where, every Burning Day, the people of Tamar come up on the ferries to stand among the cradles of their ancestors and remember they were cargo. I went past a stretch of blank wall. I went round a corner into a part of the spine I'd never seen, where the light panels stopped and my handset's little glow was the only light, and the stencils on the walls said things like BALLAST 3 and BALLAST ACCESS and HOLD AFT. I didn't stop until I couldn't hear anything behind me but my own breath.

Then I stopped, because I'd come to the end of a passage, and there was nowhere left to go.

It was a short dead-end corridor off the main shaft, three metres wide, with a great round iron hatch at the end of it. The hatch was dogged shut, its wheel rusted fast, and its rim had been welded all the way round with a thick dull bead of slag, very old. In the middle of it, on a riveted plate, in the Office's own lettering, were the words: CONDEMNED — BY ORDER OF THE OFFICE OF THE ACCOUNT. Below that, smaller: *Fourth Cistern. Closed CR 718. Contaminated.*

I remembered, distantly, the way you remember something from a schoolroom: a fever in the water, two hundred years ago. A whole cistern of the station's water gone bad. Hundreds dead in the Crown before they found it. They'd sealed the cistern and left it full, because draining it would have meant pumping the fever through the whole station. The Fourth Cistern had been sitting back here in the dark ever since, full of poison, waiting for nothing.

I hung there in front of it with my hand on the cold iron and my handset making a small pool of light on the plate, and I found that I was shaking. Not from the cold. From everything. From the vault, and Thorne's crystal eye, and Merriam touching her chain, and Bastian's face, and *her pen was found in your quarters*, and *protective custody*, and *there's been a fire*. From the fact that I'd hit a proctor-captain in the face and run, which no Assessor in the history of the Office had ever done, and that there was nowhere in the system I could go now where they wouldn't find me. From Wren. From Wren in the Crown, in some House ward's room with a window and a guard on the door, five days before her sixteenth birthday, being told her father was a murderer, being told she was safe.

I put my forehead against the hatch.

Behind it, through the iron, faintly, I could hear water. A slow, dull, irregular knocking — the water in the cistern moving, the way water moves in a tank in a station that never quite stops turning, sloshing very gently against the walls. A sound like something turning over in its sleep.

I don't know how long I stayed there. Long enough for my handset's glow to dim and go out. Long enough to start counting the knocks, out of habit, and lose count, and start again.

Then there was a light at the end of the passage. A small one, yellow, swinging. A lamp. And behind it a shape, coming slowly toward me along the dead-end corridor, hand over hand on the old Charter rails: a woman, short and heavy, in a rubber apron, with forearms like hams.

"You're a fool," said Mags Tiller, holding up her lamp to look at me. "Calla said you were. She said to bring you anyway." She looked at the hatch, at the plate, at me with my forehead on it. "Come on. Before they think to look back here. Nobody ever does, but there's a first time." She held out one big red hand. "And stop that. It's only water. It's been knocking like that for two hundred years."


# Chapter 12: The Client

*The Salvage · CR 1317*

Every diver in the Gutter knows the vents.

Ringfall was built in the Houses' time for ferries, and the Houses built everything large: the dock's air ducts are a metre square, lined with steel, and they run behind every wall in the place like a second set of corridors. Children learn them first. There's no Gutter child over the age of eight who can't get from the concourse to the old dockmaster's office without once setting foot in a passage, and no Gutter adult who'll admit to remembering how. Yara remembers. She took Pim through them when he was six, the summer their mother died, because it was the only place in Ringfall he'd stop crying.

She goes in behind the noodle cart at the eleventh hour, in the dead time between shifts, through a grille she unscrews with a coin. Then it's twenty minutes on her elbows and knees in the dark, in the dust of three hundred years and the faint warm breath of the air pumps, past the backs of a hundred rooms, until she finds the one she wants and lies down flat over its grille and goes still.

The Company has taken the old dockmaster's suite for its offices. There's a carpet down below her, new, and a long table, and two chairs pulled close at one end of it with a pot of something on a warmer between them. Aurel Kade is in one chair. Sabeth Orrin is in the other.

"— the vault door," Kade is saying. "You're sure."

"Tull marked it. A yellow cross and the number five. The Warden's crew found the mark this morning, after the window closed." Sabeth pours. She has a careful way of doing it, Yara notices, both hands on the pot. "They went in the long way, through the Hall and the aft service passage. Clear all the way, apparently. Gorse's people didn't know it was there."

"And next pass she'll go through."

"Next pass she'll try. It's a Charter vault door. A metre of iron. She'll need two windows to cut it, maybe three, and she hasn't got three." Sabeth sets down the pot. "But she'll try. She's the kind that tries."

Kade drinks. In the light from the warmer, his face is calm and attentive and slightly tired, a man going over a schedule. "And when she's through."

"Whatever's in there comes to us. Unopened. Sealed if it's sealed." Sabeth's voice is just as calm. "We take it aboard the *Remedy* and you burn it in the incinerator, in front of me, and I go home."

"Good." Kade sets his cup down. "I want to be very clear about this, Sabeth, because the Chancery's people will ask me later and I want to be able to say I was. I don't know what's in that vault. I don't know if anything is. The family that sold the instrument to the estate always believed — it was a sort of family legend, I gather — that on the Night of the Hulk, an Assessor named Fen stole something from the Office that would have destroyed the Account if it had ever come out. Proof of some kind. Something sealed." He spreads his hands. "Probably it's nothing. Probably it's a ledger of somebody's gambling debts. But the Clerk is a stickler, and the Accords are very clear: a certified contrary record defeats the writ. Not reduces it. *Defeats* it. Four hundred thousand satisfactions, and the Company's whole investment, gone on one piece of sealed paper." He looks at her. "So if there's any chance at all, I want it in the incinerator before anyone else sees it. Fifty thousand marks is a very cheap price for peace of mind."

"And if she opens it first?"

"Then we'll have to discuss what she's seen." He says it pleasantly. "She has a brother on the medical schedule, I understand."

"Yes."

"Then I don't imagine we'll have to discuss it for long."

Yara lies over the grille and doesn't breathe.

"There's Saar too," says Sabeth after a moment. "In case she balks. We bought his book from Gorse on the second day. He knows what we want."

"Does he know we own him?"

"He knows." Sabeth sips her tea. "He took five thousand on account and asked for his sister's family to be moved down the schedule. I told him I'd see what I could do."

"Will you?"

"I'll see," says Sabeth Orrin. And then, after a pause, in a slightly different voice: "She saw me. On the dock, yesterday. Coming off the shuttle with you."

Kade laughs, softly. It's the first time Yara has heard him laugh. It's a nice laugh; it sounds like someone you'd want to have a drink with. "Good," he says. "Let her. People work harder when they know who's paying."

* * *

Mitya's can is at the far end of the Gutter, in the oldest row, where the cans were bolted on before anyone thought about spacing them, so that you have to go sideways along the hull between them and the hatches open almost onto each other. Yara hasn't been inside it for three years. She knocks with the side of her fist, hard, three times, and doesn't stop knocking until he opens it.

He's in his inner suit, with his hair wet, as if he's just washed. He looks at her face and doesn't say anything. He stands back and lets her in.

It's exactly as she remembers. That's the worst thing. The same bunk with the same blanket, grey and red, that she bought him at the Saltmarch market the first winter. The same scratch on the galley shelf where she dropped a cutter tip. The same pictures pinned on the wall above the bunk: his grandmother's flat with its window, a faded House crest he found in the ring and swore was his family's, a photograph of the two of them on the Ringfall dock six years ago with their helmets under their arms, laughing at something the photographer said. On the inside of his hatch there are his dive marks, in fives. Four hundred and twelve. Below them, in a different hand, smaller, there are hers — the dives they did together, before. She'd forgotten she ever scratched them there.

"How much?" she says.

He doesn't pretend not to understand. He sits down on the bunk with his hands between his knees.

"Five thousand," he says. "On account."

"For what?"

"To tell her where we'd got to. After each dive." He looks at the floor. "And to make sure that when we found it — if we found it — it came out sealed and went to her. Not to you. Not to anyone else."

"When?"

"The day after she came to you. She found me in the bar." He laughs, a little, without any sound in it. "I thought I was being clever. I thought, a third from you, and five from her, and I'd never have to dive again. Pay off Gorse. Get out." He looks up. "Then the next day Gorse called me in and told me he'd sold my book."

"To the Company."

"Nine hundred marks. All of it, everything I owe him, everything I ever owed him. Sold it to the Company for nine hundred marks, because the Company asked, and the Company's paying everybody this month." His hands are shaking. He presses them together. "So it doesn't matter what I'm paid, Yara. They own me. Whether I take their money or not. They can call my book any day they like and I go on the schedule, lot one, first shuttle. They didn't have to pay me at all. Sabeth just thought it was polite."

"And your sister."

"Petra's got three kids. All on the schedule. Lot nine. I asked her to move them down. She said she'd see." He closes his eyes. "I know what that means. I knew it when she said it. I asked anyway."

Yara stands in the middle of his can, in front of the pictures, and the anger is so big in her that she can't find the edges of it.

"You'd have let them burn it," she says. "Whatever it was. If it could stop all this. If it could stop *them*. You'd have handed it to her and watched her take it to that black ship and burn it, and taken your five thousand, and—"

"Yes."

"And Pim—"

"Pim's on their medical schedule, Yara. Pim's the one they'll *keep*." He's on his feet now. "Do you think I don't know that? Do you think I haven't lain here every night since she came to that bar and thought about it? The Company's got your brother's name on a list for the medbay on that ship, and you're going to go into the Hulk and come out with something that might, *might*, if the Clerk likes the look of it, stop the whole thing — or might be somebody's gambling ledger — and if you hand it to anyone but her, they'll take him off the medical schedule and put him on lot one with me. You know they will. Kade said it to her face in front of you, didn't he? *She has a brother.* I can see it on you. He said it."

She hits him.

She's never hit anyone outside a shell. She doesn't do it well. Her fist catches him on the cheekbone, more of a shove than a blow, and his head goes back and he takes a step and catches himself on the galley shelf, and the photograph of the two of them on the dock comes loose from its pin and drifts down in the can's light gravity and lands face down on the deck between them.

He doesn't hit back. He doesn't do anything. He stands with one hand on the shelf and the other on his face, and looks at her, and his eyes are wet.

"I'd have told you," he says.

"When?"

"When it didn't matter any more."

She looks at him. At the long Saltmarch face, the stubble, the eyes that are always laughing at something just behind you and aren't now. At the four hundred and twelve marks on the hatch and her own small ones underneath. At the photograph on the floor.

She doesn't pick it up. She goes out and leaves the hatch open behind her, and she hears him, as she goes sideways along the hull between the cans, making a sound she's never heard him make, like a man trying very hard not to.

* * *

Mireille Dunn finds her at the noodle cart an hour later.

Yara doesn't see her coming. She's sitting on the cart's one stool with a bowl she isn't eating, looking at the concourse screen, which is showing the *Lawful Remedy* again, and the first satisfaction lists, scrolling, name after name after name in neat Charter letters. Lot one: defaulters of registration. Lot two: the Gutter's eastern rows. Lot three. The noodle-cart man has stopped serving. He's watching the screen too. He's on lot six.

"You're Tull."

The Advocate is standing at her elbow in her too-big coat, with a satchel of slates over one shoulder. Up close she's smaller than she looked in the fish-market room, and older, and her eyes are very bright and very tired.

"Who's asking?"

"Mother Quell sent me. She said you'd found the vault." Dunn sits down on the edge of the cart's counter, uninvited, and the noodle-cart man doesn't object. "I'm not going to waste your time. I know what Orrin's paying you. I know who she is; I've known since the day the *Remedy* docked. I can't pay you anything like it. I can't pay you anything at all. The Assembly's treasury wouldn't buy you a new helmet."

"Then what do you want?"

"I want you to bring it to me." Dunn says it plainly. "Whatever's in that vault. If it's sealed — and everything the Office of the Account ever kept was sealed, it was what they did — bring it to me, not to her. I'll put it in front of the Clerk. That's all I can promise you. That he'll see it, and read it, and rule on it, under the Accords, in front of everyone."

"And if it's nothing?"

"Then it's nothing, and we've lost, and we'd have lost anyway." Dunn looks at the screen. "Rhee's a hard man. He's not a bad one. He'll do what the law says. And the law says a certified contrary record defeats the writ — not part of it, all of it. If what Fen took that night is sealed, and prior to the Account or superior to it, and says the Account is false—"

"Then nobody's taken."

"Then nobody's taken. Not one. Not the noodle man. Not your brother." Dunn turns back to her. "Quell told me about your box. The brass one from the annex."

Yara says nothing.

"It's an Office seal-reader. They're very rare now; most of them were melted for brass in the Dimming. If you find anything sealed in that vault, put the seal to the box before you hand it to anyone. It'll tell you if it's true. A Charter seal, a Council seal, anything grown under a key." Dunn stands. "That way you'll know what you've got before you decide who to give it to. I'd want to know, in your place."

She slings her satchel back over her shoulder. On the screen, the lists go on scrolling.

"Why do you care?" says Yara. "You're an advocate. You've got a flat in Saltmarch. Your line won't be on lot anything."

Mireille Dunn looks at her for a moment with her bright tired eyes. Then she pulls back the cuff of her too-big coat, and turns her left wrist over, and shows it. On the inside of it, faded nearly to nothing in the thin old skin, there's a mark. Not ash. Ink. A number, in the blue-black of very old tattoos, in the plain block letters of a style nobody has used for centuries: a letter, a figure, a dash, four more figures.

"My grandmother's grandmother had it done," says Dunn. "On Burning Day. It's a family thing. We used to be very proud of it. The number of our first one, the one they carried in the Crossing. It was in the Book." She lets the cuff fall. "Every line's on lot something, Miss Tull. Some of us just know where we're filed."

* * *

The Hulk comes down for perigee at the twentieth hour, and the whole Gutter goes to watch.

It's not usual. Most passes, nobody bothers. But there's been talk on the concourse all day — the Assembly engineers have been watching the Crown, what's left of it, and they think this is the pass it goes — and by the nineteenth hour the long observation gallery on Ringfall's outer face is packed shoulder to shoulder with divers and their families, pressed up against the thick old windows, looking down.

Yara and Pim get a place near the end. Pim's wrapped in two blankets and coughing. He insisted.

Below them, Tamar turns: white and grey and cracked like an old plate, with the dark line of the shelf over Saltmarch and the scattered lights of the ice towns. Beyond it, filling half the sky, is Absalom. You forget how big it is until you look. Rust and ochre and cream, banded, streaked, with storms in it the size of worlds, slowly turning; and against the face of it, very small, coming down out of the dark on its long ellipse toward the top of the cloud deck, the Hulk.

From here she's a dark sliver. Then, as she meets the air, a spark.

The spark grows. It's red at first, then orange, then white, a tiny fierce point of light sliding across the banded face of the giant with a tail behind it that lengthens and lengthens until it's a line of fire a hundred kilometres long. The gallery goes quiet. You can hear the air pumps. You can hear someone's child asking a question and being shushed.

Then the Crown goes.

It's not dramatic. That's what Yara will remember. From this far away it isn't a tearing or an explosion; it's just that the spark becomes two sparks. The bigger one keeps going along its line. The smaller one — a long thin arc of light, curved like a fingernail paring — separates from it very slowly, and drifts a little apart, and begins to burn on its own. It gets brighter. It breaks into three. The three break into more. And then there's a spray of tiny lights across the face of Absalom, like sparks thrown up from a fire when you drop a log on it, each one trailing its own small thread of flame, falling, falling, down toward the clouds, and going out one by one.

The gallery makes a sound. Not words. The sound the fish-market room made, when the Factor said *forty marks*.

"Constance," says Pim softly, beside her.

"What?"

"That was the Constance section. The big piece. I read it on the engineers' bulletin." He's watching the last sparks fall. "Patience and Temperance went last month. That was Constance and Prudence and the gardens. There's not much ring left now."

Yara looks down at the long bright line of the Hulk climbing away from perigee, smaller now, lighter, trailing its last threads of fire.

On the gallery's screen, the engineers' bulletin updates. *Mass loss significant. Structural integrity of spine uncertain. Passes remaining revised: four (estimated). Three (possible).*

"Four," says Pim.

"Four."

"Three possible."

"I can read, Pim."

He coughs. When he's done, he says, without looking at her, "Are you going to give it to them? If you find it?"

She doesn't answer.

"You should," says Pim. "I want you to know that. If it's that or me. You should." He pulls the blankets tighter. "I've thought about it. I'm on the medical list. They'll fix my lungs. And then I'll go wherever they send me and work, and maybe it'll be all right. People come back from things. Mam used to say so." He looks at her then. His face is grey from the cough and the cold of the gallery, and his eyes are very steady, and for a moment he looks exactly like their mother. "Don't not do it because of me. That's all. Whatever you decide. Not because of me."

* * *

Mitya is waiting at her hatch when they get back.

He's sitting on the step outside it, in his diver's jacket, with his helmet in his lap, the way Sabeth Orrin sat on the step inside it the first night. There's a bruise on his cheekbone where she hit him. He stands up when he sees them and doesn't come any closer.

Pim looks at him, and at Yara, and goes past them both into the can without a word and pulls the hatch half shut behind him.

"Four passes," says Mitya.

"I saw."

"Three, maybe." He turns his helmet over in his hands. "You can't do the door alone. You know you can't. It's a metre of Charter iron and you've got two windows if you're lucky. You need someone on the cutter while you brace. You need someone on your line." He looks up. "Let me cut."

"So you can hand it to her."

"So you can have it." He says it very quietly. "Whatever's in there. You get it. You decide. I won't touch it. I won't tell her anything you don't want me to tell her — I'll tell her whatever you say. I'll tell her it was empty. I'll tell her we didn't get through." His voice breaks, a little, and he steadies it. "They own my book, Yara. They can put me on lot one whenever they like. I can't do anything about that. But I can cut a door. Let me do the one thing I'm good for."

She looks at him for a long time on the step in the dim light of the Gutter bulbs. At the bruise she put on his face. At the helmet in his hands, scraped bright along one side from the slosh in the CONSTANCE corridor, the day she found the seal-reader. A lifetime ago. Ten days.

She thinks of Kade's pleasant voice through the grille. *People work harder when they know who's paying.* She thinks of Sabeth's careful hands on the pot. She thinks of Mireille Dunn's thin wrist, and the old blue number on it, and *some of us just know where we're filed*. She thinks of Pim on the gallery, wrapped in two blankets, looking like their mother. *Not because of me.*

She doesn't trust Mitya. She doesn't trust anyone. She's been diving the Hulk for twelve years and she's never once trusted the water, either, and she's still alive.

"Front of the window," she says. "Like always."

He closes his eyes. "Like always."

"You cut. I brace. Whatever's in there, I take it. You don't touch it. You don't look at it. And when we come up, you go straight to your can and you stay there and you don't go near the bar or the dockmaster's office or anywhere else until I tell you." She waits until he opens his eyes and looks at her. "And if you try anything — anything, Mitya — I'll cut your line in the Hall and leave you for Fen."

Something moves at the corner of his mouth. Not a smile. Something older.

"Fen's in there somewhere," he says softly, "counting."

"Then he can count you."

She goes in and shuts the hatch. Through it, after a long moment, she hears him go — slowly, sideways along the hull between the cans, back toward the oldest row. And from somewhere far off on the other side of the Gutter, faint through the plating, the Ash bell rings the hour on the old band, seven notes falling; and on the galley shelf, in the dark, the brass box clicks once in answer, and its dull glass eye, for the space of a breath, glows amber.

Four passes.


# Chapter 13: The Master's Authority

*The Crossing · CR 611*

Three days before the Fork, the captain sent for her at midnight.

It was not the steward who brought the message. It was Lindqvist himself, on the comm in her cabin, in a voice she had never heard him use: slow, careful, with the edges of the words worn soft. *Purser. Would you come to the day room. If it's not too late.* She dressed and went up the spoke to the forward hub and along the short passage past the dark Lantern, where the night watch sat in their bowl of instruments with the red coal of Pell on their faces, and knocked at the door with the brass plate on it, and he said *come*.

He was sitting at his grandfather's table with the decanter in front of him and a glass in his hand, and the decanter was two-thirds empty.

"Sit down, Annick."

He had never used her name before. She sat.

"I've been trying to think when I last called a purser by their first name," said Harrow Lindqvist. "I think it was Aurelio. I think it was the night we left Callis on the third voyage, and he'd found a stowaway in the hold, a girl of about twelve, and he came up here and asked me what I wanted him to write, and I said *write whatever you want, Aurelio, I don't care*, and he wrote her into the Book as crew. Cabin girl. Paid her a wage out of his own pocket for two years." He drank. "I'd forgotten that. I remembered it tonight."

"What happened to her?"

"She jumped ship at Gethen. Married a quarryman." He looked at the glass. "I watched them put that woman out of the lock, you know. Osei. I was on the Lantern. I could have stopped it. The Factor can space a mutineer under Seventeen but the Master can stay any execution aboard his ship for seven days, pending review. Article Eighteen. You'll know it."

"I know it."

"I didn't. Stay it. I sat in my chair and watched it on the screen." He set the glass down very precisely in the ring it had left on the wood. "I haven't slept since. I lie there and I see her standing in that lock with her hands tied, talking to them. I couldn't hear what she said. The screen had no sound. I keep wondering what she said."

Annick told him.

He listened without moving. When she had finished he sat looking at the table, his thumb moving slowly round the rim of the glass.

"*Don't let them make you choose*," he said at last. "Well. She was right about that. That's all they've got." He looked up. "I'm not taking them to Shale."

She had known it was coming. She found she was holding the edge of the table.

"I'm sixty," said Lindqvist. "I've carried bonded for the Charter for thirty-four years. I told you that, in this room, the day you were made purser. I told you I'd never asked where they ended up and I wasn't going to pretend to be the sort of man who would." He laughed, a little, without much in it. "It turns out I am, after all. Too late to be any use. But there it is." He leaned forward. "I want to take her to Absalom. I'll land them on the ice with what's in the holds and every tool aboard and the ship's stores and the ship herself, if they want her for a station, and I'll give them every one of my crew who'll stay. The rest I'll take back up the Lane in the shuttles, as far as the shuttles go. And when the Receivers' court finds me, a year from now or five, I'll answer for it."

"The crew won't be paid."

"No. That's the price. I'll answer for that too."

"Quarry will stop you."

"Yes." He looked at her steadily, and she saw that under the whisky he was entirely clear. "That's why I need you."

He told her. It was simple, and it was exactly what she had been afraid of. When Quarry had recorded the Receivers' order in the Book's annex at Pell, under the Factor's authority over Company property, he had also recorded a restriction on the Master: any departure from the directed course required the Factor's countersign. The Lantern's locks had taken it. Lindqvist could not make the jump from the Fork to the Absalom branch without Quarry's code alongside his own. But a Master's authority was whatever the Book recorded. If the Purser sealed a new entry — Harrow Lindqvist, Master, without restriction — the locks would drop the countersign and take his code alone.

"Forty seconds," he said. "You told the officers that once, at a briefing, years ago. Forty seconds for the locks to re-key. That's all I need. At the Fork, when we come out of fold, the coil has to cool for six hours before it can jump again, but the navigational solution for the next jump can be laid in during the cool. I lay in Absalom. I don't tell anyone. At the end of the six hours, when the Factor comes down here with his order for you to seal, you seal me instead. Forty seconds later I jump. And by the time Quarry understands what's happened we're two days down the Absalom branch and there's nothing he can do but sit in his cabin and write a complaint."

"His proctors control the Lantern deck."

"His proctors control the Lantern's *doors*. They don't fly her." Lindqvist's mouth moved. "I've been flying ships for thirty-four years, Annick. I can make a jump with a proctor's carbine at my neck. I've done it before. Pirates, off Gethen, when I was second officer."

"And when he arrests you? Under Seventeen. A Master departing from the owners' orders—"

"Then he arrests me. Two days down the Absalom branch. And he can space me if he likes, and Nadia can fly them the rest of the way." He said the First Officer's name easily, with affection. "She's better than I ever was. She'll get them there."

Annick looked at him across the table. At the grey stubble and the worn cuffs and the wet clear eyes. At the hands round the glass, which were very steady.

"You'd have me seal it," she said, "and not tell anyone."

"I'd have you seal it and not tell anyone." He smiled for the first time. "Aurelio would have done it. You know he would. That's why he's dead." He said it quite simply, as if it were a fact about the weather. "I don't know who did it, Annick. I don't know how. But I've known since the morning you found him. I've sat in this room every night since and looked at Edwin Quarry across this table and wondered." He picked up the glass again. "Think about it. That's all I ask. You've got three days."

* * *

She did not go back to her cabin. She went to the Lantern, because she could not think of anywhere else to go, and found Nadia Szell on the watch.

The First Officer was alone in the command ring. The night watch had been cut to two since Callis — Quarry's economy — and the other officer, young Brandt, was asleep at the navigation station with his head on his arms. Szell sat in the captain's fold seat with her boots up on the rail and the whole sky above her in the dome: the thin stars of the Long Lane, the dull red coal of Pell. She didn't turn round.

"Purser," she said. "You've been with the captain."

"Yes."

"He's been drinking."

"Yes."

"He wants to run for Absalom and hand himself in to the Receivers' court in a year with his cap in his hand." Szell turned her head, then, and looked at Annick with her dark considering eyes. "And he wants you to seal him free of the Factor so he can do it. Forty seconds. Am I close?"

Annick said nothing.

"He told me," said Szell. "Last week. He tells me everything; he's told me everything for nine years. He's a good man. I told you once that good men are expensive." She swung her boots down off the rail. "Sit down. There's something I want to tell you, and I want to say it once and not have it go any further than this dome."

Annick sat in the navigator's second chair, beside the sleeping Brandt. Szell leaned toward her across the command ring.

"I'm Ruck's pilot," she said.

It was very quiet in the Lantern. The instruments ticked. Somewhere in the hull the coil, cooling, gave its slow far-off metallic creak.

"You've known there was someone," said Szell. "He'll have told you that. *A friend on the bridge.* He likes that phrase; he thinks it's discreet. It's me." She waited a moment for Annick to say something, and when Annick did not, she went on, low and fast and absolutely clear. "At the Fork, when we come out of fold, the Factor comes down to the strongroom with his order. Ruck starts the rising in the Hold at the same moment. The proctors go to the Hold, because that's where the noise is. I take the Lantern with Orme and four of the others — they're mine, they've been mine since Callis. You seal me as Master. Forty seconds, the locks re-key, and the ship's mine. Ruck's people hold the spine. I hold the bridge. The Factor's locked in your strongroom with his order in his hand. We cool, and we jump for Absalom, and we don't stop."

"And the captain?"

"The captain sits in his day room and drinks, and when it's over I'll bring him a glass." Szell's face did not change. "He wants to save them by giving himself up to a court that will hang him. I want to save them by not."

"Why you?"

"Because I can fly her," said Szell. "And because Absalom has a patent waiting for whoever's holding the Book when she arrives." She said it without any embarrassment at all. "You know what's in that Book better than I do, Purser. Not just debts. The Charter's patent for the Absalom system — the title to every world in it, granted to the Company at Callis, recorded in the Book at departure, to be vested in the colony at Landfall. Quarry wants to sell it to Shale with the debts. Lindqvist wants to drop it on the ice and walk away. I want to keep it. Someone will hold that world. Better someone who can keep four thousand people alive on a moon made of ice than a dead company or a mine." She sat back. "That's all. I've told you more than I've told Ruck. I thought you'd rather have it straight."

Annick looked at her. In the dim red light of Pell coming through the dome, the First Officer's face was calm and tired and extraordinarily intelligent, and there was nothing in it Annick could read at all.

*Watch the First Officer, Purser.*

"Benn," said Annick.

Szell's eyebrows moved, very slightly. "What about him?"

"He refused to seal the assignment. He sat in the day room for five hours and said no. And three days later he was dead."

"Of his lungs."

"Of his lungs," Annick agreed.

Nadia Szell looked at her for a long moment. Then she said, quite gently, "I liked Benn. He taught me to play cards, my first voyage. He cheated." She stood up, and stretched, and looked up at the stars in the dome. "Three days, Purser. You'll have to choose one of us. The captain, or me, or the Factor. You can't seal us all." She glanced down. "Choose the one who can fly."

* * *

She went down to the strongroom in the second watch with Haddad and a length of cable.

They had been waiting for this for two weeks. The concentrator's service log could be read only by an engineer's reader, which Haddad could not ask for; but every piece of ship's equipment reported, eventually, to the Book's annex, the great slow record of the ship's own life that grew in the lattice alongside the voyage. Annick had found the protocol in the clerks' manual: a maintenance record could be pulled into the annex directly from the device, if you had a purser's key and the right cable. Haddad had found the cable in a locker in the infirmary, behind a box of splints, where somebody had left it on the third voyage.

He held the concentrator while she opened the Book. She set the cable's end into a socket on the inside of the case that she had never used before, beside the hasp, and the other end into the concentrator's casing, and put her thumb to the bead. The page-glass flickered. Then it filled with columns she did not recognise — dates, codes, figures — and at the top, in Charter letters, *MAINTENANCE: RESPIRATORY UNIT 14-B. ASSIGNED: BENN, A.*

They read it together, in silence, with their heads nearly touching over the glass.

The casing had been opened four times in eleven months. Three were routine: filter changes, logged by the infirmary orderly, with his code. The fourth was two entries, close together, on the last night of Aurelio Benn's life.

*01:12 — Casing opened. Output valve: manual override engaged. Operator: —*

*04:40 — Casing opened. Output valve: manual override released. Operator: —*

The operator field was blank in both.

"Erased," said Haddad. His voice was very quiet. "It doesn't come blank. The casing won't open without a code. Somebody put one in and then went back afterward and scrubbed it."

"Who can scrub a maintenance code?"

He had already looked it up. He had looked it up two weeks ago, she understood, and been waiting to say it. "Three people," he said. "Officer-level maintenance access. The Master. The First Officer. The Chief Engineer."

"Varga."

"Ilse was on the coil the whole of that night. It's logged. Eleven hours. I checked." He did not look at her. "That leaves two."

They looked at the blank fields in the page-glass. Neither of them said anything.

"It isn't proof," said Haddad at last.

"No."

"A tribunal would say a Factor could get an officer's code. Bribe it, steal it. He's got twenty-four proctors and he's been through every cabin on this ship. Or that the log's corrupt. Or that the old man engaged the override himself, in his sleep, the way I said, and somebody else — the orderly — wiped the record afterward to cover a mistake." He pulled the cable gently from the casing. "It isn't proof, Annick. It's two lines with nothing in them."

"It's two lines with nothing in them," she agreed, "where there should be a name."

She closed the Book. Haddad wrapped the concentrator back in its towel, the way he always did, carefully, as if it were something alive.

"The captain loved him," he said at the door. "Thirty years. I've seen them together. If it was the captain, I don't understand anything at all."

She did not answer. He went. And she sat for a long time afterward in the dark strongroom with her hand on the Book's iron lid, and in her head, in the shadow ledger she had never written anywhere, she wrote two names side by side in a column by themselves and drew no line under either.

* * *

Tobiah Ruck was in the hollow among the cradles, alone, with his hand on Lise Saal's window.

"Szell," said Annick.

He didn't turn. "She told you."

"She told me. In the Lantern. Tonight." Annick caught the strut at the edge of the hollow and held it. Her breath smoked in the blue cold. "Your friend on the bridge is the First Officer. Who wants the patent for herself. Who'll be Master of this ship and every world at the end of the Lane the moment I seal her."

"Yes."

"And you knew."

"Of course I knew." He turned then. In the cradle lamps his broken face was gentle and tired. "Who else was it going to be? Lindqvist? He'd have handed us to the Receivers' court with an apology. Brandt? Brandt can't find his own boots. She's the only one aboard who can fly her through the Fork and down the branch with the proctors trying to stop her, Annick. She's the only one." He let go of the window. "Seal Szell. She can fly."

"Somebody with an officer's code killed Benn."

He was very still.

"I pulled the log," said Annick. "Haddad and I. The concentrator. Someone opened it at one in the morning and closed the valve and came back at twenty to five and opened it again, and erased their code both times, and only three people on this ship can do that. One of them was on the coil all night. That leaves the captain. And her."

Tobiah said nothing. A cradle pump sighed somewhere in the racks, and then another.

"Might be the captain," he said at last.

"Might be."

"Might be the Factor bought a code. Might be one of his proctors. Might be the old man's heart after all and the log's wrong." He spread his great hands. "You don't know. Haddad doesn't know. A log with two blank lines in it."

"Would it matter to you?" said Annick. "If it were her?"

The question hung in the blue air between them. Somewhere in the racks a cradle pump sighed.

"Yes," said Tobiah Ruck. "It would matter." He looked at her steadily. "And I'd still need her to fly."

She had known he would say it. She had known it before she came down. She found she was holding the strut so hard her hand hurt.

"And at Absalom," she said. "When she's Master. When she holds the patent. What does she get?"

"A ship to fly. That's all she wants. She told me."

"She told me she wants the world."

"Then she can have the world." His voice was very soft. "Let her have it, Annick. A moon made of ice and a gas giant. She's welcome. I want *them*." He put his hand flat on the cradle beside him — not Lise Saal's, another, a man's face through the frost — and then on the next, and the next, slowly, along the row, the way a man touches the heads of sleeping children going down a ward. "I want them off this ship and on the ice with nobody's number on their wrist. That's all I've wanted since Callis. If Nadia Szell wants to call herself Master of Absalom while we do it, she can call herself whatever she likes."

Annick did not answer. She could not think of anything to say that she had not already said, in this hollow, in this cold, a dozen times.

"I know what Quarry asked you," said Tobiah.

It was very quiet.

"In the brig. The night they took Dace." He did not look at her. He was looking at the frosted face under his hand. "I know what he asked. Everyone in the Wake knows what he asked. He's asked it on every voyage he's ever sailed; it's how he works." He paused. "I'm not going to ask what you said."

She could not breathe. Four in. Hold. Four out. She tried, and it would not come.

"I'm not going to ask," said Tobiah again. "I want you to know that. Not now. Not after. Marit said she wasn't angry, and I'm not either. We've three days, and I need you in that strongroom with your key, and I'm not going to ask you anything at all."

He turned away from her, back to the cradles. After a moment he began, very softly, to sing.

She went down through the struts toward the hatch. Halfway down she passed Dace, coming up, with a lamp and a tool roll, on her way to a cradle that needed tending; and Dace saw her and stopped on the strut below, and looked up at her, and for a moment Annick thought her sister would speak.

Dace did not speak. She looked at Annick for perhaps three seconds with her dark unreadable eyes. Then she turned her head away, deliberately, and went on up past her into the racks, and did not look back.

* * *

The ship's voice spoke at the change of the next watch.

*All hands. All hands. Coil temperature nominal. Fold to the Fork in thirty minutes. Secure for fold.*

Annick was in the strongroom. She had been there for six hours. She had not slept, and she had not eaten, and she had opened the Book eleven times and closed it again without writing anything, and she was sitting now with her back to the cold bulkhead and her knees drawn up and the key in her hand, turning it over and over.

Three of them. Three people who each wanted her to write one line.

The captain, drunk and clear and sixty years old, who wanted to save them by giving himself up to the court that would hang him. *Aurelio would have done it. That's why he's dead.*

The First Officer, who could fly, who wanted the world, who had liked Benn and played cards with him and whose code might be the one that had been erased from a blank line. *Choose the one who can fly.*

The Factor, who had bought her sister's life with Marit's, at a good price, and who would come down to this room at the Fork with his order and his soft hands and expect her to seal it. *Watch the First Officer, Purser.*

And Tobiah, who was not asking her anything at all. Who had known, and had sat with Lise Saal, and had said *I'm not going to ask what you said*, and meant it, and had turned away and begun to sing.

She looked at the brass plaque on the wall. Twenty-two articles in a cramped legal hand. She did not need to read them. *Article Fourteen. The Master's authority is that which the Book records.* Whatever she wrote, the ship would obey. That was the whole of it. Every other power aboard — the Factor's proctors, the captain's thirty-four years, the First Officer's officers, Tobiah's three hundred — came down, at the end, to one line in a block of crystal and the thumb of whoever held the key.

She had never in her life had a power like it. She had never wanted one.

*Fold in ten minutes. All hands secure.*

She got up. She strapped the Book into its cradle with the fold straps, the way the regulations required, and checked them twice. She strapped herself into the purser's fold seat beside it, on the wall under the plaque, and put the key back inside her collar on its cord, where she could feel it lying cold against her breastbone.

Through the bulkhead to her left, very faintly, she could hear the water of Tank Four shifting as the ship came about for the jump. A slow deep sound, like something turning over in the dark.

*Fold in one minute.*

She closed her eyes. She found she was counting — not her breath; the names in her head, the shadow ledger, from the top: every person she had entered dead in eleven years. Emeric Saal. Aurelio Benn. Marit Osei. She got to the end of them. She began again.

The coil spun up. She felt it before she heard it, a deep rising hum that came through the deck and the bulkhead and the seat into her spine, and then her teeth; and the lamps flickered; and the strongroom seemed to stretch, very slightly, in a direction there was no word for, the way it always did, the way she had felt it at every jump for eleven years and never got used to.

Then the *Merciful Increase* went into fold for the Fork, and for a long moment nothing at all was written anywhere.


# Chapter 14: We Were Cargo

*The Account · CR 929*

They took me down to Tamar in a sewage barge.

I want to be accurate about this, because it's the sort of thing people get wrong in the telling. It wasn't a sewage barge. It was a waste tender, a squat steel tank with an engine bolted to one end, that carries the Crown's night soil and kitchen slops and laundry sludge down to the reclamation plant under Saltmarch every evening, where it's broken down into water and fertiliser and gas for the refinery furnaces. Nothing on Tamar is wasted; the Houses are very proud of that. The tender has a cab for two crew and a crawlspace behind the cab, between the tank and the engine, that's warm from the exhaust and just big enough for a man to lie in if he doesn't mind the smell. Mags Tiller put me in it. She said it had carried a great many things the Office would have liked to know about over the years, and none of them had ever complained.

The pilot was a ballast man of about seventy with no teeth who sang the whole way down. Forty-one minutes. I counted. I lay in the crawlspace with my face against the warm steel and Ottilie's papers and Thorne's wafer against my chest and the key on its chain under them, and listened to him sing, and tried very hard to think about nothing at all.

I'd been a free man for about eleven hours. It felt like a great deal longer.

They'd put my face up on every screen in the station within an hour of my running. Mags showed me, in the laundry, before the barge: a public notice, in the Office's crimson border, with the photograph from my Assessor's file, which was nine years old and made me look like a man who'd just been told a joke he didn't get. *Hollis Fen, formerly Assessor of the Second Rank. Wanted for the murder of Keeper Ottilie Brask. Dangerous. Do not approach.* And underneath it, a statement from the Speaker's office, which I read three times before I understood it.

*The Houses grieve with the Office for the loss of a faithful Keeper, murdered by one of its own. It is now clear that Fen, a turncoat Assessor in the pay of the so-called Hold, was the instrument of that organisation's campaign of terror against the institutions of Liberty. The people of Tamar are asked to remain calm and to trust in the Mercy.*

"Turncoat," I said.

"Sounds nice, doesn't it?" said Mags. "Like a song." She folded the screen away into her apron. "They'll be singing it in the Crown by tomorrow. You'll see."

* * *

Calla Dray didn't spit this time. She looked at me for a long moment across the same table in the same cold room in the Sump, with the lamp between us, and then she laughed.

It wasn't a kind laugh. It wasn't unkind either. It was the laugh of someone who's been told the weather has changed in a way she'd predicted and nobody believed her.
