"The Hold's man," she said. "Hollis Fen, Marsh's boy, three hundred and nine strikes, *in the pay of the Hold*." She shook her head. "Do you know how much the Hold has in its treasury, Assessor? Eighty-one marks. I counted it this morning. We couldn't pay you to tie your boots."

"I know."

"Sit down. You look like the Deep Ice." She pushed a bulb of something across the table. It was salt tea, the bitter kind they drink in Gallows Reach, and I hadn't had it since I was twelve, and my hands shook so badly picking it up that I spilled some on the table and had to watch it bead and freeze. "Tell me."

I told her. All of it: the vault, Thorne's eye, the Tamar water, the nine years, the silence. The founding page that described an entry it didn't contain. *Interesting. Not sufficient.* The lodging. The pen in my quarters and the coat I'd sold and the dealer who'd kept it. Bastian's face in the Chief's doorway. *He said it was the only way to keep her safe.* Wren.

She listened without moving. When I'd finished she sat back.

"So you have it," she said. "What Ottilie found. You've got it in your shirt. Proof the Account's a lie."

"Proof the founding page isn't what it says it is."

"That's what I said."

"It's not what the Concordance says." I put the bulb down. "Thorne won't refuse verification on that alone. He told me so. A forgery of a lost original can still be the best evidence of what the original said. He needs one of two things. The Book — which is ash — or a sworn lodging from an officer of the Office, uncompromised, saying the Office knows the Account is false."

"And you're an officer of the Office."

"I was. As of eleven hours ago I'm a murderer in the pay of the Hold." I laughed; I couldn't help it. "He'll never take my oath. Nobody would. Not with the pen and the coat and the Speaker's statement. I'm the most compromised man in the system."

Calla looked at me across the lamp. "Then we broadcast it."

"Calla—"

"We broadcast it. Everything. Ottilie's papers, your Concordance man's crystal, all of it, on every screen in Saltmarch and every ferry and every gallery in Gallows Reach. We tell them the Account was written nine years after the Burning by a council of thieves, and they've been paying a debt that never existed for three hundred years, and on Burning Day their Houses mean to sell them to the Concordance by the shipload. And then we see." Her voice was quite level. "We see what one point six million people do with that."

"I know what they'll do with it."

"So do I," said Calla Dray. "That's rather the point."

* * *

They gave me a cot in a room off the fish-gutting shed, and I didn't sleep, and at some point in the night an old man came and sat on the end of it.

He was a cistern man. I could tell by his hands, which were white and wrinkled and soft as a drowned man's from forty years of working wet, and by the smell of him, which was the flat chlorine smell of the station's water plant. His name was Teodor Rask, he said. He'd worked the Hulk's cisterns since before I was born — the great tanks along the spine that hold the station's water, that the old ship once used for ballast. He'd worked the night shift for twenty years, because at night there was nobody to watch him, and the Hold had found that useful.

"Calla says you're asking about the Keeper," he said.

"Yes."

"I'm on the Index level most nights. There's a valve gallery behind the Index wall, for the Third Cistern feed. I do the rounds at ten and twelve and two." He looked at his soft white hands. "The night she died. I was on the ten o'clock round. There's a grille in the valve gallery looks out on the Index hatch, through the wall. I always look. Habit. You see who's going in late."

I sat up.

"There was a boy," said Rask. "Young. Grey coat. Office pin. Nice-looking. He came along the passage at about ten past and stopped at the hatch and looked both ways — not like a thief; like a boy who's been told to look both ways and is doing it properly — and then he went in." He paused. "I came back on the twelve o'clock round. Half past eleven, it was, when I got to the grille. I'm slow these days. He was coming out. He had his gloves in his hand. He was holding them by the cuffs, like this—" he showed me, two fingers pinching the air — "away from his coat. Like they were wet."

"Did you tell anyone?"

"I told Calla. Nobody else." He looked at me with his old eyes. "Who'd I tell, Assessor? The proctors? The Office?" He stood up. "I don't know his name. I'd know him again."

"I know his name," I said.

I lay there for the rest of the night with my eyes open. I thought about tea in a bulb, and eighty marks lent without a question, and *you look terrible, sir, are you all right?* I thought about a boy of twenty-four from a steward's family in House Temperance, who wanted so badly to be useful, to be seen being useful, to rise. Who'd been my junior for a year, and stood beside me in aisle nine with his face the colour of cheese, and said *Sir, do you think it was the Hold?*

*He said it was the only way to keep her safe.*

I don't know whether he meant Wren or Ottilie. I've never known. I thought about that too.

* * *

The next day was the day before the day before the Burning, and I spent it trying to persuade Calla Dray not to start a war.

I won't give you all of it. It went on for hours, in the cold room and the gutting shed and once, briefly, shouting, in a passage down by the black sea at the bottom of the Sump, where the water laps against the ice in the dark and you can hear things moving in it that nobody's ever seen. I said she'd get thousands of people killed. She said the Houses were going to get a million and a half of them killed slowly instead, on worlds nobody could pronounce, and she'd rather do it quickly, here, at home, where at least they could see who was doing it. I said the Mercy Guard had a frigate and four cutters and three hundred armed men in the Crown. She said the Hold had one point six million people who'd been told for three hundred years that they owed their lives to the Houses, and it would be very interesting to see what happened when they found out they didn't.

"And Thorne?" I said. "The Concordance? They've got a squadron coming. If there's a rising in the station on the day the Bureau's verifying title, what do you think they'll do?"

"Load faster," said Calla.

In the end we came to it the way you come to anything with someone you'd both rather kill: one piece at a time, with long silences between.

The Hold would rise on Burning Day. That wasn't mine to change, and she wouldn't have let me if it was. Every year on Burning Day the ferries bring twenty thousand people up from Tamar to the Hulk for the memorial in the Old Hold — to stand among the empty cradles and mark their wrists with ash — and this year the Hold would be among them. Laundry workers and cistern men and waste haulers and kitchen staff were already inside. When the signing began in the Hall, they would take the Hall. They would take the station's broadcast room, which is two decks above the Hall, and they would put Ottilie's papers and Thorne's reading on every screen in the system.

But they would wait. Not long. As long as I could buy them.

"If you can get your lodging to the clerk," said Calla, "and he refuses, and the Sale falls without a shot — then we'll put down our pipes and go home and let the Houses explain to everyone what the clerk said and why. And you can have your peaceful little revolution of paper." She looked at me steadily. "But if he verifies, or if they stop you, or if you're not there — we go. We don't wait for anybody. Do you understand?"

"I understand."

"And to get your lodging, you need to be clean."

"Yes."

"And to be clean," said Calla Dray, "you need the boy."

* * *

That night I wrote to my daughter.

The laundry serves the House wards' rooms in the Crown — clean sheets every day, clean towels, clean shifts for the children — and Mags said the girl's laundry came down with the rest, from a guest room in House Prudence's section, under the name of a House Prudence ward. She said a note could go up in the folds of a towel. She said she'd sent a great many things up in towels in her time.

I didn't know what to write. I sat at the table in the cold room with a scrap of paper and a pencil for a long time. Then I remembered.

When Wren was nine, and I was still allowed to see her every week, I'd taught her Ottilie's cipher. The Catechism of Mercy, line and word and letter. I don't know why. Because it was a game, and I'd loved it at twelve, and I wanted her to have something of mine that wasn't a portion. She'd been very good at it. Much better than me. She'd sent me messages in it for a year afterward, on the backs of her school drawings — *DA YOU SMELL OF FERRY*, *MAM SAYS NO* — and then she'd turned ten and lost interest, the way children do, and I'd kept the drawings in a box that had burned, I supposed, with everything else in Lower Crown Seven.

I wrote it in the cipher. I don't know if she'd remember. I hoped she would.

*Happy birthday. I am sorry I am not there. I did not do what they say. Be brave. On the Burning, stay by a door.*

It came to eleven lines of numbers. I folded it very small and gave it to Mags, and she tucked it into the hem of her apron, and looked at me for a moment with her boiled pink face, and said, "You've got a girl."

"Yes."

"How old?"

"Sixteen," I said. "Tomorrow."

Mags nodded slowly. "Mine was sixteen when they struck her man," she said. "First time. Twenty-two years ago. Lot of good it did me, being sixteen." She patted the hem. "I'll put it in the softest one."

* * *

Calla came and sat with me later. I don't know why. I think she couldn't sleep either.

She brought a bottle — not tea, this time; ice spirit, the colourless kind they distil in the galleries out of refinery alcohol and kelp, that burns like the Deep Ice — and two bulbs, and sat on the end of the cot where the cistern man had sat, and poured, and didn't say anything for a while.

"What's that?" she said at last.

I'd taken the key out from under my shirt without noticing. I do it when I'm thinking. I was turning it over in my fingers on its chain, the way I'd turned it a thousand times, and I looked down at it as though somebody else had put it there.

"My mother's," I said.

"Can I see?"

I held it out on its chain. She took it in her damaged hand, the one with two fingers gone, and turned it over in the lamplight. A long iron key the length of my palm, its bit cut in an intricate pattern of notches, its bow set with that dull bead of something that might have been glass. Very old. Very worn. You could see where generations of thumbs had rubbed the shank smooth.

"Ice families all have something," said Calla. "Did you know that?"

"I know."

"Mine had a spoon. Pewter. With a ship on the handle — a long thing, with a dome at one end, like a seed. My grandmother said it came from the Crossing. She used to let me hold it on Burning Day." She turned the key over. "My mother sold it the year Jory was struck, to pay the shortfall. Forty marks. The dealer said it was the oldest thing he'd ever bought. He's probably sold it to some House family by now, to put in a cabinet."

"Mine said not to sell it," I said. "That was all she said, when they lifted me. *Don't sell it.*"

"And you didn't."

"I didn't."

"What does it open?"

"Nothing. Nobody knows. It's just old." I took it back from her and let it hang. "Every family in Gallows Reach has some piece of junk like it. You know that."

"Gallows Reach," said Calla. "The first ones sent down." She drank. "Do you know who they were? The first Struck? The very first ones, the year of Landfall?"

"No."

"Neither does anyone. The Houses never wrote it down. Or they did and it's in a drawer somewhere in your Office." She looked at the key swinging on its chain between us. "Strange, isn't it. That they'd send people down to the ice to found the oldest town on Tamar and not write down who they were. You'd think they'd want to remember. You'd think they'd want them in the Catechism. *Who did we send to the ice?*"

"Maybe they were ashamed."

"The Houses?" Calla laughed. "The Houses have never been ashamed of anything in three hundred years."

She was quiet for a while. Then she said, without looking at me, "What did he say? Jory. When you struck him. You remember them all, they say."

I remembered.

"He asked me how long," I said. "I told him two years. He nodded. Then he said, *Will you tell my sister I'm sorry about the boots.* That was all. I didn't know what it meant. I wrote it down. It was in the file." I looked at her. "I never told you. I should have. It was in the file for seven years."

Calla Dray sat very still on the end of the cot with the bulb in her damaged hand.

"I had a pair of boots," she said at last. "Good ones. Saltmarch leather. I saved for them for a year. He borrowed them the week before, to go to a dance in the Clear quarter, and he got drunk and lost one in a canal." She laughed — a strange, short, surprised sound — and put her other hand over her face. "I didn't speak to him for three days. Then you came." She sat with her hand over her face for a long time. Then she took it away and poured another for both of us, very steadily, and drank hers, and stood up.

"Get some sleep, Fen," she said. "Tomorrow we go and fetch your boy."

* * *

The squadron came out of the Lane the next morning.

I watched it in the gutting shed with forty Hold people and two hundred fish, on a screen somebody had wired to the Saltmarch public feed. There's a camera on the ice shelf that watches the sky for the ferries, and someone in the feed office had turned it toward the Lane point, the way you'd turn your head toward a noise, and kept it there.

You could see the Lane point from Tamar if you knew where to look: a faint smear of light in the black above Absalom's rim, where space is thin. Mostly nothing came out of it. For three hundred years nothing came out of it at all. Then six years ago the first Concordance survey ship, and since then, every year or so, one or two more. Never more than two.

Five came out that morning, one after another, and slid into Tamar orbit in a line.

Two were escorts. Long and white and smooth, like the *Probity*, but meaner, with their weapons showing now — clusters of dark blisters along their flanks. They took station at either end of the line. Between them came the transports.

I've tried to describe them since and I can't do it. They were bigger than the Hulk. Each one. Bigger than the Hulk and the Crown and the docks and everything else in Increase Station together: vast blunt white cylinders, ribbed like the inside of a fish, with rows of hatches down their sides, hundreds of them, standing open. They had no lights showing except at their bows. The rest of them was dark. Not dark like a ship at night. Dark like a room nobody lives in yet.

"What are those?" said a child at the front of the shed.

Nobody answered her.

I stood at the back with my arms folded and counted. I couldn't help it. Ottilie's lot list was in my shirt, and I knew the figures by heart: lot one, Gallows Reach, eleven thousand four hundred. Lot two, the Deep Ice. Lots three to seven, the refineries. Eight, the Sump. Nine, the Clear quarter partials. Three hundred and six names. Eleven lines below my daughter's.

The transports hung in the black above Tamar with their hatches open. I didn't know how many people each of them could hold. I didn't want to know. I found I was working it out anyway — from the length of them, and the spacing of the hatches, and the size of a cradle in the Old Hold, which I'd stood among every Burning Day of my life — and I stopped, because I didn't like the number, and then I worked it out again to check.

"Like hulls," said Calla softly, beside me. I hadn't heard her come. "Waiting to be filled."

On the screen, very slowly, the five ships settled into their places in orbit and were still. A caption came up across the bottom of the picture, in the Speaker's office's elegant script: *The Houses welcome our friends of the Concordance of Worlds, who come to share in the celebration of the three hundred and eighteenth Burning.*

Somebody at the front of the shed began to laugh, and then stopped.

Wren was sixteen that day. I hadn't forgotten. I stood in the gutting shed with forty people and two hundred fish and the transports hanging on the screen, and I thought: she's sixteen, she's Clear by the law of every year but this one, and she's in a room in House Prudence with a guard on the door and a note in a towel that she might not be able to read. I thought: tomorrow. I thought: Bastian. And I found that my hand had gone up under my shirt and was holding my mother's key, hard, the way you'd hold the rail of a spoke car that has stopped between levels, and I couldn't make it let go.


# Chapter 15: What Is Writ

*The Salvage · CR 1317*

"You don't cut a door," says Mitya. "You cut what holds it."

They're hanging in the water in front of the vault with their lamps on the iron. Three passes. The Hulk at the front of this window was so hot they had to wait an hour on the sled for the Mouth to stop glowing, and even now, deep in the spine, the water is warmer than Yara has ever felt it — not warm, nothing in the Hulk is ever warm, but a degree or two off the cold, enough that she can feel the difference through the shell like a breath on her neck. The ice wall behind them in the Office passage has gone back another eight metres since last time. The cabinets that came down at them are standing clear of it now, a tangle of torn steel. The thing in the coat is gone. She doesn't know where.

The door is what it was. A metre of black Charter iron, filling the passage. The great wheel at its centre. The ruined red letters above it.

WHAT IS WRIT — — — OWED

"Look," says Mitya. He takes her lamp hand in his gauntlet and moves it, slowly, along the seam where the door meets its frame. "See those? Under the frame lip."

She sees them. Every half-metre or so, round the whole edge of the door, there's a dark square in the frame: the end of a bolt, as thick as her wrist, driven out from the door into the iron of the wall.

"Dogs," says Mitya. "The wheel drives them. Twelve, I'd guess. Turn the wheel and they pull back and the door swings. It's a Charter door; it's built to hold against vacuum and fire and a ship breaking up round it. Quell's people went at the face of it with lances, the poor bastards. You'll never cut a metre of iron with a lance. But the dogs are only iron as thick as your arm." He lets go of her hand. "Cut the dogs and the door's just a door."

"How long?"

"Each one?" He considers it, floating there, his head tilted in the helmet the way it always tilts when he's pricing a job. "Twenty minutes. Maybe twenty-five. They're good iron."

"Twelve dogs."

"Four hours. Five. If the cutter holds. If nobody bothers us." He unclips the cutter from his back. "We've got six hours of window and five of air."

She looks at him. Through the faceplate, in the lamp, his face is calm and absorbed and very nearly happy, the way it always is when there's something difficult in front of him that he knows how to do. There's still a faint shadow on his cheekbone where she hit him.

"Then cut," she says.

* * *

He cuts.

She braces. That's the work: she holds the cutter's head steady against the seam while he guides the arc, because in the water the cutter's thrust will push it off the iron if nobody holds it, and she keeps the line clear, and she watches the passage behind them. The arc boils the water white. The bubbles stream up past her faceplate in a hissing silver curtain and gather against the ceiling of the passage in a trembling sheet. The iron glows red, then orange, then a fierce small white, and drops of slag break away from it and cool instantly into black beads and drift.

The first dog takes twenty-two minutes. When it goes, she feels it through the cutter — a sudden give, a lurch — and Mitya laughs on the suit band, softly, and says *one*.

The second takes twenty. The third, twenty-six; it's thicker, or the iron's harder there. Between the fourth and the fifth he changes the cutter's tip, and she watches the passage while he does it, and it's empty. Her lamp goes a long way down it in the still water, all the way to the cabinets and the ice, and there's nothing there but the slow drift of slag beads and old cards.

"Where's Kell?" she says.

"Home, if he's got sense."

"He hasn't."

"No." Mitya fits the new tip. "Ferro'll have told him to stay off. Ferro lost an ear, you know. Frostbite, round the back where the seal went. Kell says he's going to have you for it." He sets the cutter back against the seam. "Brace."

She braces.

They don't talk much. They never have, in the water; there's no need. She knows from the angle of his elbow when he's going to shift the arc and from the sound of his breath when the iron's fighting him. Six years. Three of them in the same bunk. She'd forgotten what it was like, working with someone you don't have to watch.

At the sixth dog, he says, without looking up from the arc, "I told her about the cross."

She doesn't move. She keeps the cutter head steady on the iron.

"The yellow cross. And the five. I told her the morning after, when she came to the bar." His breath is very even. "I didn't tell her about the passage. The long way, round through the Hall. Gorse's people found the cross because they were looking for it — they'd have found it anyway, it's on the main way in. But they didn't know how we'd got to it. They still don't. I didn't tell her."

"Why not?"

"I don't know." The arc hisses. "Because it was ours, I suppose."

The sixth dog goes. *Six*, he says. He moves along the seam to the seventh.

She watches the back of his helmet in the lamp light, the scraped bright patch on the shell where the slosh threw him against the doorframe. She thinks of the four hundred and twelve marks on his hatch and her small ones underneath.

"Mitya."

"Mm."

"Brace yourself," she says, and takes the cutter head in both hands.

* * *

At the ninth dog, the Hulk moves.

It isn't like the slosh. It isn't like the groan in the Office that brought the cabinets down. It starts below hearing, a vibration that comes up through the iron of the door and the frame and the walls of the passage and into her boots and her gauntlets and her teeth, so deep and so steady that for a moment she thinks it's the cutter. Then the cutter stops — Mitya's taken his thumb off it — and the vibration doesn't.

"She's turning," says Mitya. His voice is very quiet. "Feel it? She's turning over."

The tumble. When the Crown went at the last perigee, the Hulk lost a third of her mass on one side, and since then the engineers' bulletins have been saying the same thing in longer and longer words: her spin is changing. Her slow end-over-end roll is becoming something else, a wobble, a precession. Every hour, a little more. And somewhere in her, now, something has given way to it — some beam or bulkhead that held her shape for seven hundred years has decided it's done — and the whole long iron body of her is beginning, very slowly, to twist.

"Keep cutting," says Yara.

"Yara—"

"Three to go. Keep cutting."

He cuts. The vibration doesn't stop. It rises and falls, rises and falls, like the breathing she used to feel through the sled clamps in the first passes, only bigger now, much bigger, and with a sound in it that she doesn't like at all: a long, low, grinding moan, iron on iron, coming from somewhere down the spine toward the stern.

The ninth dog goes. *Nine.*

That's when the lamps come round the corner.

Four of them. Yellow. Not from the Office passage, where she's been watching. From the other way: from the service corridor, the long way round through the Hall, the way that was theirs.

"Mitya."

"I see them." He's already turning, the cutter coming round in his hands. "How—"

"Doesn't matter."

It doesn't. They come down the corridor fast, kicking hard, two abreast, and the one in front has something in his hands that isn't a cutter: a long-barrelled thing with a fat canister on its side that she recognises after a moment as a hull-spiker, the kind welders use to nail patches over breaches. It fires steel bolts the length of a finger. In air it'd go through a shell like paper. In water it's slower, but not much.

"*Tull.*" Kell Amory's voice on the open band, thick and hoarse. "You don't move. Saar, you don't move. Put the cutter down. Back off from that door."

"Kell," says Mitya, very reasonably. "We're nearly through. Nine of twelve. Why don't we—"

The spiker coughs. Yara sees the bolt — a silver streak in the lamp light, trailing a thread of bubbles — and it goes past Mitya's helmet a hand's breadth from the faceplate and strikes the vault door behind him with a sound like a bell, and stays there, quivering, buried to half its length in the iron.

"Next one's in your seal," says Kell. "Like Ferro's. Back off."

Mitya backs off. Slowly, with the cutter held out to one side, away from his body. Yara backs off with him. They hang in the water a few metres from the door with the four yellow lamps on them, and the vibration comes up through the water into her shell, and the grinding moan from the stern goes on and on.

"Gorse wants what's in there," says Kell. "So does the Warden. There's a bounty on it now, did you know? Warden's paying a hundred thousand. Higher than your Vey woman." He laughs, harshly. "You did the hard work, Tull. I'll give you that. Nine dogs. We'll do the other three."

"She's turning, Kell," says Yara. "Feel it. She's coming apart."

"She's been coming apart for thirty years."

"Not like this."

And as she says it, the Hulk moves again.

This time it's not a vibration. It's a blow — a single huge shuddering lurch that comes through everything at once, as if the whole spine has been struck by something the size of a world. The water in the passage jerks sideways. Yara's slammed against the wall, her helmet ringing. The lamps swing wildly. Someone's screaming on the open band. And from down the service corridor, the way Kell's crew came, there's a sound she's never heard in twelve years of diving the Hulk: a long tearing shriek of metal, high and rising, going on and on, as if the ship herself were crying out.

Then the ceiling comes down.

Not all of it. A section of the service corridor's roof, three metres back from the vault door, where an old Charter beam crosses it. The beam tears loose at one end in a cloud of rust and silt and swings down through the water like a door slamming, slow and enormous and absolutely unstoppable, and everything under it goes dark.

* * *

When the silt clears, Kell's crew are gone.

She can see their lamps a long way off, back down the service corridor, going fast. One of them is trailing a thread of silver bubbles. They don't look back. She doesn't blame them. She'd have gone too.

She finds Mitya under the beam.

It's lying diagonally across the passage now, one end still fixed to the wall and the other wedged against the floor beside the vault door, and Mitya's between it and the floor. Not crushed. Pinned. It's caught him across the thighs and pressed him down against the deck, and the cutter's gone, and his lamp is shining up at the ceiling at a strange angle, and when she gets to him and puts her faceplate against his she can see his face.

He's very calm. That's the first thing. He looks at her through the glass with his long Saltmarch face, and he isn't laughing, and he isn't frightened. He looks the way he looked at the door. As if there's something difficult in front of him that he knows how to do.

"Hello," he says.

"Hold still."

"I'm holding." His voice on the suit band is thin. "Can't do much else."

She gets her shoulder under the beam. She braces her boots against the deck and pushes, and nothing happens. She pushes again. In the water she has no weight to put into it, nothing but her legs, and the beam is a beam from the spine of a ship eleven hundred metres long, and it doesn't so much as tremble.

"Yara."

"I'll get the jack. There's a jack on the sled—"

"The sled's an hour back. Yara." He reaches up with one gauntlet and puts it on her arm. "Look at my gauge."

She looks. The air line from his tank to his helmet runs over his left shoulder and down under his arm, and where it goes under his arm it goes under the beam, and the beam has pinched it nearly flat. The gauge on his chest reads eleven minutes. As she watches, it goes to ten.

"I can cut it," she says. "I can cut the beam. Where's the cutter—"

"Under me, I think. Or down the passage. It went when she hit." He tightens his hand on her arm. "Ten minutes, love. You can't cut a beam that size in ten minutes. You couldn't cut it in an hour." He waits for her to understand it. "And you've got your own air to think about. How much?"

She doesn't look.

"How much, Yara?"

"Two hours."

"An hour back to the sled," says Mitya. "If the corridor's still there. If she doesn't turn over again." He's quiet for a moment. The vibration comes up through the deck into both of them. "You have to go."

"No."

"Yes. You know it. I can see you knowing it." He smiles then, a little. It's not much of a smile, through the glass, in the bad light. "Don't make me watch you work it out. I hate it when you count."

She stays. She stays with her faceplate against his and her hand on his gauntlet on her arm, and she doesn't count anything. The gauge goes to nine, and to eight.

"I'd have told you," he says. "About Sabeth. I said that, didn't I? In the can."

"You said it."

"I meant it." His breath is starting to come short. She can hear it on the band, quick and shallow, the way the bonded used to breathe in the old stories, saving air. "And the passage. I didn't tell her the passage. I want you to know. They must have followed us. Kell must have — after the cross — he must have waited in the Hall—"

"I know."

"It was ours."

"I know, Mitya."

"Tell Petra." He stops. He breathes. "Tell Petra I tried. With the schedule. Tell her I asked." Another breath. Seven minutes. "And tell Pim—"

"What?"

He thinks about it. She can see him thinking about it, through the glass, with his eyes on hers. Then he laughs, a short breathless sound.

"Tell him the copper," he says. "The bar I lost. In the slosh. Tell him I'm sorry about the copper. He'll know." His hand tightens on her arm. "He was counting it for the clinic. He told me once. Forty-one the kilo."

She doesn't trust her voice. She nods, inside the helmet, so that he can see it.

"Now go," says Dmitri Saar. "Before you can't."

She goes.

That's the thing she'll never be able to tell anyone afterward, not Pim, not Quell, not anyone: that she went. That she took her hand off his arm, and pushed off from the deck beside him, and turned her lamp toward the service corridor, and went, while he still had six minutes of air and his eyes open, because he was right and she knew it. She went down the corridor kicking hard, and she didn't look back, and she didn't count.

At the corner, she stops. She can't help it. She turns her lamp back down the passage toward the vault.

He's still there, under the beam. His lamp is still shining up at the ceiling at its strange angle. Beside him the vault door stands in the dark with nine of its twelve dogs cut and Kell's spiker bolt still buried in the iron where it struck, and above it, in what's left of the red paint, the letters.

WHAT IS WRIT — — — OWED

He lifts one hand. Slowly. A small wave, the way you'd wave to someone getting on a ferry.

She takes the marker spray off her harness and puts it against the wall of the corridor beside her, at the corner, and draws a skull. Small and neat, the way they do it in the Gutter. Under it, she writes *M.S.*

Then she goes.

* * *

The sled is where they left it. The corridor holds. The Mouth is glowing faintly when she comes out of it, and the Hulk is turning beneath her as she clamps on — she can see it now, from outside, the new wrongness of it, the long body twisting very slightly against itself like a towel being wrung — and she runs the sled back to Ringfall with forty minutes of air and her hands shaking so badly she can barely hold the stick.

The shuttle dock is full.

Not of bailiffs. Of people. Hundreds of them, standing in rows on the dock floor between lines of black-coated bailiffs, with bags, with children, with nothing. Lot one. The defaulters of registration, and the people from the Gutter's eastern rows, and anyone else on the first satisfaction schedule whose name came up today. The *Lawful Remedy*'s shuttle is at the far berth with its ramp down, and a bailiff at the foot of the ramp with a slate is reading names, one at a time, in a pleasant voice, and the people whose names are read go up the ramp.

Nobody is fighting. That's what Yara will remember. Nobody is screaming. They're just going. A man with a bag over his shoulder. A woman holding a little girl by the hand. An old diver Yara knows by sight, with one arm, the one who laughed in front of the concourse screen the first day — *The Account? That's a story* — going up the ramp with his head down.

She doesn't stop. She goes along the side of the dock past them in her mother's unpainted shell with her helmet under her arm and the smell of the Hulk coming off her, and nobody looks at her, and she doesn't look at anybody, and she goes up the stair into the Gutter and along the concourse past the writs and down the row to her can.

The hatch is open.

She stands in front of it for a long time. Then she goes in.

The can is very neat. Someone has made the beds. Pim's blanket is folded at the foot of the top bunk, and his slate is on the shelf beside it, squared to the edge, and the brass seal-reader is on the galley shelf where she left it, and everything is exactly where it should be except Pim.

On the bottom bunk, on her pillow, there's a sheet of film.

It's printed in the Company's neat Charter letters, with the key-and-sword at the top.

*Pim Tull, minor, of the line of TULL, has today been admitted aboard the* Lawful Remedy *for medical assessment, as scheduled. He is comfortable and in the care of the Company's physicians. His condition is treatable.*

Under that, in a different hand — a real hand, a pen, a pleasant even script —

*The Factor would welcome a conversation at your earliest convenience. — A.K.*

She sits down on the bunk with the film in her hands. She sits there for a long time. Through the plating, very faint, she can hear the bailiff on the dock reading names. She can hear the air pump and the heater and the faint far vibration of Ringfall's hull. And under all of it, so low that she isn't sure she's hearing it at all, something else: a deep slow shudder, coming from very far away, from out in the dark beyond Tamar, where the Hulk is climbing toward apogee with its long body twisting and a man lying under a beam in front of a door with nine of its twelve dogs cut.

On the inside of the hatch, her mother's marks. Two hundred and nine, in fives.

She gets up. She finds the stylus Pim uses for his slate. She goes to the hatch and kneels in front of it, under her mother's marks, and for the first time in twelve years she begins to scratch her own. Not all of them. She doesn't know how many there are; she's never counted. Just one. One short line, under the two hundred and nine, cut deep into the paint.

Then a second line beside it, for the one she's going to make next.

Three passes.


# Chapter 16: The Fork

*The Crossing · CR 611*

The Fork was two stars that had been dying together for a billion years.

Annick saw them on the strongroom's little repeater screen as the ship came out of fold: a white dwarf, small and fierce, burning blue-white like the tip of a cutter, and close beside it a swollen red companion whose outer layers it was slowly drinking. Between them, a bridge of glowing gas. It was the only waypoint on the Long Lane with a name that wasn't a number or a person; the old navigators had called it the Fork because from here the Lane split, one branch running down to Shale and the other on to Absalom, and because, Benn had told her once, it looked like a thing you'd eat with. She had thought it was a joke. Looking at it now, the white star with its thread of red fire, she thought it looked like a wound.

*Fold complete. Coil cooling. Six hours to next jump. All hands stand down from fold stations.*

She did not stand down. She unstrapped herself from the purser's fold seat and checked the Book's straps, and then she sat down again and did not move.

The message from the Factor came in the first hour, on the purser's comm, in his soft voice. *Good morning, Purser. I hope the fold was easy. I'll come down at the end of the cool with the order, as we discussed. Five hours and fifty minutes. Do have some breakfast.*

She did not have breakfast. She sat with her back to the cold bulkhead and her knees drawn up and the key at her throat, and listened to the ship.

Ships have a sound. Every purser learns it. Not the hum of the ventilation or the tick of the coil, but under those, a kind of murmur made of everything at once — feet on decks, voices through bulkheads, hatches, pumps, the slow shift of water in the tanks — that tells you, if you've listened to it long enough, whether the ship is well. For eleven years the *Merciful Increase* had murmured at Annick like a sleeper. For the first four hours at the Fork she murmured the same. Then, at four hours and forty minutes, she began to hold her breath.

Annick heard it. She could not have said how. The murmur thinned. The feet on the decks above her stopped moving in their usual patterns. The ventilation went on, and the coil ticked, and the water in Tank Four shifted on the other side of the bulkhead; but the voices were gone, and the hatches had stopped, and there was a silence underneath everything, very faint, like the silence in a room where people are waiting for someone to speak.

At four hours and fifty-one minutes, the Hold began to scream.

* * *

It came through the bulkhead on her right, the one that divided the strongroom from the Hold, and it was not a human sound at first. It was the cradles. Every cradle in the Hold had an alarm, a small sharp two-note tone that sounded when its sleeper's temperature rose outside the cold-sleep band, and Annick had heard one or two of them go off in eleven years, briefly, before a tech came and set them right. Now they were all going. Hundreds of them. A vast shrill pulsing chorus that rose and rose as more of them joined, until it filled the spine and came through the iron into her teeth.

The fast thaw. They had talked about it in the hollow among the cradles, Marit and Tobiah and the cradle techs, in low voices. *It can be done, if you've someone who knows the systems and is willing to kill a few to wake the rest.* Four hundred sleepers, brought up from the cold in twenty minutes instead of the six hours the Charter's medical regulations required. Their hearts would race and stutter. Their blood would thicken. Some of them would fit, and some of them would wake screaming and not stop, and some of them would not wake at all. The rest would come out of their cradles into the blue cold of the Hold confused and naked and shaking, and the techs would put a tool in each of their hands and point them at the hatch.

She sat with her hands pressed flat against the deck and listened to it.

Under the alarms, after a while, there were voices. Shouting. Someone sobbing, close to the bulkhead, on and on. And then a new sound, which she had never heard on a ship in her life and recognised at once: the flat hard crack of a flechette carbine, firing in a closed space, and the long shriek of the needles striking iron.

The proctors had come down to the spine.

*Security. Security.* The ship's voice, everywhere. *Disturbance in the Hold. All proctors to the spine. All crew remain at stations.*

The carbines fired again. And again. Someone screamed — not the confused scream of the thawed; a scream with a name in it — and then there was a sound like a door slamming, very loud, and a roar of voices, many, coming from aft, and the carbines firing faster, and the voices coming on.

Annick got up. She went to the iron door of the strongroom and put both hands on it. Then she threw the bolts.

There were three, on the inside, massive steel tongues that slid into sockets in the frame. They had been put there by the Charter for exactly this — so that a purser might lock herself and the Book in, in the event of a ship being taken, and hold out until she could do what Article Nine required. She had never thrown them. They were stiff. She had to set her feet against the frame and pull with her whole body to move them, and they went home one by one with a sound like the Book's lid closing, deep and soft and final.

Then she went back to the cradle and opened the Book, and turned on the strongroom comm, and waited.

* * *

Kolar came at five hours and four minutes.

Annick heard her before she saw her: the sergeant's voice in the passage outside, flat and hard through her helmet grille, giving orders. Then a fist on the door, three blows.

"Purser."

Annick did not answer.

"Purser. Factor's orders. You and the Book are to come with me to the Factor's cabin for your own protection. Open the door."

"No," said Annick.

There was a pause. Through the iron she could hear the battle going on in the spine — farther off now, aft, toward the coil, and it seemed to her that the carbines were fewer and the voices more. Then Kolar said, quite calmly, "Cutter," to someone beside her, and a moment later the door began to tick.

It was a thermal lance. Annick knew the sound; she had heard the coil gang use them on the housing. A slow deep ticking as the iron took the heat, and then, very faintly, a smell — hot metal, coming through the seams of the door — and on the inside of the iron, in the dim strongroom lamplight, a small dull spot that slowly began to glow.

It was at the height of the middle bolt. They were cutting the bolt.

She watched it. There was nothing else to do. The spot glowed dull red, then brighter. She could feel its heat on her face from two metres away. The Book lay open in its cradle beside her with the page-glass awake and blue and the stylus clipped beside it, and her key was at her throat, and she had no idea at all, for perhaps the first time in her life, what she was going to write.

The comm spoke.

It was Szell. Not her voice on the ship's channel; her voice on the purser's line, the hard line that ran from the Lantern down the spine to the strongroom and nowhere else. Very quiet, very clear, a little out of breath.

"Purser. The Lantern is mine."

Annick put her hand on the bead of the key.

"Orme's on the command ring. The captain's—" a pause, a breath — "the captain's down. There were bonded on the bridge deck. We've got the doors. The Factor's proctors are all in the spine. I need you to seal me. Now. Forty seconds, Annick. Seal me and I'll lock the spine hatches and they're done."

Behind her, the spot on the door was turning orange.

She thought of the captain at his grandfather's table with the decanter two-thirds empty. *Aurelio would have done it. That's why he's dead.* She thought of two blank lines in a maintenance log. *Choose the one who can fly.* She thought of Tobiah in the hollow with his hand on Lise Saal's window. *And I'd still need her to fly.*

The captain was down. There was no one else.

She put her thumb to the bead.

"Entry," she said. Her voice was perfectly level. "Annick Vell, Purser of the *Merciful Increase*, holding the Key and the Book together. Under Article Fourteen, the Master's authority of this ship is vested in Nadia Szell, First Officer, without restriction or countersign. From this hour."

"Sealed," she said, and took her thumb away.

The page-glass flickered. Somewhere far off through the iron and the length of the spine, she felt it go — a faint shudder in the deck, a change in the note of the ventilation, as every lock in the Lantern dropped the old codes and began to search for the new. She counted. She could not help it. One. Two. Three.

At twenty-six the spot on the door went from orange to yellow.

At thirty-one the lance stopped.

She heard Kolar's voice in the passage, sharp, and another voice answering, and then the sergeant shouting something she couldn't make out. Boots. A hatch slamming. And then a new sound, aft, very loud — the great grinding boom of the spine's emergency bulkheads closing, one after another, sealing the spine into sections — and the voices in the passage outside the strongroom rising, confused, as the proctors understood that the doors behind them were shut.

At forty, the comm spoke again.

"Got it," said Nadia Szell. "Thank you, Purser. Stay where you are."

* * *

It was another hour before anyone came.

She sat in the strongroom with the bolts thrown and the Book open and listened to the end of it. It did not take long. The proctors were twenty-four, and the bulkheads had cut them into three groups, and the Lantern held the ship. On the other side of the iron the voices came and went: the proctors shouting at each other, then shouting at someone else, then the carbines firing in short bursts, and voices answering that were not proctors' voices, many of them, getting nearer. A long silence. Someone beating on a hatch. Then, very close outside the strongroom door, a sound she would remember for the rest of her life, though she never told anyone: a woman's voice, Kolar's, saying *no* — just that, *no*, twice, not loudly, in a tone of mild surprise — and then a number of other sounds, wet and heavy, and then nothing at all.

At six hours and twenty minutes, someone knocked on the door. Gently. Three times.

"Annick." It was Haddad. "It's over. You can open it."

She opened it. It took both hands and her feet against the frame for each bolt, and the middle one was so warped from the lance that she had to strike it with the strongroom's fire extinguisher to move it, and when she finally swung the door open the doctor was standing in the passage outside with blood on his hands to the elbows and on the front of his coat and in his hair, and his embarrassed face was grey.

"Dace," she said.

"Alive." He caught her arm before she could move. "Alive. A needle in the shoulder and one along the ribs. She lost a lot of blood. I've packed it. She's in the infirmary." He held on. "Annick. Joss Imre's dead."

She looked past him down the passage.

They had laid them along the wall. She did not count them; she would count them later, in the Book, every one. Bonded in dun, and bonded in nothing at all, naked from the cradles, their skin still grey and wet with gel. Proctors in grey. Two crew. At the near end, where the passage turned toward the Hold, Vashti Kolar lay on her back with her helmet off and her pale cropped hair stuck to her forehead, and her face like a fist was not like anything now. Someone had closed her eyes.

Joss Imre was a little way along. The boy with the knife scar. Haddad had saved the eye; she could see that. It was closed.

"There's a lot of them from the thaw," said Haddad quietly. "Forty-one. Their hearts. I couldn't—" He stopped. "There'll be more. Some of them are still fitting."

"Where's Tobiah?"

"Lantern. They all are. Ruck and the First Officer and the rest." He looked at her. "Master Szell sent word. She wants the Purser and the Book in the captain's day room."

"The captain."

"Dead. On the bridge deck. Shot." Haddad wiped his hands on his coat, uselessly. "There were bonded up there. They came through the forward spoke while Szell's people were taking the Lantern. There was fighting on the deck outside the day room. He came out to stop it." He did not look at her. "That's what they're saying."

"And the Factor?"

"Alive. They took him in his cabin. He didn't resist." The doctor's mouth moved. "He was dressed for it. He had his good coat on."

* * *

She carried the Book to the Lantern herself.

She would not let anyone else touch it. Two bonded with carbines took from proctors fell in on either side of her at the spoke — young men she did not know, still shaking, who looked at her with a kind of awe — and she let them, and they went forward up the spine past the bodies and the scorched bulkheads and the great Hold hatch, where the alarms were silent now and the cradle techs were moving among the racks with lamps, slowly, checking the ones that had not woken. She held the Book against her chest in both arms. In zero gravity it had no weight. It had never felt so heavy.

The forward hub was crowded. Bonded and crew together, in a strange uneasy mixture, standing about the passage outside the Lantern with weapons and without them, talking low; and as Annick came through with the Book in her arms they fell quiet and made room for her, and she heard someone say *the Purser*, and someone else say *she sealed it, she held the door and sealed it*, and a hand touched her shoulder as she passed, briefly, the way you touch someone for luck.

The day room door was half open. She stopped outside it to shift the Book in her arms. And through the gap, before she had decided to listen, she heard Edwin Quarry say:

"— need a legal basis. That's all I'm telling you. You can't simply announce it."

She stood very still.

"The patent," said Quarry's soft voice. "That's the whole of it. The Charter's patent for the Absalom system is recorded in the Book. At Landfall it vests in the colony — in whoever the Book names as holding it for the colony. If you want to hold this world, Master Szell, you need the Purser to record a council as trustee of that patent. *The Founders*, call it. Something with a ring. You and Mr Ruck and whoever else you need to keep happy."

"And the debts?" Szell's voice.

"Burn them." Quarry, pleasantly. "Burn the Book. Publicly, at Landfall, in front of everyone. They'll weep. They'll worship you for it. You'll never in your life have a better day." A pause; the clink of glass. "And then, when they've stopped weeping, you raise an account. Not the Charter's debts. Nothing like it — you're merciful; you've burned all that. Something smaller. Something that sounds fair. What each of them owes for the ship that carried them, and the air, and the tools, and the stores, and the work of the people who'll keep them alive on that ice for the next fifty years. You'll need it. You'll need some way of deciding who works and who eats, and nothing works like a debt. Call it something they can love." He seemed to think. "Liberty. Call it Liberty. *What do we owe? We owe our Liberty.*"

There was a silence.

"They'll never accept a debt," said Tobiah Ruck.

It was the first time she had heard his voice since the hollow. It was very tired.

"They'll accept anything after a fire," said Quarry. "Trust me. I've been doing this for thirty years."

The silence went on. Annick stood outside the door with the Book against her chest and did not breathe.

"Somebody's got to pay for the air," said Tobiah at last, quietly. Not to anyone. As if to himself. "That's the trouble. That's always been the trouble. She said it to me — the Purser. *How will you feed them? Who'll decide?* I told her we'd work it out." A long breath. "Somebody's got to pay for the air."

"Then we're agreed," said Nadia Szell.

"And the Purser?" said Tobiah.

"She'll seal what you put in front of her," said Quarry. "She sealed the Master for you today, didn't she? She's very good. She gave me Osei for her sister, without a tremor. She'll give you this."

"Leave her sister out of it."

"I'm only telling you what she is."

"I know what she is," said Tobiah Ruck. His voice did not rise. "I've known since the brig. Leave her sister out of it."

Another pause. Then Szell, lightly, the way she had said *I liked Benn* in the Lantern: "We'll need her willing. Not frightened. Frightened clerks make mistakes. The old man would never have sealed any of it, Edwin, you know that. He'd have sat in this room and said no until we were all dead of old age. I made sure he'd never be asked."

"I wondered," said Quarry softly.

"Did you."

"I wondered why you were so very sure, at Pell, that the new purser would be easier." The clink of glass again. "I'm grateful. It's made my life a great deal simpler."

Tobiah said nothing. Annick waited for him to say something. She waited for what felt like a very long time, outside the half-open door, with the hub murmuring behind her and the Book's iron edge pressing into her arms. He did not say anything at all.

"And Lindqvist," said Quarry.

"Caspar did it cleanly," said Szell. "On the deck outside. There were bonded right there — they'd come through the spoke. Nobody will ever think anything else." A pause. "He'd have handed them all to the Receivers' court with an apology. I told the Purser so."

* * *

She went in.

She did not knock. She pushed the door open with her shoulder and went in with the Book in her arms, and they all turned.

Szell was in the captain's chair at the head of his grandfather's table, in her First Officer's coat with the collar open and a smear of something dark along her jaw. Quarry sat at her right hand in his good coat, unbound, with a glass of the captain's whisky in front of him and his soft hands folded. Tobiah stood at the viewport, where Szell had stood the day Annick was made purser, enormous against the white star and its thread of red fire, with a proctor's carbine slung over his shoulder and blood on his sleeve. There were two others she did not know well — a bonded woman from the coil gang and Lieutenant Orme, a lean fair officer with very still hands — and there was the decanter, on the table, nearly empty, in the ring it had left on the wood.

"Purser," said Nadia Szell, and stood, and smiled her warm surprising smile. "Well done."

Tobiah crossed the room in two strides and put his arms round her.

He did it carefully, as he did everything, so as not to crowd; he put his great arms round her and the Book together and held them, briefly, and she felt his chin rest for a moment on the top of her head. He smelled of blood and cordite and the cold of the Hold. "You held the door," he said into her hair. "Haddad told me. You held it with the lance coming through and you sealed it. Thank you. Thank you."

She said nothing.

He let her go. He looked down at her, and his broken face was wet, and she could not tell — she would never be able to tell, not then and not in all the years afterward — whether it was for the dead in the passage, or for her, or for what he had just agreed to on the other side of the door.

"Sit down, Annick," said Szell. "You must be exhausted. Edwin, pour the Purser a drink."

Quarry poured. He held the glass out to her across the table with a small polite inclination of his head, the way he had held out the film with the Receivers' order on the day she was made purser, and his pricing eyes went over her face, and she saw him see that she had heard.

He said nothing. He only smiled very slightly and set the glass down in front of her chair.

She did not sit. She set the Book on the captain's grandfather's table, beside the decanter, in a patch of white light from the dying star, and stood back from it.

"We'll jump for Absalom as soon as the coil allows," said Szell. "Six hours. The Absalom branch is two jumps; three weeks, perhaps less. And then we'll be home." She lifted her own glass. "There's a great deal to decide. We'll need you, Purser. For all of it. There's no one else who knows the Book."

"No," said Annick. "There isn't."

Szell looked at her for a moment with her dark considering eyes. Then she nodded, as if a reading had come back as she expected, and drank.

Outside the viewport, very slowly, the Fork began to turn. Annick felt it through the deck: the thrusters firing, the long body of the ship coming about on its axis, away from the branch that ran down to Shale. The white star and its red companion slid across the glass, and out of it, and were gone, and there was nothing beyond the viewport but the thin stars of the Long Lane and, somewhere ahead of them, too far to see, a gas giant and a moon made of ice.

Annick stood by the table with her hands at her sides and the key lying cold at her throat. She did not drink. She looked at the Book in its patch of light, and at the people round it, and at the ring the captain's decanter had left on his grandfather's wood. And in her head, where she had kept her other accounts for eleven years, she began without meaning to on a new page; and found, for the first time in her life, that she knew exactly what she was going to write on it, and that there was nobody in the world she could tell.


# Chapter 17: Burning Day

*The Account · CR 929*

I went up to the Hulk on the Burning in a clean grey coat that wasn't mine.

Mags found it in the laundry. An Assessor's long coat, sent down from the Office the week before for cleaning, with a burn on one cuff where its owner had leaned on a lamp, and a name stitched inside the collar that I didn't know. It fitted me badly across the shoulders. I put my own pin on it — I'd had it in my trouser pocket since the night in the Sump, and nobody had thought to take it off me, because nobody had thought I'd want it — and Mags looked at me and said I looked like an Assessor, God help me, and that was the idea.

The pilgrim ferries started at five in the morning. Every one of them full: forty seats and two hundred people standing, packed in so close that you didn't need to hold the rails. Gallows Reach families, and refinery families, and people from the Sump in their best coats with their children on their shoulders; old women with bowls of ash wrapped in cloth on their laps, to mark the wrists of the ones who couldn't afford the Office's; ice miners still grey from the shift, who'd come straight from the galleries without washing. It's the one day in the year the ballast of Tamar goes up to the Hulk in its thousands, to stand in the Old Hold among the cradles of the Crossing and remember. The Houses encourage it. It's good for the Mercy.

Nobody looked at me. On the Burning, an Assessor's grey is just a colour. I stood among them in the hot crush with my hand inside the borrowed coat on the papers against my chest, and across the cabin, wedged against the forward bulkhead with a cloth bundle in her arms, Calla Dray stood looking at nothing, and didn't look at me either.

There were forty-one of hers on that ferry. I'd counted them as they boarded. I'd stopped counting at the dock, because there'd been forty-one on the last ferry too, and there would be on the next.

* * *

Bastian was in the Old Hold.

I'd known he would be. Every junior Assessor in the Office works the Burning — marshalling the crowds in the Old Hold, keeping the lines moving past the ash bowls, making sure nobody climbs the cradle racks or scratches their name on a shell where the Speaker will see it on her walk-through. It's a junior's job. I'd done it myself for nine years. And Bastian Oduya had worked the Burning since he was twenty, and was proud of it, and had told me so, more than once, over tea.

The Old Hold on the Burning is a thing I can't describe to you properly, and I've tried. A cylinder two hundred metres long and sixty across, racked from end to end with empty cradles — white shells the shape of coffins, their lids standing open, their windows dark, rising in tiers on a lattice of black struts in every direction until they're lost in a blue dimness the lamps can't reach. In the Founders' time they were full. Three thousand six hundred of them. The Houses keep them as they were, cleaned and polished, every lid open, every window clear; and on the Burning the people of Tamar come up in their thousands and float among them in the blue cold, and touch them, and look through the windows at nothing, and mark their wrists with ash. It's very quiet. Even with ten thousand people in it, it's very quiet. People whisper in the Old Hold the way they whisper in a room where someone is sleeping.

He was at the third ash station, on the aft rack, in his grey coat with a slate in one hand, directing the line. I came up behind him through the crowd. Teodor Rask and two cistern men came up on either side. I put my hand on his shoulder, the way Marsh used to put his on mine, and said, "Bastian."

He turned. He saw my face. And I watched him understand it — all of it, in about a second and a half — and go white, and open his mouth, and not say anything.

"Come with me," I said. "Quietly. Please."

He came.

There's a cradle bay on the aft rack, three tiers up, where a section of the lattice was damaged in a fire a century ago and never repaired, and the Office roped it off: a hollow among the cradles, out of sight of the main lines. We took him there. The cistern men held his arms — not hard; he wasn't fighting — and Teodor stood at the edge of the hollow and watched the crowd below, and I floated in front of Bastian Oduya among the open lids with my back to an empty cradle and looked at him.

"Ten past ten," I said. "You went into the Index at ten past ten. You came out at half past eleven with your gloves in your hand, holding them by the cuffs. Mr Rask saw you."

Bastian looked at Teodor's soft white hands. Then at me.

"I didn't—" he said.

"Don't."

He stopped. His mouth worked. He was twenty-four, and he looked about fifteen, and there were tears standing in his eyes — not falling; in zero gravity they don't fall, they collect, and his eyes were brimming with them, swimming, so that he had to blink and blink to see me at all.

"He said she was going to give the Hold a weapon," he said. It came out all at once, high and fast, as if it had been waiting behind his teeth for nine days. "The Chief. He called me in. He said Keeper Brask had found something in the archives that would start a war — that the Hold would use it to rise, and thousands would die, and the Concordance would come in with guns. He said she was old and she didn't understand what she was doing. He said I only had to get her papers back. Just the papers. He said she trusted me; she'd let me in." He swallowed. "She did. She was waiting in aisle nine. She had her glasses off and her gloves on. She thought I was — I don't know who she thought I was. She looked so surprised."

"And?"

"She wouldn't give them to me. She said she'd already sent them. She laughed. She said, *You're too late, dear, and you'll never find them, and I'm very sorry the Chief has made you do this.*" He was shaking. "And I — I took her by the arms. Just to frighten her. That's all he said, Sir, *frighten her*. And she said *Bastian, let go*, very quietly, like you'd say it to a child, and I—"

He stopped. He couldn't go on. The cistern men held his arms and looked at the cradles.

"He told me what to do after," said Bastian at last, very low. "The paint. The words. I'd never seen them on a wall. I'd only seen them on the feed. He told me to put the pen in your rooms when the proctors searched. He said — he said if you were arrested, your girl would be taken into custody, and she'd be safe there. He said if you weren't, the Speaker's guard would deal with it themselves. He said—" He looked at me with his brimming eyes. "He said it was the only way to keep her safe. Sir. I believed him. I went to see her, to — to make sure she was real. To see her birthday was right. I don't know why. I'm sorry. I'm so sorry."

"The man in the spoke," I said.

"I didn't know about that. I swear I didn't. Not till after." He was crying properly now, if you can call it crying in zero gravity: the tears coming away from his eyes in little trembling globes that drifted off among the cradles. "I lent you the money. Do you remember? For her portion. I gave you eighty. I wanted — I wanted—"

"I remember," I said.

I'd taken out my handset at the start. I'd had it in my hand, in the coat pocket, with the recorder running, since *ten past ten*. I took it out now and held it up so he could see it, and I watched him look at it and understand that, too.

"Will you swear to it?" I said. "Before the Verifier. Under your name."

He stared at me. Then, slowly, he nodded. "Yes. Yes. I want to. I want to, Sir, I've wanted to every day—"

"Today," I said. "In the Hall. When I tell you."

"Yes."

I believed him. I want you to know that. I looked at his face, the face of a boy from a steward's family in House Temperance who'd wanted all his life to be useful, and I believed him absolutely, and I nodded to the cistern men, and they let go of his arms.

He went.

Not fast. He didn't run; you can't run in zero gravity. He simply pushed off from the cradle behind him, very hard, with both feet, and went out of the hollow between Teodor and the nearest strut, head first, like a diver, down into the crowd below. Ten thousand people in the blue dimness. Grey coats and ballast coats and children on shoulders and old women with bowls of ash. I saw him for perhaps two seconds, a grey shape going down through the lattice among the open lids. Then he was gone, and there was only the crowd, whispering.

"Let him go," I said to Teodor, who'd turned to go after him.

"He'll tell them."

"He'll tell no one. He's going to find somewhere very small to hide and stay there for the rest of his life." I put the handset back in my pocket. I found my hand was steady. "I've got it. I've got it under his voice. That'll have to do."

* * *

I should have gone straight to the Hall then. Calla's people were waiting. Thorne was waiting, though he didn't know it. The signing was in an hour.

I went to Marsh.

I've told you why I went the first time, and I couldn't make it make sense. I can't now either. Except that this time I knew. I'd known since the fig tree, and I'd known since the spoke, and I'd heard it in Bastian's voice ten minutes before with my own handset recording. I didn't go to Marsh to be told it wasn't true. I went because I'd been taking things to him for twenty-nine years, and I wanted to take him this. I wanted to put it on his desk in front of him like a page of sums and watch him look at it.

His office door was open. He was alone.

He was misting the juniper. He floated by the glass sphere in his grey coat with the Chief's chain of brass links round his neck, holding the little brass bulb, and he was squeezing it, gently, a puff at a time, so that a fine mist drifted into the sphere through its valve and settled on the twisted branches that went every way at once. He didn't turn round when I came in.

"I thought you might come," he said.

I shut the door behind me. I had my sidearm. I didn't take it out.

"Bastian," I said.

"Yes." He misted. "He told me you'd taken him. Not in words; he hasn't got the courage for words. He sent me a message on my handset five minutes ago. It said *sorry*. That was all." He set the bulb down on its magnetic shelf. "Poor boy. He'll have gone to ground somewhere. I hope he stays there."

"You told him to kill her."

"I told him to get her papers back." Marsh turned round. He looked old, and tired, and entirely calm. "No. That's a lie, and I'm too old to tell you lies, Hollis; you were always too good at hearing them. I knew what he'd do. I've always known what people will do. It's the only gift I've ever had. I saw it in you in the dirt outside that schoolroom in Gallows Reach — that you'd do anything at all to be lifted, and that you'd spend the rest of your life paying for it, and that you'd be very, very good at your job." He smiled a little. "I was right about you. I was right about Bastian. I'm sorry."

"She was your friend."

"She was more than that, once. A long time ago." He looked at the juniper. "She'd have started a war. You know she would. The Hold with her papers in its hand, on the Burning, with the Concordance squadron sitting in orbit. Thousands dead. Tens of thousands. And at the end of it the Concordance would come down and take the system anyway — by force, under a military governor, and the people of Tamar would be shipped off to Ostrava with nothing, and the Houses with them, and nobody would get anything at all." He turned back to me. "The Sale is the soft way, Hollis. The only soft way there is. The Houses take the price and the peerage and go, and the Concordance takes the system without a shot, and the people go to Ostrava under contract — hard contracts, yes, long ones, but contracts, with terms, with an end. It's not good. I'm not telling you it's good. I'm telling you it's the least bad thing on the table. Ottilie couldn't see that. She saw the founding page and nothing else."

"The founding page is a forgery."

"Yes," said Marsh. "Of course it is."

He said it the way you'd say the time.

"Every Chief Assessor is told," he said. "On the day of his appointment, in the Speaker's rooms, privately. I was told nineteen years ago by Speaker Constance's father. The Account was written in the ninth year after Landfall, in Tamar water, under the Council's seal, because the people had begun to ask what they owed and why, and the Founders needed something written down to show them. They wrote what they wanted to be true and sealed it, and then it was." He spread his hands. "Merriam doesn't know. Most of the Keepers don't. Ottilie found it on her own, by asking the rings. She always was the best of us."

"And you've known for nineteen years."

"I've known for nineteen years." He drifted to his desk and put one hand on the brown teapot, as if for warmth. "Every ledger is a forgery, Hollis. Every one. That's what Ottilie never understood, and what you've never let yourself understand, though you've known it since you were twelve. Someone decides what happened — who owes, who's owed, what it cost — and writes it in a book and seals it, and then it's true. Not because it happened. Because it's written. The Charter's Book was a forgery too: a debt for a passage nobody asked to take, at a price nobody could pay. The Account is a forgery of a forgery. Whatever comes after will be the same. The only question there's ever been is who holds the pen."

I stood by the door with my hand on the sidearm in my coat and didn't say anything. I'd come to put something on his desk in front of him and watch him look at it. He'd looked at it. He'd looked at it nineteen years ago.

"There's one other thing," said Lucan Marsh. "Then I'll let you go and do whatever it is you've come up here to do. She found something else. In the old Charter manifests — the voyage papers of the *Merciful Increase*, in the dead stacks, that nobody's read since Landfall." He looked at me. "A ship's purser carries a key. Did you know that? Two keys, on a Charter ship. The manifests list them. Key number one, the master, broken at the death of the old purser during the Crossing and committed with his body, it says. And key number four-four-seven, the assistant's. The last line beside it says only *in the Purser's keeping*." He paused. "Never recovered."

I didn't move.

"Ottilie remembered a boy of twelve," said Marsh softly, "frightened of everything in the Hulk, showing her an old iron key on a chain his mother had given him. She remembered he'd said it didn't open anything. She remembered the number on the shank, because she remembered every number she ever saw; she was like you that way." He held out his hand. "May I?"

I took it out. I don't know why. I took the chain from round my neck and held the key up in the lamplight between us, the long iron key with its notched bit and its dull bead in the bow, that had hung against my breastbone for twenty-nine years; and I turned it, and looked at the shank, where generations of thumbs had worn the iron smooth; and there, so faint I'd never once in my life thought to look for it, were four small stamped figures.

0447.

*D-0447.* The card in her shoe. The dead account she'd hidden her notes behind. Not a portion number. A message. To a boy who'd shown her his mother's key.

"She thought it might open the purser's box," said Marsh. "The little safe in the vault, under the plaque. The one nobody's opened in three hundred years. She thought — I don't know what she thought. She was a romantic. Some dead woman's letters, I expect. A will. A bottle of something." He was still holding out his hand. "Give it to me, Hollis."

"No."

"Give it to me." Very gently. "It doesn't matter now. It never did. Whatever's in that box is three hundred years too late for anyone."

"No," I said.

He looked at me for a moment longer. Then he sighed, and let his hand fall, and touched something on his desk.

The door opened behind me. There were four of them — Mercy Guard, in dress blue for the Burning, with carbines. They'd been waiting in the passage. Of course they had.

"I'm sorry," said Lucan Marsh. "I'm going to let you watch. I think you should. Ottilie used to say that to you, didn't she? When you were a boy. *You should know what you're serving.*" He nodded to the guards. "His sidearm, please. And the key. Gently. It was his mother's."

They took the sidearm. They took the key. One of them held it out to Marsh on its chain, and he took it, and looked at it in his palm for a long moment, and put it in the pocket of his coat.

They didn't find the papers, or the wafer, or the handset. They didn't look. They were Mercy Guard, not proctors; nobody had told them to.

* * *

The Hall of the Account on the Burning is the most beautiful thing in the system, and I'd seen it every year of my life, and I'd never seen it from where I saw it then: on the forward gallery, under the Lantern stair, with my hands cuffed in front of me and two Mercy Guardsmen holding my arms.

Below me the great iron cavern was full. Twenty thousand people, the Office said afterward, though I don't know how they counted. The ballast of Tamar floating shoulder to shoulder in the zero gravity, in their best coats, with their children; filling the Hall from the deck to the ribs, from the bow hatch to the stern. And among them and over them, let down from the ribs on cords, the banners. Long strips of grey silk, ash-coloured, hundreds of them, swaying very slightly in the current of the ventilation, so that the whole Hall seemed to be full of slow grey smoke.

At the centre, on its disc of scorched black iron, the Ash Case in its column of glass.

Before it, a dais. On the dais, a table of black wood, and at the table, the heads of the Seven Houses in their plain unbleached linen. Severine Constance at the centre, silver-haired, grey-eyed, perfectly still. Beside her, in white, a tall woman I didn't know, with the stylised mountain of the Ostrava Labor Trust on her collar. On the far side, apart, in plain black with his document case open in front of him, Isaak Thorne. Behind the Houses, in a row, the Office in grey: Merriam touching her chain, the Keepers, the senior Assessors. And Lucan Marsh, at the end of the row, with his hands folded in front of him and the key in his pocket.

The Speaker rose and read the Mercy.

I won't give you all of it. You know it, or your grandparents did. It's the long form of the Catechism, the version the Houses read on the Burning: how the Increase carried us in the dark, and the Purser would have sold us, and the Founders saved us, and burned the Book before all the people on this very spot, and asked only for our Liberty in return. Severine Constance read it beautifully. She had a low, clear, carrying voice, and she didn't hurry, and twenty thousand people floated in the grey smoke of the banners and said the responses with her, the way we'd said them in the schoolroom, in the same singsong, with the same little hitch before the hard answers.

*What was her name?*

*Her name was Annick Vell, and it is written in ash.*

I looked for Wren.

The House wards were in the side gallery, opposite me, across the Hall: the children of the Houses and their clients, and the guests, in white, in a long row behind a brass rail. Sixty of them. I went along them face by face. I knew I'd know her. I'd have known her through ice.

She was eleventh from the end. In a white dress that wasn't hers, too big at the shoulders, with her hair pinned up the way the Crown pins it, and her hands on the rail. She wasn't saying the responses. She was looking at the crowd — searching it, the way I'd searched the wards, face by face — and as I watched, her eyes came up from the crowd to the forward gallery, and along it, and found me.

She saw the cuffs. I saw her see them.

I didn't do anything. I couldn't. I looked at her across the whole width of the Hall of the Account, across twenty thousand people and the banners and the Ash, and I moved my lips without any sound at all. One word.

*Door.*

I don't know if she saw it. I don't know if she'd read the towel. She looked at me for a long moment with her mother's face and mine. Then she turned her head — very slowly, as if she were only looking at something along the gallery — and looked at the small service door at the end of the wards' rail, eleven places away. And she took her hands off the rail, and began, very slowly, a little at a time, as if she were only shifting to see better, to move toward it.

*And the Book was burned.*

The Speaker sat. Thorne stood.

He didn't say much. He never did. He opened his document case and took out a slate and read from it in his planed-smooth timetable voice: that the Bureau of Titles of the Concordance of Worlds had been asked to verify the title of the Houses of Absalom to the instrument known as the Account of Liberty, for the purposes of its conveyance to the Ostrava Labor Trust under the Concordance labour code. That the Bureau had examined the instrument. That the Bureau would now state its finding.

He looked up. His crystal eye went across the Hall — swept it, end to end, the way it had swept the dock the day he arrived — and stopped, for no more than a second, on the forward gallery. On me.

I don't know what he would have said. I've thought about it a great deal. I've thought about it more than about almost anything else.

He never said it. Because somewhere in the crowd below, on the deck near the bow hatch, a man shouted.

I didn't see him. I heard him: one voice, hoarse, a miner's voice, from the middle of twenty thousand. Three words. *We were cargo!* — not ice-script; you can't shout in ice-script; but you could hear the galleries in it — and then a single flat crack, very loud under the iron ribs, and the voice stopped.

A Mercy Guardsman on the bow gallery had fired into the crowd.

For one second, perhaps two, nothing happened at all. Twenty thousand people floated in the grey smoke of the banners in perfect silence, and the Speaker sat frozen at her table, and Thorne stood with his slate in his hand and his crystal eye on the bow, and I heard, from a long way off, a child begin to cry.

Then the Hall roared.

It came from everywhere at once. Not a shout; a sound like the Hulk itself makes in the deep night, a vast iron groan, rising. The crowd moved. All of it, at once, the way water moves when a hatch blows — toward the bow, toward the shot, away from it, every direction — and in the movement I saw them: the cloth bundles opening, the pipes and the coil-gang cutters and the ugly home-made guns coming out of them, forty-one on every ferry, Calla's people, rising out of the crowd all over the Hall with their faces set.
