The Mercy Guard on the galleries fired again. Something exploded near the stern hatch, a flat hard thud that I felt in my teeth. The banners were swinging wildly now, tearing loose from their cords and drifting, grey silk turning slowly in the air over the crowd like smoke from a fire that hadn't started yet.

The guards on my arms let go of me and unslung their carbines.

I looked for Wren. The wards' gallery was a confusion of white dresses, children screaming, House servants pulling at arms. I couldn't see her. I couldn't see the door.

On the dais, Lucan Marsh had turned, very calmly, and was moving away from the table toward the aft hatch — the one that leads down the spine past the Index toward the Office and the vault — with one hand in his coat pocket.

And then the lights went out.

All of them. Every lamp in the Hall, every panel, every screen. It was so sudden and so complete that for a moment there was no sound at all, only the dark; and then, in the dark, twenty thousand people began to scream.


# Chapter 18: The Empty Box

*The Salvage · CR 1317*

The inside of the *Lawful Remedy* is white and quiet and smells of nothing at all.

Yara has never been anywhere that smelled of nothing. Ringfall smells of scorched metal and fish sauce and people; the Hulk smells, through the shell's filters, of rust and cold; even the Ash Church smells faintly of lamp oil and old ice. The *Remedy* smells the way the inside of a new shell smells for the first hour, before you've sweated in it: clean, filtered, empty. The corridors are wide and softly lit and lined with a pale material that gives a little under her hand. There's no sound but the air. The bailiff who brought her up on the shuttle walks a step ahead of her, unhurried, his long black coat whispering, and doesn't speak.

They pass a long window. Not to the outside — to a room. It runs the length of the corridor, thirty metres, and on the other side of the glass there are bunks, in tiers, six high, very neat, and on the bunks are people in grey. Hundreds. Sitting, lying, talking low. A woman braiding a girl's hair. A man with one arm, lying on his back with his eyes open, looking at the bunk above him. Nobody's crying. Nobody's doing anything. They look like people waiting for a ferry that's late.

"Satisfaction hold," says the bailiff, without turning his head. "Lot one. Very comfortable. Through here, please."

* * *

Pim is in the medbay.

It's a white room with a white bed, and he's sitting up in it in a white gown with a clear mask over his nose and mouth, and a thin tube from the mask to a machine beside the bed that hums. On the machine's screen there's a picture of two shapes like grey trees, slowly turning, and inside the trees, like weather, patches of darker grey that are slowly — very slowly, as she watches — getting paler.

"Yara." His voice through the mask is muffled. And strong. She notices that before anything. "They let you come."

"They asked me."

"Look." He points at the screen. "That's me. My lungs. That's the scarring. It's cleaning it out, a bit at a time. Doctor Hallam says six more days and it'll be all gone." He takes a breath — a long one, deep, all the way down, the kind of breath she hasn't heard him take since he was ten — and lets it out, and laughs. "It doesn't hurt. I can breathe, Yara. Listen. I can just *breathe*."

She sits down on the edge of the bed. She takes his hand. It's warm. It's never warm.

"Mitya," says Pim, and stops. "They told me. The Factor came and told me himself. He said it was very brave." He looks at her over the mask. "Was it?"

"Yes."

"He said Mitya asked them to move Petra's family down the schedule. And they did it. As a mark of respect." His eyes are very steady. "Did he? Ask them?"

"Yes."

Pim nods slowly. He's quiet for a while. Then he says, "Did he say anything? At the end?"

"He said he was sorry about the copper. The bar he lost. In the slosh." She has to stop. "He said you'd know."

Pim looks at her for a long moment. Then he closes his eyes, and turns his face away toward the humming machine and the grey trees on its screen, and his hand in hers holds on very hard.

* * *

The Factor's office is the only room on the ship with a window to the outside.

Through it, Tamar is turning, white and grey and cracked like an old plate; and beyond Tamar, filling the lower half of the glass, rust and cream, banded, enormous, Absalom. Somewhere against the face of it, too small to see from here, the Hulk is climbing toward apogee for the second-to-last time.

Aurel Kade stands at the window with his hands behind his back.

"My condolences," he says, turning. "Mr Saar was a brave man. I understand he died trying to save you." He gestures to a chair. "Please."

She doesn't sit. Sabeth Orrin is standing by the door, very still, in her good grey wool, with her hands folded. She doesn't look at Yara. She looks at a point on the wall about a metre to the left.

"You've seen your brother," says Kade. "He's doing very well. Doctor Hallam is very pleased with him. Six days and his lungs will be as clean as mine." He smiles a little. "That's not a threat, Miss Tull. I want to be clear. That's simply the case. The Company treats its assets. It's in the writ."

"And after six days?"

"After six days his medical satisfaction is complete, and he's scheduled for transport with the next lot. The Company has contracts on eleven worlds. He's young; he'll be placed somewhere with good terms. Light industry, perhaps. Clerical. He reads, I'm told." Kade sits on the edge of his desk. "Or."

He lets it hang. She's heard Sabeth do it. She wonders, distantly, if they learned it from each other or from someone else.

"Or you bring me what's in the vault," says Aurel Kade. "Unopened. Whatever it is. And when you do, your brother's treatment is completed at the Company's expense, and the Company executes a deed of release for the line of TULL — the whole line, Miss Tull, the whole four million, discharged — and you and your brother are given passage to Vey on the next ship out, with Mrs Orrin's fifty thousand marks to start you." He spreads his hands. "Vey's a good world. Clean air. You'd never have to dive again."

She looks at him. At the plain grey suit and the straight back and the calm attentive tired face. At Sabeth by the door, looking at the wall.

"You know I found the door," she says.

"I know Mr Saar cut nine of its twelve bolts before he died. Mr Amory of the Warden's crew was very forthcoming." Kade's face doesn't change. "I know there's one pass left before the Assembly's engineers expect the Hulk to break up, and two at the very most. I know that you're the only diver alive who knows the clear way in. And I know that whatever's on the other side of that door has been waiting there for nearly four hundred years, and might be nothing at all — a dead man's papers, a ledger, some gambling debts — and that I'm offering you your brother's life and your own freedom for it, sight unseen." He tilts his head. "You see, I'm not asking you to do anything wrong. I'm asking you to bring me something you haven't seen."

"And burn it."

"And let me decide what to do with it. Yes." He stands up. "You don't have to answer now. You have until the end of the next window. If you come back from it without the cache, or if what's in the vault turns up anywhere but in this room — before the Assembly, before the Clerk, on the band — then I'm afraid your brother's schedule proceeds as written, and I'll have no choice in the matter. Nor will he." He walks her to the door. "I'm sorry, Miss Tull. Truly. It's a very hard thing to be asked."

In the corridor outside, a man is coming the other way. Narrow, pale, in a high-collared grey robe that falls straight to the floor, with a bead of dull crystal on a chain on his chest and a slate in his hand. Ansel Rhee, the Clerk-Notary of the Chancery of Vey, walking slowly, writing as he walks.

He looks up as she passes. His pale eyes rest on her face for a moment, without any expression at all. Then he nods, once, very slightly — the way you'd nod to someone whose name you'd written down — and goes on along the white corridor, writing.

* * *

Mireille Dunn and Mother Quell are waiting for her at the can.

They're sitting on the step outside the hatch, side by side, the small sharp advocate in her too-big coat and the broad old priest in her grey robe and her diver's harness belt, and they stand up when they see her coming along the hull.

"No," says Yara.

"You haven't heard what we—"

"I know what you're going to say. You're going to say it's everyone. If it's what Kade's afraid of, it's everyone. Four hundred thousand. The noodle man. Petra's kids. Mitya's dead for it, so I owe it to him." She stops at the hatch. "Pim's on that ship. He can breathe. I sat on his bed and held his hand and it was warm."

Dunn says nothing. Quell looks at her with the one good eye.

"Your mother," says Quell.

"*Don't.*"

The old woman is quiet for a moment. Then she nods.

"All right," she says. "I won't." She reaches into the pocket of her robe and takes out something — a short coil of old cable, braided copper in a cracked grey sheath, with a brass fitting at each end — and holds it out. "Then take this. For your box."

Yara doesn't take it. "What is it?"

"A lead. Off the bell. There's a socket on the side of your seal-reader, under the coil — I saw it when you came to the church. This fits it. The other end goes into any suit's band jack." Quell is still holding it out. "If you're ever in there and you need to talk to me — to anyone — put your box on the band through your suit, and the bell will hear it. The array sends what you give it. I told you." Her good eye is very steady. "That's all. I'm not asking you for anything. I'm only giving you a way to be heard, if you want one. Your mother would have—" She stops herself. "Take it."

Yara takes it. She doesn't know why. She puts it in the pocket of her jacket, and she goes into the can, and she shuts the hatch on both of them.

* * *

Two passes. She goes in alone.

The Hulk at the front of the window is barely the Hulk any more. Yara sees it from the sled, coming in, and for a moment she doesn't recognise it. The long iron body has twisted on itself along its whole length — not much, a few degrees, but enough that the lines of it are wrong, like a face seen in a bent mirror. The stump of the ring is gone entirely. The leading face is a black glassy scab from bow to stern. And the plumes from its trailing side aren't plumes any more but a single continuous cloud, white, glittering, streaming away behind it into the dark like the tail of a comet, so that the whole Hulk seems to be moving inside its own fog.

The Mouth is still there. She goes in.

Down the ring to the forward spoke. Down the spoke to the spine, with the water so warm now she can feel it through the shell like a bath. Through the Hall, where the rotted banners have mostly come down and lie in drifts on the deck like grey leaves, and the empty column on its scorched disc has tipped over and lies on its side. Down the long service passage aft, past STORES and HOLD AFT, slowly, with her lamp on the walls; and at the corner, the skull. Small and neat. *M.S.*

She turns the corner.

He's where she left him. Under the beam, on his back, with his lamp still burning at its strange angle — the cell on the lamp will last a year, divers say; it's the last thing to go — and his faceplate turned up toward the ceiling. She doesn't look through it. She doesn't need to. She knows what's there. She hangs in the water beside him for a while with her hand on his shell.

"Hello," she says, on the suit band, to nobody.

Then she finds his cutter. It's under his left hip, where he thought it was, wedged between his shell and the deck. She has to work it out a centimetre at a time, and when it comes free it comes all at once and she nearly loses it into the passage, and she holds it against her chest for a moment before she can do anything else.

Three dogs. Alone. With nobody to brace.

She braces herself. She wedges her boots against the beam — the beam that killed him — and her back against the frame of the door, and she sets the cutter's head against the seam with both hands and her whole body behind it, and she cuts. It's slower like this. The thrust keeps pushing her off the iron and she keeps having to stop and set herself again. The first dog takes forty minutes. The second takes thirty-five. Between them the Hulk shudders twice, long deep lurches that come through the iron into her teeth, and both times she stops and waits with her eyes shut and her hands on the cutter, and both times it passes.

The third dog goes at an hour and fifty-one minutes.

She feels it through the cutter. A lurch, a give. And then, slowly, without her touching it, the vault door begins to move. Not much. A hand's breadth. The Hulk's tumble does it: the great iron slab, freed of its bolts for the first time in nearly four centuries, swinging very slowly on its hinges as the whole ship turns around it, the seam opening in front of her like a mouth.

Dark water beyond. And cold. Colder than the passage. Colder than anything in the Hulk.

She puts her shoulder to the door and pushes, and it swings, and she goes in.

* * *

The vault is a box of black iron ten metres on a side, and it's full of ice.

Not all of it. The perigees have reached in here too, through the door and the walls, and melted the outer skin of it, so that she swims in through a layer of water a metre deep into a room that's half frozen still — the far wall and the floor and one corner locked in old grey ice, cloudy and fissured, with the near side clear. Her lamp goes into the ice and gets lost. Her breath in the helmet is very loud.

In the middle of the floor, rising out of the ice, there's a cradle.

That's what it is. She knows it at once, though she's never seen one: a padded iron bracket on a pedestal, the kind you'd make to hold something precious absolutely still. It's small. Built for a thing the size of a big book. Around it, frozen into the ice at its base, there are steel bands — four of them, thick as her wrist, snapped and twisted and splayed out across the ice like the petals of a flower — as if something much larger than the cradle had been strapped down on top of it once, and torn loose, and taken.

Empty.

On the wall beside the cradle, a shelf. Iron, like the walls. Three boxes strapped to it in a row, each the size of the cradle's missing thing, each with a lead seal across its hasp and a number stamped into its cover. She swims closer with the lamp. I. II. IV.

There's a gap between the second and the fourth.

On the other wall, above a crust of ice, a brass plaque. Green with verdigris, crusted with something white, covered in tiny writing she can't read through the crust. Old Charter letters, in a cramped hand, in columns. She puts her gauntlet on it. Brass. Good heavy brass, a metre wide. Eleven the kilo, Gorse would say. She can't move it; it's bolted into the iron. She turns on her helmet camera and takes it in, slowly, top to bottom, column by column, out of habit — the way you photograph anything you can't carry, so you can come back for it, or sell the picture to someone who will.

Beneath the plaque, set into the wall, a little steel safe a hand's breadth square.

Its door is open.

She hangs in front of it for a long time. It's very small. It has a keyhole and no handle. Its door stands open on its hinge, and inside it there's nothing. Nothing at all. A little steel box with ice in the bottom of it and her lamp shining in.

And floating above the ice in the corner of the vault, tethered by one arm to the iron pedestal of the cradle with a belt somebody looped through a handhold nearly four hundred years ago, there's a man.

He's in grey. A long grey coat, torn, and under it a grey suit. He's turned away from her, face down to the ice, his white hair drifting round his head in the water. He's been frozen and he's thawed and he's been in the water a long time, and she doesn't want to see his face, and she doesn't look. But round his neck, on top of the coat, there's a chain. Heavy brass links, and on them a medallion the size of a palm. On the medallion, a pair of scales.

An Assessor's coat. An Office chain.

She knows the song. Everyone in the Gutter knows the song. *Fen was an Assessor, grey was his coat.*

"Oh," says Yara softly, on the suit band, to nobody. "So this is where you got to."

She looks at him for a long time. At the grey coat and the chain and the belt looped through the handhold of the empty cradle. At the empty safe in the wall and the snapped steel bands on the ice and the gap on the shelf between two and four. *Fen's cache.* Something Fen stole from the Office on the Night of the Hulk. Something the people who lost it would have killed to keep. *If he'd taken it out of the Hulk, someone would have used it.*

He hadn't taken it out. He'd brought it in here. Here, to the vault, to the strongroom of the old Charter ship — and whatever it was, it was gone. Taken off the cradle, or out of the safe, by somebody, sometime, in four hundred years. Torn loose and carried away. And the Turncoat had stayed. Tied himself to the pedestal of the empty cradle with his own belt, so he wouldn't drift, and stayed here in the dark with nothing, counting, until he froze.

*Fen's in there somewhere, counting.*

She doesn't search his pockets. She doesn't touch him. She can't. She turns away from him to the shelf, and puts her gauntlets on the box marked IV, and works at its strap until the strap gives. It's heavy — not in the water, but she can feel the mass of it, the way it doesn't want to move — and she clips it to her harness with two carabiners and feels it settle against her hip, iron and lead, a box the size of a big book with a seal across its hasp.

Kade wants something sealed.

At the door she stops and looks back once. The lamp makes a small moving room of light in the black water. The empty cradle. The open safe. The plaque with its columns of tiny writing under the crust. And the grey man in the corner, tied to nothing, drifting very slightly on his belt as the Hulk turns round him, with his face to the ice.

She doesn't make a mark. She doesn't know what she'd write.

* * *

She comes up into the Gutter at the end of the window with twenty minutes of air and an iron box on her hip, and nobody looks at her.

In the can, she unclips it and sets it on the galley shelf, beside the brass seal-reader. They look like they belong together. The same sunburst-and-scales on both lids, though the box's is so worn she can barely see it. She sits on the bottom bunk and looks at them.

Then, because she has nothing else to do, she takes the seal-reader down, and turns it over, and finds the catch that Pim found, and presses it. The panel slides back. She holds the dull glass eye of the reader against the lead seal on the box's hasp.

Nothing. Then, very faintly, the eye glows amber, the way it glows when the bell rings. A small sound, a click. And across the glass, so faint she has to cup her hand round it to read, a line of tiny Charter letters, scrolling.

*M.I. VOYAGE IV. CLOSED UNDER SEAL CR 597. ALL OBLIGATIONS DISCHARGED OR TRANSFERRED. SEAL TRUE.*

She reads it three times.

A closed book. A ship's ledger from a voyage more than seven hundred years ago, its debts all paid, its people all landed and dead on worlds she's never heard of. The seal true. Of course it's true. Who would bother to forge a dead book?

She sets the reader down. She sits on the bunk in the dim can with her hands between her knees and looks at the iron box on the shelf and the brass eye glowing faintly beside it, and then going out.

Two passes. One window left, if the engineers are right, and maybe not even that. Pim in a white bed with a mask on his face, breathing all the way down. Mitya under a beam with his lamp burning. A grey man in an empty vault, tied to nothing. Four hundred thousand names on the concourse screen, scrolling. And on the shelf, the only thing she's brought out of the Hulk in two weeks that anyone might pay for, a box of debts that were settled seven hundred years ago.

On the inside of the hatch, under her mother's two hundred and nine, there are two short lines in fresh scratches. She looks at them. She doesn't add a third.

Somewhere far off on the other side of the Gutter, the Ash bell rings the hour, seven notes falling. The seal-reader on the shelf clicks once in answer and glows, and goes dark.

She sits in the dark and doesn't count anything at all.


# Chapter 19: Down the Sea

*The Crossing · CR 611*

Absalom came up out of the dark on the twenty-second day after the Fork, and everyone who could stand at a viewport stood at one and watched it.

Annick watched from the Lantern, because the Master had asked her to. She stood at the rail of the command ring beside Nadia Szell, under the great armoured dome, with the Book's key at her throat and her hands at her sides, and looked up.

It filled a quarter of the sky. Then a third. A gas giant, the survey records had said, a little larger than the Charter's standard and a little cooler, with a system of moons; she had read the entry in the Book a dozen times without ever seeing it. Nothing in the entry was like this. It was banded — rust and ochre and cream and a deep bruised brown — in stripes that ran round it like the grain in a cut stone, and every band was moving, very slowly, against the ones beside it; and where they met, along every edge, there were storms. Curls and eddies and spirals of cloud, white and gold and the colour of old blood, each one as big as a world, turning, turning. A long way down one flank there was a single great eye, a dark oval storm with a pale rim, that seemed to look at nothing at all. The light of the sun lay across it at a low angle, so that half the giant was in shadow, and on the shadowed half, very faintly, there were flashes. Lightning. Each flash, Szell said, would light a continent.

And against the face of it, small and white and cracked like an old plate, a moon.

"Tamar," said Szell beside her. "The survey called it Absalom IV. Ice, all the way down. Eight hundred metres of it at the thinnest, then a sea." She was quiet for a moment. "It's where we'll live."

Behind them, on the Lantern, the bonded woman from the coil gang who now sat on the Council was crying, silently, with her hand over her mouth. Lieutenant Orme at the helm was not. Tobiah was not there; he was in the Hold, where he had been most of the twenty-two days, among the cradles, with the people who had been thawed at the Fork and the people who had not.

"Tomorrow," said Szell, "we'll go into orbit. And at noon, in the cargo bay, we'll burn the Book."

"Yes, Master."

"And after, you'll seal two entries." The Master's voice was pleasant, unhurried. "The Council as trustee of the patent, for the people of the hold. And the opening of the Account. Edwin's drafted them both; they're very short. I'd like it done the same afternoon, before anyone's had time to think too much about it." She turned her head and looked at Annick with her dark considering eyes. "You don't object."

"After the Burning," said Annick. "Not before. So that it's clean."

Szell considered that. Then she nodded, slowly, as if a reading had come back as she expected.

"After," she agreed. "So that it's clean."

* * *

Dace was in the infirmary.

She had been there for twenty-two days. The needle in her shoulder had gone deep, and the one along her ribs had opened her side like a seam, and Haddad had packed the wounds and closed them and then opened them again when they went bad in the second week, and for four days at the end of the first jump Annick had sat beside her through the ship's night and listened to her breathe — too fast, too shallow, in the bonded way, saving air that nobody was billing any more. Dace had not woken for most of those four nights. When she did, she had looked at Annick sitting there and turned her face to the wall.

She was sitting up now, propped on pillows in the ring's light gravity, with her shoulder strapped and a great white dressing on her side under the gown, and she watched Annick come in across the long room and sit down in the chair by her bunk, and did not turn away.

"Tobiah came," said Dace.

Annick waited.

"Yesterday. He sat where you're sitting. He asked how I was, and I said I'd live, and he said good." Her sister's voice was flat and steady. "Then he told me about Marit. That the Factor asked you for a name, for me, and you gave him hers. He said you did it for me. He said he wanted me to know that, because I'd find out anyway, and he wanted me to hear it from someone who didn't hate you for it."

"Does he not?"

"He says not." Dace looked at her. "I do."

Annick said nothing.

"She talked to me in the Hold," said Dace. "Marit. Every night for three months. She taught me law. Did you know that? The guild settlement, and the bonded codes, and the Ordinances, all of them — she said I had a head for it. She said when we got to Absalom she'd make me her clerk. She was going to write the colony's charter. She'd started it. On paper, in the Hold, in pencil, under her pillow." Dace's voice did not shake. "They found it when they took her. The proctors. They burned it in the galley furnace. Kofi saw."

"I'm sorry."

"I know you are. You always are." Her sister leaned forward a little, wincing at the shoulder. "You think I don't know what you did. You think I don't know you lay in that strongroom all night with your hand on the Book and did your sums and got me on one side and her on the other and found me heavier. I know. I'd have done the same, maybe. I don't know. I hate you for it anyway."

"Yes," said Annick.

"Is that all? *Yes?*"

"What else would you like me to say?"

Dace stared at her. And then, unexpectedly, terribly, her face broke. Not much. Her mouth went, and her eyes, and she put her good hand over them and sat there in the bunk with her shoulders shaking and made no sound at all; and Annick sat in the chair and did not touch her, because she did not know if she was allowed, and the infirmary hummed round them, and somewhere at the far end of it one of the thawed was moaning in her sleep.

When Dace took her hand away, her eyes were red and quite dry.

"Why are you here?" she said.

So Annick told her.

She told it level, in the clerk's voice, very low, leaning close to the bunk, the way you'd read out a bill. She told her about the day room door at the Fork and what she had heard through it. Quarry's voice: *burn the Book; they'll weep; then raise an account; call it Liberty.* Tobiah's: *somebody's got to pay for the air.* Szell's: *I made sure he'd never be asked.* Orme on the bridge deck. *Nobody will ever think anything else.* She told her about the two entries the Master wanted sealed tomorrow afternoon, after the fire, before anyone had time to think too much about it.

Dace listened without moving. When it was done, she sat looking down at the dressing on her side, and said nothing for so long that Annick thought she would not speak again.

"Then tell them," she said. "Tomorrow. In the cargo bay. Stand up in front of everyone and tell them."

"Tell them what? That the woman who gave Marit Osei to the Factor overheard something through a door?" Annick shook her head. "Szell holds the ship. Every lock on her answers to Szell; I made sure of it. Tobiah holds the people. They love him. They'd follow him into the coil. They've been in the dark for fourteen months, and tomorrow he's going to give them a fire, and they'll weep, and anything I say after that will be the Ink trying to keep her Book." She looked at her sister. "And then they'll take the Book from me and burn it anyway, and the key with it, and seal whatever they like."

"So you'll let them."

"No," said Annick.

Dace looked at her. Something in her face changed.

"I need you to keep something," said Annick. "For a long time. Maybe for the rest of your life. Maybe longer — maybe you'll have to give it to someone, and they'll have to give it to someone. And never use it. Never sell it. Never tell anyone what it is, unless the day comes when—" She stopped. She did not know how to say it. "Unless the day comes when someone can stand up and say it to all of them, and be heard."

"Why me?" said Dace. "You don't trust me. I don't trust you."

"That's why," said Annick. "You're the only person on this ship who doesn't believe a word I say. So you'll check."

Her sister looked at her. Then, slowly, she held out her good hand across the blanket, palm up, and Annick took it, and held it. It was cold. It was always cold. Annick held it in both of hers, the way she used to on the stack roofs of Callis when Dace was five, and they sat like that without speaking while the infirmary hummed round them and the ship came down toward the moon where they would live.

* * *

She went to the strongroom at the change of the night watch and did not turn on the lamp at first.

She floated in the dark beside the cradle with her hand on the Book's iron lid, the way she had on the night before Marit died, and listened to the ship. The murmur had changed again since the Fork. It was louder, more broken: there were nearly two thousand people awake on the *Merciful Increase* now, the Wake and the thawed and the crew, crowded into a ring built for a few hundred, and the ship did not sleep any more; it muttered all night, like a city. Under the murmur, on the other side of the left-hand bulkhead, the water of Tank Four shifted in its long slow sleep. On the other side of the right-hand bulkhead, in the Hold, the cradles that were still full — one thousand nine hundred and four of them; she had counted — hummed in the blue cold.

She turned on the lamp. She opened the Book. She put the key in the hasp and turned it, and the lid unlocked with its deep, soft sound like a door closing far away, and the page-glass woke and filled with the voyage, blue-white, trembling.

She did not scroll back. She did not need to. She knew what was there.

She put her thumb to the bead.

"Entry," she said. "Annick Vell. Purser of the *Merciful Increase*, Voyage the Fifth, holding the Key and the Book together. In orbit of the world Absalom. Charter Year six hundred and eleven, the night before Landfall."

The page-glass took it. She watched the words grow in their neat column.

"I record that at Pell the Receivers of Vey ordered this ship and the debts of her hold sold to the Shale Concern, where the hold would have been worked until it died; and that Factor Edwin Quarry sought my seal to it, for a commission of two in the hundred.

"I record that Purser Aurelio Benn refused to seal it. And that First Officer Nadia Szell closed the valve of his air while he slept, and opened it again when he was dead. The concentrator's log records it, with her code erased. She has said it in my hearing.

"I record that at the Fork the bonded of the hold rose against the Factor; and that I sealed Nadia Szell as Master of this ship, because there was no one else who could fly her; and that Captain Harrow Lindqvist was shot on the bridge deck by Lieutenant Caspar Orme, on her order, and not by any bonded hand.

"I record that after the rising, Nadia Szell and Tobiah Ruck and Edwin Quarry agreed among themselves to burn this Book before the people at Landfall, and to raise in its place an account of what every person freed owes for that freedom, and to call it Liberty, and to keep it.

"I record that I gave the name of Marit Osei to the Factor, to save my sister's life; and that Marit Osei was put out of the forward lock for it, and her debt passed to her granddaughter, and I sealed it. Let it be written against me. It is owed."

She stopped. She breathed. Four in. Hold. Four out. It did not help. It never had.

"Now therefore," she said.

"Under Article Twenty of the Ordinances, I discharge every debt written in this Book. Of every person of the hold, the living and the dead, and of their heirs and assigns, forever. Emeric Saal's, and his children's. Marit Osei's, and her granddaughter's. Every mark of passage, and every meal, and every litre of air. Nothing written in this Book is owed by anyone.

"And whereas the Charter's patent of the world Absalom and all the worlds of its system stands recorded in this Book, to vest in the colony at Landfall: I vest it in the people of the hold and their heirs, in common. To be held by no one person, and no council, and no house. And no account of debt shall be raised against them under colour of this Book, or of any copy of it, or of any entry claiming to be made from it.

"And under Article Nine, this ship being taken by parties not lawfully in command, I commit this Book to the ballast, that it be not suborned. They will burn the dead book of the third voyage in its place tomorrow, and believe they have burned this. Let them."

She looked at the page-glass. At the words in their column, blue-white, trembling a little, as if the light were not quite sure of itself.

"What is written is owed," she said. "I have written what we owe each other."

She did not say *sealed*. Not yet.

* * *

She carried the Book into the Hold with the key still in its hasp and the lid open, so that the page-glass lit her way.

There was nobody there. At this hour, since the Fork, there was never anybody there; the cradle techs worked the day watch, and the thawed would not come in. The great hatch stood open. She went through it into the blue cold, and up into the racks, hand over hand on the struts with the Book held against her chest in one arm, three tiers in and halfway down, to the hollow.

Most of the cradles here were still full. Lise Saal's was. Annick floated in front of it and looked through the frost on the window at the thin young face, the straight nose, the mouth that turned down at the corners, the rime on the lashes. Above it, the two smaller cradles. Tam. Orrin. Further along the row, a cradle she had never looked at before, that she had had to find in the manifest this afternoon: a small white shell, child-sized, with a girl's face behind the frost. Nine years old. Dark, with her grandmother's narrow chin. *Osei, Abena.*

Around her, above, below, receding into the blue dimness on every side, one thousand nine hundred and four of them. Sleeping. Owing nothing.

*Article Eleven. An entry read aloud under seal before witnesses stands as the Book itself.*

*It was meant for wrecks*, Benn had said. *It never worked very well. Witnesses forget.*

She put her thumb back on the bead, and read it to them.

All of it. From *Entry* to *each other*. She did not raise her voice. In the Hold there was no need to; the cold carried every word out among the racks and the struts and the frosted windows, and gave it back to her, very faintly, from a long way off. She read it the way she had read Emeric Saal's transfer in the coil gallery, the way she had read every bill and every death and every name for eleven years: level, clear, without hurry. She read the part about Marit. She read the part about the patent. She read *nothing written in this Book is owed by anyone* to one thousand nine hundred and four sleeping faces behind the frost; and not one of them moved, and not one of them heard, and the cradles hummed, and the pumps sighed in the racks.

When she came to the end she stayed for a moment with her thumb on the bead.

Then she said the last line. It was not in the Ordinances. She had not planned it. It came out of her in the clerk's level voice, as if she had been writing it all her life.

"Read aloud under seal, in the Hold, before witnesses. Who were asleep."

Her voice shook. On *asleep*. She heard it, and the Book heard it, and took it, and grew it into the lattice where it would stay.

"Sealed," she said.

* * *

Back in the strongroom she worked quickly, because there was no longer any reason not to.

She took the key from the hasp. The page-glass went dark and the lid locked, and the Book was only a block of iron again, heavy in her arms. She set it on the floor beside the pedestal, against the drop hatch, where the red lever stood with its seal-wire looped through it.

She went to the dead shelf and unstrapped VOYAGE III.

It was identical to the Book in every way she had ever been able to find, except for the number stamped on the cover plate and the seal across its hasp: a bar of soft grey lead, crimped over the steel, with the Charter's mark pressed into it. She worked the lead off with the blade of the strongroom's pen-knife, slowly, careful not to scar the steel. It came away in one piece, a little curled strip the length of her finger. Underneath, the hasp was clean and bright. The number plate on the cover she could do nothing about; but it was small and low on the iron, below the sunburst, and nobody aboard but she had ever looked at a Book closely enough to read it, and tomorrow nobody would. She set VOYAGE III in the cradle, where the Book had been. In the dim lamplight it looked exactly as it always had.

She took the curl of lead to the bench under the plaque and flattened it with the base of the bead press until it was a rough grey plate the size of her thumb. With the point of the pen-knife she scratched into it, small, in plain clerk's capitals, the only thing she could think of that would mean something to someone who held the key and nothing to anyone who didn't.

ART. 9 — A.V.

Then she put the key into the privy box.

The little steel door opened to it the way it always had. Inside, on the shelf where generations of pursers had kept their wills and their letters, there was a single thing: a squat brown bottle with a wax seal, Callisian brandy, that Aurelio Benn had been too frightened to drink for twenty-eight years and had left, under the Ordinances, to a sister in the Callis hill district who would never receive it.

Annick took it out. She broke the wax with her thumbnail. She drank.

It was terrible. It burned all the way down and lay in her stomach like a coal, and she coughed, and her eyes ran, and she laughed — once, aloud, in the dark strongroom, a sound she did not recognise — and drank again. Then she put the bottle back in the privy box, beside the little grey plate of lead, and shut the door, and turned the key, and it locked.

The Book was where she had left it, against the drop hatch.

She knelt beside it. She broke the seal-wire on the red lever and drew it out of its loop and put it in her pocket. She pulled the lever. Under the floor something heavy moved — a bolt, a counterweight — and the drop hatch swung down and open, and beneath it there was a short dark throat of lead-lined iron, slanting away into the deck toward the left-hand bulkhead, just wide enough for one thing. From somewhere at the bottom of it, faint and cold, came the smell of water.

Beside the drop hatch, set flush in the floor, there was a round iron plate a metre across with eight bolts round its rim, that she had walked over for eleven years without thinking about. *Recovery port*, said a small stencil on its edge. *Ballast 4 well. For use at port by lawful owner only.* So that whoever came after, with the right to, could go down and fetch back what had been committed. She looked at it for a moment. Then she looked away.

She lifted the Book in both arms. It was heavy. It had never been so heavy.

"Down the sea," she said, very softly, aloud. She did not know who to.

She slid it into the throat of the drop.

It went with a long low iron sound, sliding, gathering speed, the lead sleeve hissing against the chute, down and away into the dark under the deck; and then, after what seemed a long time, from very far off on the other side of the bulkhead, so faint she was not sure she heard it at all — a sound like something heavy entering water. And then nothing.

She closed the hatch. She pushed the lever back up. She took the seal-wire out of her pocket and looked at it, and it was snapped, and would not loop; so she reached up to the back of her own head, where for twenty-two days, since the Fork, she had been tying her hair the way her sister tied hers, and pulled out the twist of fine steel wire she had begged from the coil gang's stores, and looped it through the lever and twisted it closed. It was not Charter wire. It would do. Nobody aboard had ever looked at it closely enough to know.

Then she sat on the floor of the strongroom with her back against the cradle, where a dead book from a voyage twenty-three years gone now lay in the Book's place, and let her hair fall round her face, and waited for the shaking to stop.

* * *

She went to the infirmary in the last hour before dawn watch. The long room was dim and full of the breathing of the thawed. Haddad was asleep at his desk with his head on his arms.

Dace was awake. She watched Annick come down the row between the bunks and sit in the chair, and she did not say anything, and she held out her good hand, palm up, on the blanket.

Annick put the key in it.

Her sister's fingers closed round it. She looked at it — the long iron key, the notched bit, the dull bead in the bow, the small stamped number on the shank — and turned it once in the dim light. Then, without a word, she lifted the edge of the great white dressing on her side, wincing, and slid the key in under the packing against the wound, flat, along her ribs, and smoothed the dressing back down over it with the flat of her hand.

"Haddad changes it every morning," said Annick.

"Not this one he won't," said Dace. "I'll do it myself. I've watched him for three weeks." She lay back against the pillows, pale, with her hand over the dressing. "What does it open?"

"A box."

"What's in the box?"

"Something that will tell whoever opens it where to look."

"And where's that?"

Annick looked at her sister. Then she leaned close and told her. Two words, very low, so that even Haddad asleep at the desk ten metres away could not have heard.

Dace closed her eyes. When she opened them again they were wet, and she was almost smiling.

"The song," she said.

"The song."

"*When the Book goes down the sea.*" Her sister's hand tightened on the dressing. "I always thought that was just a rhyme."

At the end of the long room, at his desk, with his head on his arms, Yusuf Haddad had opened his eyes. He did not lift his head. He looked down the row of bunks at the two of them, at Dace's hand flat on the dressing; and then, very deliberately, he closed his eyes again, and turned his face the other way.

* * *

They burned it at noon, in the cargo bay, before all the people.

The *Merciful Increase* had come into orbit of Tamar in the morning watch, and through the bay's long ports the moon hung white and cracked and very close, with the vast banded face of Absalom behind it filling half the sky. The bay had been emptied of stores. Nearly two thousand people filled it: the Wake in their dun, and the thawed in whatever they had been given — crew coveralls, blankets, the sleeping greys of the dead — and the crew in their uniforms, all floating together in the zero gravity in a great close silent mass, holding the stanchions and the cargo rails and each other. Children on shoulders. Old men held up between their sons. Ama Brook from the galley, at the front, with her grey braids and her face wet.

At the centre of the bay, on a disc of black iron welded to the deck for the purpose, stood a crucible: a squat steel bowl on three legs, packed with thermite.

The Council of the Founders floated beside it. Seven of them. Szell in a clean Master's coat. Quarry in his good one. Orme, the bonded woman from the coil, two others. And Tobiah Ruck, enormous, in plain dun, the same as the Wake, with his scarred head bowed.

Annick floated a little apart, at the edge, where the Purser stood at any entry. She held nothing. Her hands were empty.

Szell brought the Book from the strongroom herself. She carried it through the crowd in both arms, slowly, so that everyone could see it — the iron case, the worn sunburst on its cover, the bright steel hasp — and the crowd parted for her and closed behind, and a sound went through it, low, like wind. She set it in the crucible. Nobody looked at the little number plate below the sunburst. Nobody looked at anything but the Book.

Tobiah spoke.

Annick would never be able to remember afterward exactly what he said. She would remember that it was short, and that his voice was low and soft and carried to every corner of the bay without his raising it, and that he did not say anything about Liberty. He said that they had been carried in the dark. He said that they had been counted, and billed, and sold, and that some of them had died of it, and he said their names — Emeric Saal, Marit Osei, Joss Imre, and others, a great many others, more than Annick had known he knew. He said that this was the Book in which every one of them had been written down. He said that from today, on this world, nobody's name would be written in it again.

Then he took the striker from Szell's hand and lit the crucible.

The thermite caught with a sound like tearing cloth. White light — light so fierce that the whole bay went white with it, every face, every hand — and a roar, and a pillar of white smoke boiling up toward the ribs of the ceiling and spreading out under them in a grey cloud. In the heart of the light, the iron case of the dead book of the third voyage blistered, and blackened, and ran. The sunburst on its cover went soft and slid. The hasp fused. Something inside it cracked, very loud, like ice breaking.

The crowd made a sound Annick had never heard a crowd make. Not a cheer. Something lower and older — a great broken cry that went up from two thousand throats at once and went on and on, and turned, as it went on, into weeping. People held each other. People hid their faces. An old man near the front was on his knees in the air, if you can kneel with nothing under you, with his hands over his eyes. Ama Brook had her arms round a girl Annick did not know.

And then, from somewhere at the back, near the hatch, a man's voice began to sing.

*Heave the iron, heave it slow.*

Another took it up. And another. Not quietly, this time; not under their breath, the way the Wake had sung it when Marit went out of the lock. Loud. The whole bay. Two thousand voices in the white light and the grey smoke, the coil gang's song, the quarrymen's song, the song nobody aboard had known the meaning of —

*Heave and heave and we'll be free,
when the Book goes down the sea —*

Annick floated at the edge of the bay with her empty hands and her face lit white by the burning of a book whose debts had been paid twenty years before, and listened to them sing it. She did not sing. She looked across the crucible and found that Tobiah Ruck was looking at her, through the smoke and the light; and his broken face was wet, and he was singing with the rest; and she could not tell, she would never be able to tell, what he was thinking.

* * *

"There's no key," she said.

They were in the strongroom: Szell, Quarry, Orme, Tobiah, and Annick. Two hours after the fire. The bay was still full of smoke; you could smell it even here, in the spine, through the iron. On the strongroom bench, beside the cradle where VOYAGE III had lain until noon, there were two sheets of film in Quarry's elegant hand and a new lattice block, grown that morning in the ship's laboratory, small and clean and the colour of weak tea, waiting for its first entry.

"I'm sorry?" said Szell.

"There's no key. I haven't got it." Annick stood by the empty cradle with her hands at her sides. "I can't seal anything. Not the trusteeship. Not the Account. Nothing. There is no purser's key on this ship any more."

There was a silence.

"Where is it?" said Szell. Quite gently.

"Gone."

"Gone where?"

Annick said nothing.

Orme searched her. He did it thoroughly and without any expression, as he did everything; he searched her coat and her clothes and her boots and her hair, which she had let fall, and then he made her take off her clothes, and searched her, and then he searched the strongroom — the bench, the shelves, the cradle, the dead books on their straps, the gap where the third had been. He tried the privy box, which would not open. He looked at the drop hatch and the red lever with its loop of wire, and Annick stood naked by the cradle and watched him look at it, and did not breathe; and he looked away. He went to her cabin and searched that. He came back and shook his head.

"The lock," said Quarry softly. "She went to the forward lock last night, I expect. Or the galley furnace." He looked at Annick with his pricing eyes, and there was something in them she had not seen before — not anger; something closer to interest. "It doesn't matter, Master. The Book's ash. You don't need a purser's seal for a council's account. A council has its own seal. I'll have one made."

"It won't be the same."

"No," said Quarry. "But nobody will know the difference. Not for years. Not ever, if you're careful." He did not take his eyes off Annick. "I've always wondered what she'd cost. I see I was wrong about the price."

Szell looked at Annick for a long moment. Then she said, in the same pleasant unhurried voice she used for everything, "Then she's a traitor. She hid the key so that the Charter's creditors could claim us back. So that one day, when the Receivers came up the Lane, there'd be someone with a key to the Book's own records, ready to sell us to them." She nodded slowly, as if testing the shape of it. "*The Purser would have sold us.* That's what we'll say. They'll believe it. They watched her enter Marit's death."

"They'll want her put out of the lock," said Orme.

"Yes."

"No," said Tobiah Ruck.

He had not spoken. He had stood by the door the whole time, through the search, with his great arms folded and his eyes on the floor. He lifted his head now.

"No more lock," he said. "Not today. Not on the day we burned it. I won't have it." He looked at Szell. "There's a survey party going down to the ice tomorrow. To find a site. Cut the first galleries. Send her with them. Let her dig."

"She'll die on the ice."

"Maybe. Maybe not. That's for the ice." His soft voice did not rise. "Send her down."

Szell considered him. Then she shrugged, very slightly. "As you like," she said. "The ice, then." She turned to the door. "Edwin, the seal."

They went. Orme went. Quarry went last, and at the door he stopped and looked back at Annick standing naked by the cradle, and then, very briefly, at the drop hatch in the floor with its loop of fine steel wire; and then at her again. He smiled very slightly.

"A Factor learns the Ordinances too, Purser," he said. "All twenty-two." And went.

Tobiah stayed.

He picked up her clothes from the floor, where Orme had thrown them, and held them out to her without looking at her, and she took them and put them on. He waited until she had. Then he looked at her — for a long moment, steadily, the way he had looked at her across the mess the night Marit died. And then his eyes went, as Quarry's had, to the floor beside the pedestal. To the drop hatch. The red lever. The twist of wire through its loop that was not Charter wire.

"Down the sea," said Tobiah Ruck, very quietly.

It might have been a question. She did not answer it.

He looked at the lever. Then he looked at her again, and something moved in his broken face that she would think about on the ice for the rest of her life, and never be sure of — and he turned without a word, and ducked his great head under the red letters over the door, and went out, and left the strongroom to her.

* * *

The survey shuttle went down at dawn watch the next day with nine people in it: six crew volunteers with sledges and lances and two months' stores, and Annick Vell under guard, and her sister.

Dace had walked out of the infirmary at four in the morning on her own feet, against Haddad's orders, with her shoulder strapped and her side bound tight under a clean dressing she had changed herself, and gone to the shuttle bay and sat down on the bench beside her sister and said to the guard, "I'm going too," and refused to be moved. Nobody had argued long. One more mouth on the ice was one less in the ring.

Haddad had come to the bay to see them off. He had brought a bag of dressings and a bottle of something and a book. He gave Dace the dressings and Annick the book, and did not say anything at all, and Annick did not look at the title until much later.

The shuttle fell away from the *Merciful Increase* and turned, and for a moment, through the small port beside her seat, Annick saw the ship. The whole of her, at once, for the first time in eleven years: a long dark seed-shaped spine eleven hundred metres from bow to stern, with the great ring turning slowly about her middle and the dome of the Lantern catching the light of the sun at her bow, hanging against the banded face of Absalom. She looked small. She looked very old.

Somewhere in the middle of her, in the dark beside the Hold, there was a tank of black water twelve metres deep. And at the bottom of it, in the silt, in its lead sleeve, the Book lay with every debt in it discharged and every name in it free and the world it had been given held by no one; in the ballast, where the Ordinances said it should be, waiting — as long as it had to — for someone lawfully in command to come for it.

Beside her, on the bench, Dace's good hand found hers. Under the clean dressing on her sister's side, against the wound, Annick could feel through her own palm the faint hard shape of the key.

Below them, rising, white and cracked like an old plate, the ice.


# Chapter 20: The Fourth Cistern

*The Account · CR 929*

In the dark, somebody took hold of my wrists.

I didn't fight. I couldn't see; there was nothing to see. Twenty thousand people screaming in a black iron cavern, the carbines on the galleries firing at nothing, muzzle-flashes going off like lightning in the ribs of the ceiling and lighting, for a hundredth of a second each, a frozen picture of everything — faces, hands, banners torn loose and turning, a child upside down in the air with her mouth open — and then black again. Someone took hold of my wrists, and something cold slid between them, and there was a sharp snap, and the cuffs fell away.

"Up the Lantern stair," said Teodor Rask's voice in my ear. "Then left. Calla's got the broadcast room. Go."

"Thorne—"

"Your clerk's on the dais. Under the table, if he's got sense. Go."

The emergency lamps came on.

Red. All of them at once, on every rib, a dull blood-coloured glow that turned the Hall into the inside of a furnace and the grey banners into slow black smoke. The Mercy Guardsmen who'd held my arms were gone — down into the crowd, or dead; I didn't see. On the forward gallery beside me a man in ballast grey was lying on his back in the air with his arms out, turning slowly, and his face was gone. I took the carbine out of his hands. I'd never fired one. I took it anyway.

I didn't go up the Lantern stair. I went down.

It's a long way from the forward gallery to the dais, through a crowd in panic in zero gravity. You can't walk. You can't swim. You go hand over hand along whatever you can reach — a rail, a cord, a stranger's coat, a stranger — and the crowd moves round you like water in a tank that's been struck, in surges, one way and then another, and you hold on through the surges and go on in the slack between. I went down the gallery stanchions to the deck and along the deck by the bolts in the iron plates, flat, under the crowd, the way miners go along a gallery floor when there's gas. It took me four minutes. I counted.

He was under the table. He had sense.

Two of his ratings were crouched in front of it with their white backs to the crowd and their sidearms out, and they turned them on me as I came along the deck, and Isaak Thorne said from under the black wood, in his flat timetable voice, "Let him through."

I went under. There was just room. He was folded up against the table's pedestal with his document case held against his chest and his crystal eye glowing faintly gold in the red dark, perfectly calm.

"Assessor," he said. "I'd hoped you'd come."

"Commander. I lodge."

"Under what standing? You're charged with murder." He said it without any emphasis at all, as if reminding me of an appointment. "I can't accept the oath of a man under a cloud. I told you."

I took out my handset. I found the recording and turned it to full and held it up between us in the red dark, under the table, with the crowd roaring and the carbines cracking outside; and Bastian Oduya's voice came out of it, high and fast and shaking, the way it had come out of him in the cradle bay among the open lids. *He said she was going to give the Hold a weapon. The Chief. He called me in.* Thorne listened. His crystal eye clicked. *She said, Bastian, let go, very quietly, like you'd say it to a child, and I—* It went on. *The paint. The words. I'd never seen them on a wall. He told me to put the pen in your rooms.* I'd heard it four times by then. I hadn't got used to it. I don't suppose I ever would have.

When it stopped, Thorne was quiet for a moment.

"Is he alive?"

"I don't know."

"Will he swear to it?"

"No. He'll never be found. That's all there is."

"That's his voice," said Thorne. "Under his name, recorded, with the circumstances. And the Chief Assessor?"

"Told me himself. This morning. In his office. *Every Chief Assessor is told.* He's known for nineteen years." I hadn't recorded that. I hadn't thought to; I'd gone to him to put something on his desk, not to take something away. "You'll have my oath for it."

Thorne looked at me with both eyes for a long moment in the red dark under the table.

"Then make it," he said. "Formally. In full."

So I made it. Under the black wood table in the Hall of the Account on the Burning, with twenty thousand people screaming round us and a dead man's carbine across my knees. I gave my name and my rank and my office. I swore before the Bureau of Titles of the Concordance of Worlds that the Account of Liberty was false; that its founding page had been composed nine years after Landfall in Tamar water under the seal of the Founders' Council, and described an entry it did not contain; that the Office of the Account knew it to be false, its Chief Assessor having said so to me that morning in his own words; and that Keeper Ottilie Brask had been killed for proving it, by the hand of Assessor Bastian Oduya, on the Chief Assessor's order, as the recording I'd given the Bureau showed.

I said it level. It's the one thing they train you for.

"Lodged," said Isaak Thorne.

He closed his document case. He looked out past his ratings' white backs at the furnace-red Hall and the crowd and the turning black banners. Then he took a small flat thing from his collar — a disc of the same pale gold as his eye — and pressed it.

"*Probity*," he said. "Thorne. Open my line to the station net and to the squadron, all channels. And find me whoever's holding this station's broadcast room."

* * *

It was Calla who was holding it, of course.

Her voice came over every speaker in the Hall a minute later — hoarse, out of breath, furious, ice-script in every syllable — telling twenty thousand people to get down, get down on the deck, get their children down, the Hold had the doors and the Hold had the broadcast and nobody else needed to die today. Then she stopped, in the middle of a sentence, and there was a pause, and when she spoke again her voice had changed.

"There's a — someone wants to say something." Another pause. "The Concordance man. The clerk. He says it's about the Account. Everyone shut up and listen."

Then Thorne's voice, flat and planed and absolutely unhurried, coming out of every speaker in the Hulk, and the Crown, and every ferry, and every screen in Saltmarch and Gallows Reach and the Sump; and, Calla told me afterward, the bridge of every ship in the Concordance squadron, and the white-collared woman from Ostrava in her suite in the Crown, and the Speaker of the Houses in hers.

"This is Verifier-Commander Isaak Thorne of the Bureau of Titles of the Concordance of Worlds. I was asked to verify the title of the Houses of Absalom to the instrument known as the Account of Liberty, for the purpose of its conveyance. I have examined the instrument. I have received this day the sworn lodging of an officer of the issuing office, impeaching it. The Bureau finds that the instrument's founding entry is not what it purports to be." The smallest pause. "The Bureau of Titles declines to verify. Title is not proved. The conveyance is void. The Ostrava Labor Trust has no instrument in this system and may take no satisfaction under it." Another pause. "That is all."

The Hall was silent.

Not quiet. Silent. Twenty thousand people, in the red dark, in the zero gravity, with the banners turning over them like smoke; and nobody made a sound. I could hear the ventilation. I could hear someone, far off near the stern hatch, breathing very fast.

Then somebody started to laugh.

I don't know who. A woman, somewhere in the middle of the crowd. A high, cracked, astonished sound, like someone who's been told a joke at a funeral and can't help it; and it went on and on, and someone else joined it, and someone else, and then the whole Hall was making a sound I'd never heard a crowd make and have never heard since — not a cheer, not weeping, something between them and older than either, a great broken roar that went up into the iron ribs and came back down and went up again, and didn't stop.

Beside me, under the table, Isaak Thorne took off the gold disc and put it back in his collar and closed his eyes — both of them, the real and the crystal — for a moment, as if he were very tired.

"Thank you, Assessor," he said. "You'd better go and find your daughter."

* * *

She'd found the door.

I didn't know it until I got there. I went up through the crowd toward the wards' gallery, hand over hand through the roaring, with the carbine on its strap over my back, and the white dresses were all gone from behind the brass rail, and the service door at the end of it was standing open on a narrow stair going down. I went down. At the bottom there was a laundry passage, hot and wet, lit by the same red emergency lamps. And halfway along it, sitting on a pile of clean sheets in a white dress too big at the shoulders, with her hair coming down out of its Crown pins and Mags Tiller's big red arm round her, was Wren.

She looked up and saw me. She didn't move. She sat on the sheets and looked at me with her mother's face and mine, and her mouth opened, and nothing came out of it.

Then she was off the sheets and across the passage and I had her.

I don't remember what I said. I don't think I said anything. I held her, in the red hot wet laundry passage, with her face in my borrowed coat and my face in her hair, and she was shaking, and I was shaking, and Mags Tiller sat on the sheets behind her and looked at the wall and wiped her eyes with the back of her hand.

"Line seven," said Wren at last, into my coat. "Word four. *Door.* I remembered. I was so — Da, I couldn't remember line seven, I had to say the whole Catechism in my head under the blankets to find it, and the guard heard me and thought I was praying—"

"You found it."

"I found it." She pulled back and looked at me. Her face was wet. "They said you killed her. The Keeper. They said it every day. They made me—" She stopped. "You didn't."

"No."

"I knew you didn't. I told them. I told them you couldn't even kill the fish in Mam's tank when it got the rot, you made me do it." She laughed, and it turned into something else. "Da. It's my birthday. It was. Yesterday."

"I know, love. I'm sorry."

"Is it Clear? Am I?"

I looked at her. I thought of the clerk at the Clearances counter, and *pending the Speaker's review*, and four hundred and six marks lodged against a portion that wouldn't close.

"I don't know," I said. "I think — I think there might not be an Account to be Clear of any more. I don't know what that means yet. Nobody does."

The Hulk shuddered.

It came through the deck and the walls and the pile of clean sheets, a single deep blow, as if something enormous had struck the station very hard somewhere a long way off. The red lamps flickered. Somewhere above us, through decks of iron, there was a long rolling crash, and then a sound I knew from schoolroom drills and had never in my life heard for real: the slow grinding boom of emergency bulkheads closing, one after another, all along the spine.

Mags Tiller was on her feet. "That's not ours," she said. "We've nothing that does that."

My handset spoke. Calla's voice, very fast.

"Fen. Where are you?"

"Laundry, Crown underside. I've got Wren."

"Get her to the stern dock. Now. Severine's ordered the frigate to fire on the Hulk. She's trying to take the broadcast room back with the guns. The bow docks are holed — we've lost the forward spoke. I've got a ferry at the stern taking out wounded. Get her on it."

* * *

We went aft along the underside of the Crown through the service ways, Mags in front with her lamp and Wren between us, and twice more on the way the Hulk shuddered under us and the lamps went out and came back, and the third time a pipe burst over our heads and filled the passage with steam; and we came out at last onto the stern dock, where the ferry was.

It was one of the pilgrim ferries. Its ramp was down and its cabin was full of wounded — people from the Hall, on the floor and the seats and each other, with Hold people moving among them with dressings — and Calla Dray was standing at the foot of the ramp with a carbine and blood on her face, shouting at someone in the cabin. She turned when she saw us. She looked at Wren for a long moment.

"This is her," she said.

"Yes."

"She looks like you. Poor thing." She jerked her head at the ramp. "In. Find a seat or a floor. We go in two minutes."

Wren didn't move. She was holding my hand. "Da—"

"Go on, love. I'll come after."

"You'll come *now*."

"I'll come after." I took her face in my hands. "There's something I've got to fetch. It's mine. It was your grandmother's. Somebody's taken it and I want it back."

"The key," said Wren. She'd seen it every week of her childhood, swinging on its chain when I bent to hug her. "Your ice key. Da, it's an old key, it doesn't open anything—"

"I know," I said. "I want it anyway."

She looked at me for a long time. Then she nodded, slowly, the way she'd nodded at nine when I told her the cipher was a secret just for us; and she let go of my hand and went up the ramp into the ferry, and at the top she turned round and looked at me, and I lifted my hand, and she lifted hers.

"Fen, you fool," said Calla Dray beside me.

"Yes."

"Marsh has it?"

"Marsh has it. And Marsh walked off that dais with his hand in his pocket toward the vault before the lights went out. I saw him."

Calla looked at me. Then she turned her head and spat, deliberately, on the deck between us, the way she had in the cold room the first night. A line.

"Severine's yacht broke dock ten minutes ago," she said. "From the Crown hub. She's burning for the Lane with her House guard and half the Constance plate. Merriam says she took a copy of the Account with her — they made one last month, for the Trust's lawyers. A full copy, under the Council's seal." She gripped the carbine. "She'll sell it somewhere. Some day."

"Then let them try to collect on it."

"Oh, they will. Somebody always does." She stepped back onto the ramp. "We'll be at Saltmarch in forty minutes. I'll put her with her mother myself. Then I'm coming back for this station." She looked at me. "If you're in it when I do, I'll buy you a drink. If you're not, I'll tell her you said something brave."

"Tell her I'm sorry about her birthday," I said.

Calla Dray looked at me for a moment longer. Then something happened to her scarred face — I don't know what to call it; it wasn't a smile — and she nodded once, and turned, and went up the ramp, and it closed behind her.

* * *

The spine was dark when I went down into it. The emergency bulkheads had closed every hundred metres, and I had to go through the crawl-hatches beside them one at a time, with the carbine on my back and my handset for a lamp. Past the Old Hold, with its great double hatch shut and somebody hammering on the other side of it. Past the Index, where the cabinet doors had burst open in the shock and the passage was full of drifting cards, white in the handset's glow, turning slowly, like snow.

The vault door was open.

It shouldn't have been. It's a Charter door, a metre of iron, and it closes itself on any loss of pressure in the passage; the drills say so. But someone had wedged it — a steel chair from the Keepers' antechamber, jammed between the door and the frame — so that it stood a hand's breadth open, and there was light inside. Not red. White. The vault's own lamps, on their own cell, burning as they'd burned for three hundred years.

I went in.

He was at the wall under the brass plaque. He'd tied himself to the pedestal of the old cradle with his belt — looped it through a handhold on the iron and round his waist, so that he wouldn't drift while he worked — and he was floating there at the end of it in his grey coat with the Chief's chain round his neck, in front of the little steel safe in the wall. Its door was open. My mother's key was in the lock.

The Account sat behind him on the cradle where it had always sat, strapped down with its steel bands, a great grey crystal animal on a stool too small for it. He'd turned his back on it.

He had a bottle in one hand. A squat brown bottle with a broken wax seal. In the other, a pistol. He wasn't pointing the pistol at anything. He was holding the bottle up to the light, and looking at it, and laughing — very quietly, his shoulders shaking — the way a man laughs when something has turned out to be exactly as foolish as he'd said it would be.

"A bottle of something," said Lucan Marsh. "I told you. Didn't I tell you?" He turned his head and looked at me. He didn't seem surprised. "Three hundred years, and nobody could open it, and the Houses tried drills, and it ate the drills. And inside it there's a bottle of brandy. Callisian, I think. Somebody's drunk about a third of it." He held it out toward me, gravely, across the vault. "Would you like some? It's quite terrible."

"Where's the rest of it?"

"The rest of what?"

"What was in the box."

He looked at me for a moment. Then he opened the hand that held the pistol, and I saw that there was something else in it, held against the grip with his thumb: a small grey thing, flat, the size of a thumb.

"This," he said. "Only this." He held it up to the white light and read it, squinting. "*Art. Nine. A.V.*" He looked at me. "What does it mean, Hollis? You were always better at puzzles than I was. Ottilie thought so."

I didn't answer. I didn't need to look. I'd floated past the brass plaque on the wall above his head when I was thirteen and never once read it; I'd floated past it again six days ago with Thorne and never once read it. I read it now. Twenty-two articles in a cramped legal hand, and the ninth of them about a third of the way down, in letters so small you'd need to be close.

"*Should the vessel be taken*," I said aloud, "*by any party not lawfully in command, the Purser shall commit the Book to the ballast, that it be not suborned.*"

The vault was very quiet.

"The ballast," said Lucan Marsh slowly.

I watched him understand it. I watched him in the white light, at the end of his belt, with the brandy in one hand and the little grey plate in the other, the way I'd watched three hundred and nine people understand the figure on the tally: the stillness first, and then behind the stillness something beginning to move very fast. *A.V.* *The Purser would have sold us.* *Her name was Annick Vell, and it is written in ash.* His eyes went to the Account on its cradle behind him. Then down. To the floor beside the cradle's pedestal, where there was a small square hatch with a red lever, and beside it, flush in the iron, a round plate a metre across with eight bolts round its rim and a stencil on its edge I'd never in my life looked at.

"She didn't burn it," he said. Softly. Wonderingly. "Oh, Hollis. She didn't burn it."

And he raised the pistol.

I don't know what he meant to do with it. I've thought about it a great deal. I've thought he meant to shoot me, because I was the only other person alive who knew. I've thought he meant to shoot the plate in the floor, which wouldn't have done anything at all. I've thought — down here I keep coming back to it — that he meant to shoot himself, because he was sixty-eight, and he'd known for nineteen years that every ledger is a forgery, and he'd just learned that there was one that wasn't. I don't know. I'll never know. He raised the pistol, and I had a dead man's carbine on a strap across my chest with my hand already on it, and I'd never fired one in my life.

He fired first. I felt it go into my side, low, on the left, under the ribs — not pain, not yet; a blow, like being kicked by something very large — and the recoil sent him back on the end of his belt against the wall, slowly, and his free hand opened, and the brandy bottle turned away from him end over end through the white air.

I fired. Three times. I don't remember deciding to. The carbine bucked in my hands and threw me back against the door frame, and the noise in the iron room was so loud it wasn't a noise, it was a place, and when I could see again Lucan Marsh was floating at the end of his belt by the cradle's pedestal with his arms out a little in front of him and his white hair spread round his head, turning very slowly, with his back to me.

I don't know how long I stayed there. Long enough to start counting. I got to nine. That was where the man in Spoke Five had stopped, and I don't know why, but I couldn't go past it.

Then I went across the vault and took my mother's key out of the lock.

* * *

The little grey plate had drifted off his palm. I found it against the brass plaque, turning slowly, and put it in my shirt with Ottilie's papers. The bottle I let go.

I was bleeding. Not badly, I thought; not yet. A slow dark seep from the hole in my side that came away from me in small trembling beads in the zero gravity and drifted, and caught the white light, and hung. I pressed my hand over it and held it there, and it went on seeping between my fingers.

There's an emergency locker in every room in the Office. Fire hoods — soft clear bags with a collar seal and a little bottle of air on the side, ten minutes' worth, for smoke. I took two. I took the steel bar from the Keepers' fire kit. Then I knelt on the iron by the cradle's pedestal, beside the drop hatch with its red lever, over the round plate with eight bolts.

*Recovery port*, said the stencil. *Ballast 4 well. For use at port by lawful owner only.*

Ballast Four. I knew what that was. I'd hung with my forehead against its hatch six days ago in the dark, at the end of a dead-end corridor, listening to the water knock. *Fourth Cistern. Closed CR 718. Contaminated.* Two hundred years of poison, sealed up and left.

I put the bar to the first bolt.

It took a long time. They were three hundred years old and they'd never been turned, and every one of them fought me, and the hole in my side opened a little more with every heave. Twice the Hulk shuddered round me and the white lamps flickered, and once there was a long low grinding from somewhere aft that went on for nearly a minute, and I stopped and held on to the pedestal with my bloody hand and waited for it to finish. Lucan Marsh turned slowly at the end of his belt above me the whole time. I didn't look at him.

The eighth bolt came free at — I don't know. I'd stopped counting. I put my shoulder under the rim of the plate and heaved, and it lifted, slowly, a great iron disc that in any gravity would have taken four men; and I swung it up and over and let it settle against the side of the pedestal, and looked down.

A shaft. Two metres across, iron-walled, with rungs set into one side, going down. Three metres, perhaps. And at the bottom of it, filling it from wall to wall, black and perfectly still, water.

Not ice. Water. Cold enough that I could feel it on my face from up here, breathing up out of the well like the breath of something asleep. In the white light from the vault it didn't shine. It just lay there, black, a floor you could fall through.

*Contaminated.* Two hundred years. A fever in the water, hundreds dead in the Crown.

I put on the fire hood. I sealed the collar. I opened the little air bottle and heard it hiss. I tied my handset to my wrist with its own cord and turned its light to full. And I went down the rungs, one at a time, with my hand pressed to my side, into the Fourth Cistern.

* * *

I won't tell you about the cold. I can't. There aren't words for it that you'd believe. I'll tell you that it went through the borrowed coat and the shirt and the skin and into the hole in my side like a hand, and that my heart stopped — I felt it stop, I swear it, for a beat, two — and then started again, very fast and very hard, as if it were trying to get out of me. I'll tell you that the handset's light went about two metres into the black water and stopped, and there was nothing in it at all. No walls. No floor. Just black, and the fine pale drifting specks of three hundred years of silt, turning in my light like stars.

It's twelve metres to the bottom. I know that now. I knew only, then, that the rungs went on down the side of the well and then the well opened out into something much bigger, and that I let go of the last rung and kept going, down, kicking, with my hand on my side and the hood's little bottle hissing at my ear, into the dark.

I found the bottom by hitting it.

Silt. Soft, deep, cold, rising round me in a slow grey cloud that swallowed the light. I put my hands into it. I don't know what I thought I'd find. I don't know what I thought it would look like. I went along the bottom on my knees with my hands in the silt, sweeping, feeling, the way you'd look for a dropped coin in a dark room, and the cloud rose round me and the hood fogged and the bottle hissed and my heart went on trying to get out.

I nearly missed it. I'd gone past it. My left hand touched something hard and straight-edged under the silt and I'd gone on for two more sweeps before I understood what I'd touched and went back.

A sleeve. Lead. Grey, soft, dented, the length of my forearm and a bit more, open at one end, lying in the silt at the foot of a curving chute that came down out of the dark above. I put my hand inside it.

Iron.

* * *

I don't remember getting back up. I remember the rungs. I remember the hood's bottle running out somewhere near the top, the hiss stopping, and tearing the hood off my face in the well with the water up to my chest and breathing the air of the shaft — cold, stale, three hundred years old, the best air I've ever breathed. I remember that the thing in my arms was so heavy I couldn't climb with it and had to hold it against my chest with one arm and climb with the other, one rung at a time, and that the hole in my side was open now, properly, and I could feel it opening with every rung.
